MISSING SIBYL

MISSING SIBYL OR FOUND CHAPTER I PLEADING 

"It is of no use to plead longer, Victor, for I will not hamper your life with such a promise while you are not yet through college, and have to make your own way. I, myself, have just finished high school, while she, whom I called and thought to be my mother, looked on with gentle, loving eyes and kind admonitions, guided me through all the hoyden years of my young life. I was ever an active child with a part in all school activities, as you know. How it must have taxed her to meet my needs with never a word of complaint. This crushing blow of her death, how can I stand it. It seems to me that she lived by force of will power to see me through high school that I might be enabled to make my own way in life." Sibyl buried her face in her hands, while tears came, of which she was not ashamed. "All the more reason, dearest, why you should come to my sheltering arms, for, Sibyl dearest, I love you, and the joy of my life would be to make you happy as my beloved wife. Darling, darling, consider my love." "Oh, please, please; Victor, you are the most wonderful friend, I know, and you ever have been. I 11

MISSING SIBYL 

have a very high regard for you, but, Victor, I don't believe it is love. I think it is a great friendship that 

I feel for you. But don't think that I am not grateful for the great honor which you have conferred upon me by asking me to be your wife. I can't say, 'Yes,' 

Victor, I can't say it." 

And so Sibyl steeled her heart against her dearest love, when she realized he would have to give up a career, and labor in the lower walks of life to support a home, a wife, and perhaps children. "Really, Vic, I have a hard thing to tell you. Mrs.

Brinton was not my mother. She never told me until she was certain she was dying, and then there was not time to tell me all. I do not know who I am, other than that my name is Gordon, and that my mother was unhappily married. "One gloomy, rainy evening there was a skidding car and a crash near Mrs. Brinton's front gate. A

young woman, who was unconscious, was carried into Mrs. Brinton's home. She hastily summoned a physician and a nurse, but they could not save the woman's life. That same night I was born, and before noon the next day, she had rather suddenly passed away. 

But before dying, she had told Mrs. Brinton that she was fleeing from a base husband and seeing Mrs. 

Brinton had no children, begged her to keep me. "She had with her only five hundred dollars, a wedding ring, and a locket, in which was her husband's picture. She took them off, requesting they be kept for me. These Mrs. Brinton carefully locked away. 

She died with the word "write" on her lips. She was trying to tell me more, when her lips were sealed in death. The second day after her funeral, a real estate agent called upon me and asked if I wanted to sell my small home. In astonishment I said: 'Why, this is not my house; it belongs to mother.' With a

MISSING SIBYL 

13 chuckle he informed me that she had sometime ago deeded the property to me and added that he could sell it, furnished, for two thousand dollars, which is quite enough for it in this small town.' 99 "Do you think of selling it?" questioned Victor. "Oh, I am considering," responded Sibyl. She did not explain that she had already sold and that she was "considering" of quietly leaving the little town. of Bilton. "Now, Victor, dear friend, I am very, very tired, and must rest.' 99 99 

At this Victor blushed, saying, "Pardon me, dear

Sibyl, I know you must be tired and broken, so if there is anything I can do, let me know, or rather, I'll drop in tomorrow to see if there is anything I can do.' 

Lovingly taking her hand, which he kissed, he said again, “I'll see you tomorrow." Falteringly, he went down to the gate, where, with a smile and a wave of his hand, he passed out of Sibyl's life, not dreaming it would be many, many days before he would see her again. 

Sibyl fumblingly and tearfully groped her way to her room, saying to herself, "Oh, Vic, darling, darling. 

I love you to the very depths of my soul, but I will not be a millstone around your neck and ruin your career as a lawyer. So it's goodbye now, forever.

But you don't know it and I am on the quest of finding out who I am, so I am leaving this little town, where mother and I have lived so happily ever since 

I was four years old." Soliloquy, soliloquy! How it accents our thoughts and guides our lives more often than we believe!

Victor Whiteside, with a heavy heart, returned to his room, where he had none to greet him, being orphaned of both parents. There he studied during school months and went out to labor during vacation

MISSING SIBYL 

months. Thus his studies made him a slave to his time and money, and made him feel very lean indeed, in a financial way. It was with heartfelt pangs that he thought his love not reciprocated. Musing to himself, he thought, Well, I can only be a man to myself, but I can only be her friend. God bless her and help her find happiness somehow, someplace and someone worthy of her, in her sweet gentle heart. Oh, my darling! good bye! But I'll remember.

Sibyl had her baggage all packed and sent to the depot, and at the midnight hour left what had so long been her home, which she had quietly sold through her agent, who would immediately look after it. The midnight train carried her away, and thus she dropped out of the life of the small town. She disappeared and none knew until Victor dropped by after luncheon and found her gone, and that she had dropped out of his life, even as a friend. 

As he stood there stunned by the knowledge, he echoed in prayer the promptings of his heart. "Oh, God! God, give her friends, wherever she may be."

Little he knew how much she would need them. blessings, like troubles, tumble upon us when least expected. But

Sibyl had planned carefully, had secured a ticket for just one station out of her way, and from there had transferred to another railroad an hour later. 

The object was to throw off her track any who might try to locate her whereabouts, as she wished to be effaced altogether from the little town which she had learned to love, with pleasant school girl associations and dear friends who knew nothing of her sad birth.

CHAPTER II ARRIVES IN THE CITY 

Arriving in the city of St. Louis next day, in late afternoon, she was almost terrified by the commotion and bustle all about her. As she was making her way to a policeman an engaging young man briskly intercepted her by saying, "Miss, you seem to be alone. 

May I be of any help to you?" "You may not," replied Sibyl. "I will seek safer authority." The young man hastily slunk away, with the eyes of the policeman upon him. Turning to the uniformed man, she asked, "Sir, can you tell me where to find a cheap, respectable and quiet rooming house?" "Yes, Miss, I certainly can. Take this coming street car, go four blocks, and the second house to your right will give you access to just such a house, until you look about and find something that might better suit your needs; but shy clear of such help as you just encountered.' 

"Oh, thank you so much, I am a stranger here and do not know places." "Oh, that's all right, just don't get panicky, but use your head to think and your tongue to ask the right ones, for with that pretty brown head of yours-" "Pretty brown head?" interrupted Sibyl. "I thought there were supposed to be no pretty heads, except red ones." And laughingly continued, "That's one other thing I'm glad I escaped, I loathe red hair, for there's always a bunch of freckles with it. But I suppose that's my poor taste." 15

MISSING SIBYL 

Dick Blessington handed her on the car and said, "So long," with a smile thrown in. 

Well! thought Sibyl, that's my first chuckle. But oh, mother, mother, if you were only with me!

She easily found the rooming house, and engaged a small room that was well lighted, well aired and clean. This will do for the present, she thought. 

After a bath, she realized she was famished, for she in her sorrow and concern had not felt hunger. 

Mrs. Helen Winger, her kindly landlady, directed her to a moderate-priced and clean restaurant, where she found it quiet. Her hunger satisfied, she retired early to bed for she was very fatigued. Snuggling in her pillow, she thought of victor, home and mother until she drifted off into slumber, from which she was awakened by the sunlight streaming across her face. 

Springing from her bed, she said, "Thank God, for such a glorious night's rest. I must not loiter, as I must hunt work, for I do not intend to use my little nest egg of money unless accident or sickness overtakes me.' 

As she bathed and dressed, she sang a little song of praise, which her mother had so much loved. Thinking of her, her voice was filled with a sweet pathos which was very touching. Snatching up her hat and handbag, she tripped downstairs and out, seeking a nearby place for breakfast. She 

Guess I'll make it a sturdy breakfast, for I'll surely hit the pavement hard this day, sensed Sibyl. was so filled with anxiety that she scarcely noticed what she was eating. She was alert, and carefully noted the class of people who were eating there. Respectable, she observed, and felt safe. 

Securing a paper, she hastened to a park that was near by and seating herself in a park chair, was busily scanning the want ads, when she was startled by a

MISSING SIBYL 

17 massive dog which had approached her and dropped an envelope into her lap. He had carried it in his mouth, and stood wagging his tail in friendly manner. 

Looking for a superscription, she found the word "Lady" superinscribed on the back in the chirography of a bold, masculine hand. She sensed for a surety that someone was in hiding, and had sent the welltrained dog. So, without opening the missive, she wrote just below the word "Lady" the words, “Can't read, but can write and can also blow a whistle." 

Placing the envelope in the dog's mouth, she bade him go, and as she watched, noticed he took it to a chair placed behind a large tree, in which sat a man. 

Well! it won't do to jolly anyone here. Mashers seem plentiful everywhere, thought Sibyl.

CHAPTER III A SINGING ENGAGEMENT 

She at once arose and went back to her room, where in privacy she scanned with care the want ads which most appealed to her. Hastily locking her door, she started at once in order to be early and avoid a long wait. As she turned from her door, a gentleman accosted her, saying, "I beg pardon, Miss, I heard you singing, and wondered if it would be possible to secure you to fill a place which my sister is too ill to fill." "Now, that all depends. Let us descend to the parlor below, and discuss the matter." Leading the way down, she asked him if he had any acquaintance with the landlady. Taking the chair which she motioned him to, he replied, "Certainly." At such a statement she touched a button and upon the entrance of 

Mrs. Winger, asked Mrs. Winger, "Do you know this gentleman ?" "Why, yes, my dear, he is Mr. Ernest Mosley, who has roomed here a long time, and I've always found him to be a worthy person. Why do you ask?" "Let me explain," voiced the young man. "I saw

Miss Brinton's name on the register. She has done the right thing by referring to you.' "I heard her singing in her room, and very much admired her voice. My sister, Myrtle, whom you know, is sick and unable to sing at Mrs. Waldron's reception as she promised. So I've asked Miss Brinton to fill the vacancy, if she feels so disposed." 18

MISSING SIBYL 

19 "Myrtle sick! I'm sorry to hear that. Is it anything serious?" questioned Mrs. Winger. "We are hoping it may not be serious, yet she is far from being able to appear. So, if Miss Brinton would fill the place, it would help Myrtle out as well as Miss Brinton, who would be making a handsome return to herself, for Mrs. Waldron pays generously. 99 "Truly," voiced Mrs. Winger. "I have heard of her beneficence and she is a most estimable lady, with a large circle of friends. One who could please her and her circle is indeed fortunate." "But it is doubtful," interposed Sibyl, "that I should be able to do so on short notice, even if my voice is sufficient, with no pianist. 99 "Myrtle plays her own accompaniments." "Oh, she does? Well, that way I can manage all right. I know a lot of the latest hits and a lot of fine, old songs as well." "I must say you are a lucky young woman, and I know you will make the grade nicely," observed Mrs. Winger.

Having desired assurance and directions, Sibyl outlined her program, and personally saw Mrs. Waldron so that she should not be painfully surprised and disappointed. "Indeed I am most fortunate," declared Mrs. Waldron, "for on such short notice I might not find one to fill the place, and Miss Brinton, you will not need any startling costumes." "Thank you, dear Mrs. Waldron. I can probably make out by your kind decision." Thus, the matter being settled, Sibyl added to her wardrobe a dainty evening costume, assembled her program carefully, and practiced faithfully so that all should pass off efficiently. But it was with trepidation that she undertook the beautiful task, as she feared a critical audi-

MISSING SIBYL 

ence. She steeled herself to the task by saying to herself: "I came to work and this is a job that came to me out of a clear sky. So I will do my best."

The night of the party Sibyl arrived rather early, saying to the butler, "Please report to Mrs. Waldron that Miss Brinton came early that she would not have the worry of a possible disappointment." "Right this way in the library, Miss Brinton. I will report at once. 19 

Mrs. Waldron appeared shortly, saying, "How kind of you, Miss Brinton, to come early, and so thoughtful. It is the last minute worries that fairly kill a hostess, and I thank you for coming. Now rest awhile." "Thank you, dear Mrs. Waldron, for your courtesy.

I will stay in seclusion until you are ready for my part of the entertainment."

The cars rolled up disgorging a great mass of people. "Mercy!" breathed Sibyl, "where in the world will she put them? It seems that even large as this house is, it can't hold them all. This is a bigger affair than 

I thought for, but I must not get the shivers. She has a good orchestra, too. At least from the glimpse 

I got, they looked capable, indeed."

But Sibyl, too, was there, and she stood dressed in her pretty white graduating frock, and sang light, sweet, catchy songs with which she meant to appeal to the young guests. And they enjoyed them with smiles, giggles and cheers. Later she appeared in a colorful evening gown, which suited her color and form nicely. In it she appeared older, appealing more to the older audience by singing sweet, pathetic, touching songs which brought tears as well as smiles and cheers. She responded in a gracious, unselfish manner to encores. At last she was relieved, and later Mrs.

MISSING SIBYL 

21 Waldron was besieged with requests to be introduced to "your young friend with that promising voice." 

She smilingly acquiesced, saying: "Dear Miss Brinton, these my friends, wish to meet you. May I present them?" "Dear Mrs. Waldron, how kind you are to me, and how sweet of them to think of it, for I know it is most unusual." 

One young man, seeing this knot of ladies entering the library and noting his mother among them, hastily stepped to her side, lifted her hand and placed it on his arm. She, looking up and smiling, said teasingly, "Bennie, you're not a lady. Look after the young ladies here." "Truly, mother," he retorted, "you are yet, and mighty attractive. May I attend?" He smiled his prettiest, while she tenderly patted the arm of her only son, Benton Armstrong, and permitted him to lead her to Sibyl. 

Upon being presented he said: "Miss Brinton, I was in the park a few mornings since, where you were scanning a paper. I saw a trained dog take a letter to you, and you without opening it wrote something on the back of it, placed it in the dog's mouth and motioned him away; I saw that pariah read it, chuckle, and slink out of the park, followed by his dog, which was an intelligent creature. I am studying to be a physician, and there may come a time when I may tell some lady to use your tactics, for I saw it worked. 

Now, will you be gracious enough to tell me what the moving power was?"

At hearing this the ladies laughed and eagerly joined in his request, exclaiming, "How interesting! 

Do, Miss Brinton, let us hear the secret." 

Blushing and smiling, Sibyl was compelled to say: "The envelope read only 'Lady.' 999

MISSING SIBYL 

"Of course I wouldn't open it, but I wrote these words: 'Can't read, but I can write and I can blow a whistle, and I signed it 'Lady.' 999 

With an outburst of laughter and echoes of "You'll do! Great! Fine!" the group of congratulators moved away, leaving her with the very cockles of her heart warmed. 

Bennie, looking back smilingly, thought, "What a strong, young girl she is, facing the world alone on her own." 99 The next morning found Sibyl looking through the ads for a place of employment. Walking from place to place until her feet ached and her very soul flagged. 

Hearing only the words, "Place filled." Yet, with an indomitable will, she kept on trying. 

One day, picking up a late issue, she discovered a new one close in the vicinity of which she happened to be domiciled. Hastening there, she happened to be first and it was an emergency call for help. There had been an accident. The firm felt fortunate to secure so attractive and intelligent help at once. The pay was small, but it was a foothold until she could gain something better. Her next effort was night study of stenography.

CHAPTER IV SALESLADY AND ARRESTED 

She must get into more lucrative work, and felt that as a stenographer she would get a better wage and not be standing, standing all day long until she was so weary that she could scarcely rest at night. 

Some of the girls she was sure she could like, but a few acted offish, rather queerly, as though they resented her presence. She observed nods and whisperings concerning herself, and was at a loss to understand it. So, stepping over to one of the girls with whom she had lunched, asked: "Sarah, why does Jennie and that other girl act so strangely? They look at me as though I were guilty of some misdemeanor." 

Sarah, with a smile, responded, "You are guilty." "Of what, pray, and I'll make restitution, for I want to be honest." 99 "Well, dear neighbor, you remember you refused to be made up in a party with that chap downstairs, and he is a cousin to Jennie." "So I remember, and now I've got to start in bucking a fool hate like that, have I? But how did you know about it?” "I overheard Jennie and Vera talking about it and they decided they'd play the game of 'even up' with you yet. So I caution you to watch your step, for 

Jennie will not scruple at anything.' 99 "Oh! I do so thank you, Sarah. That puts me wise." "I'll stand by you, Sibyl, and watch, too," promised Sarah. 23

MISSING SIBYL 

The new friends puzzled about what would be the best way for an alibi, for it looked like trouble was certainly on the way, and both naturally concluded it would come to a matter of supposed theft. Next morning they met at the outer door, when Sarah said, "Sibyl, we must exchange lockers, for it's easy to have a key made and I believe there will be their field of operation. They know we both have lockers and that is one thing that makes them jealous, for they both use the common cloak room, while we have the lockers by paying for their use.' 99 "Yes, Sarah," responded Sibyl, "I have noticed that also, and have rather wondered if it was wise in the firm's having lockers, as it divides the girls into caste, as it were, thus making jealousy quite natural; but so it is, and I am very glad to have a locker.' 99 "I, too," reiterated Sarah. "Let's change lockers now. Our coats are the same color." "Oh, no! no" expostulated Sibyl. "I'll not have you getting into trouble on my account." "I won't. It will be fun, for it will rebound on them. I've got a little fight in my blood, and I would like to see them squelched." "Well," augmented Sibyl, "if we do exchange, let's tag our coats by sewing our names into the sleeves of our coats, thus instantly proving our possession.' 99 "That's fine!" replied Sarah. "Tomorrow we will start the change. Jennie knows where your locker is, I saw her look in yesterday when you went to your locker, so she knows perfectly well which is yours." "Mercy! Sarah, it makes me feel prickly, with shivers running up and down my spine."

As she parted from Sarah, she had a feeling like a premonition that tomorrow would be too late. So

MISSING SIBYL 

25 she turned her steps at once to the superintendent's office, and luckily found him in and not busy. "Mr. Stiles, may I intrude upon a minute of your time? Just long enough to ask if I may change lockers? I am expecting a coup to be pulled off and I want to be away from that locker." "Why, Miss Brinton, what do you mean? Surely nothing will happen here. Everything seems to be going along smoothly." "I am afraid it is in seeming only, and I am sure, Mr. Stiles, that you, like myself, would not like a scene created; so I came straight to you." "Please explain, I must know facts," chipped in 

Mr. Stiles. "I am almost ashamed to tell, as it seems so small and insignificant, Mr. Stiles. I refused to be made up in a party of four persons employed here, and ever since they have been acting a little queerly, and a couple were heard to say they would 'even up' on me." "I will go with you at once, and we will make the exchange right now. We want no trouble here. The papers, too, would get it and we certainly wish to avoid such publicity," said Mr. Stiles. "Please let no one see us moving my belongings." "Ha, ha! That makes me remember. There's a lady's coat hanging in a locker in the office. I'll get it and hang it in the locker you are vacating, and watch developments." He, turning away, laughingly said, "Fraidcat." 

Sibyl smiled at his little joke, and returned to Sarah telling her of the change of plan, and they both eagerly watched and waited. 

The next day, just before lunch an officer stepped up to Sibyl, saying, "Miss Sibyl Brinton, you are under arrest." She was expecting something like that, yet

MISSING SIBYL 

when she looked around on the accusing and startled faces, she paled and trembled and felt as if she had been struck a blow, but did not lose her presence of mind. "For what am I under arrest, and at whose instigation?" questioned Sibyl. "Well, you come along and find out and don't make a hullabaloo in here," replied the officer. "Mr. Officer, this is a good store and so am I a good girl. I take only a step at a time, so it behooves me to know where I am stepping. I insist on knowing by whom and what for." 

By this time a knot of clerks and customers were gathered around. One of the male clerks drew near the officer, saying, "The lady is perfectly within her rights and need not move until she hears the complaint." "You are accused of shoplifting expensive cosmetics, and said articles are now within your locker. Furthermore, you were seen putting them there. Mr. 

Tuttle saw you.' 99 "I'll go along, Mr. Officer. You have your duty to perform. I only wanted to know from what source.' 99 Brought before the Judge, she declared her innocence. "I have a key to my locker and I ask the presence of the Superintendent to verify it." "9 Mr. Glidden, the proprietor of the store, was there. 

He immediately came forth, saying, “I'll bring him.'

So he whisked his car away and soon returned, bringing the Superintendent who explained about the exchange of lockers, and having investigated found the cosmetics in Sibyl's first locker. He brought those and explained everything to the Judge, thus proving it to be a case of blackmailing.

MISSING SIBYL 

27 "Miss Brinton, have those parties arrested. Such a base act should not be lightly passed over." "No, Judge Eaton. Life's too short and I'm pretty busy both day and night. I am studying stenography at night. If I should prosecute them it would ruin them, and they have to live as well as myself. I won't raise my hands against a vile jealousy, although I shudder when I think of what might have happened to me.' 99 Mr. Glidden hastened to inform her and the Judge that those parties would be summarily dismissed from his service. He would not stand for anything like that. "Well, Mr. Glidden, that and a guilty conscience will be their punishment. I daresay, when we get back to the store they will have flown," predicted 

Sibyl, and her prediction proved to be true. "Plucky girl," voiced the Judge. "Come, Miss Brinton, get in my car and I'll take you home. You are excused from the store the rest of the day. Stay at home and rest." "No, Mr. Glidden, I want to go back to the store and show those people and clerks that I'm not in jail," interposed Sibyl. "Pretty well said, and now didn't I hear you say you were studying stenography?" interrogated Mr. 

Glidden. "When you are finished, let me know. I'll be able to place you in a more lucrative position either with myself, or an old friend who will shortly lose his secretary, who is about to be married." "Oh, thank you! kind Mr. Glidden. Strange, isn't it, that what seems a great misfortune opens the door to a great blessing?" responded Sibyl. "Again you've voiced a truth; but when the door opens it's a proof that one has stood the test, Miss 

Brinton. Now you've had a hard day. Leave the

MISSING SIBYL 

store early and rest, and again I want to add how sorry I am that you were subjected to this indignity," comforted Mr. Glidden. 

Sarah rushed to meet Sibyl with outstretched hands, saying, "I've been so worried, even though I knew you were safe." "Yes," chirped Sibyl laughingly, "your red-rimmed eyes tell me so. You dear friend. If it had not been for you, think where I might have been right now!" "Oh! but, Sibyl, you should have seen them! It was as good as a show. How they did scurry! Jennie ran over a child, which went screaming and crying to its mother. She never stopped to apologize, but scuttled out of here as fast as she could. I wonder where she is now?" "Yes, where?" reiterated Sibyl. "Tuttle looked as if he'd never had a drop of blood in his veins. Take it from me, they are thinking it's your move next, and you ought to act," advised Sarah. "How did he get away from the trial?" "It fairly sickens my soul to think of it and what might have been," answered Sibyl. "I came back from the police station, to let you and the rest know that all is well. I have been given the rest of the day and I'm going." She haltingly departed amid their congratulations and smiles of gladness. "What! Home a little early, aren't you?" interrogated Mrs. Winger as Sibyl entered the hall. "Come upstairs to my room," requested Sibyl. Mrs.

Winger went along with misgiving, for naturally she had the honor of her house to shield, and so was usually expecting something derogatory to turn up.

Sibyl threw herself on her bed, motioning her landlady to a chair, which she accepted while carefully scrutinizing Sibyl. "I was arrested for shoplifting and hiding the spoils'

MISSING SIBYL 

29 in my locker, but I proved my innocence. My friend, Sarah, overheard Jennie Priest say she would 'even up' on me for refusing to be one of a party of four with her cousin, Mr. Tuttle, and herself. Tuttle works downstairs. I changed my locker without her knowledge, but the Superintendent knew it. I gave him the key to the old locker, but they must have had one made, for they sneaked expensive cosmetics into that locker and then complained against me, so 

I was arrested. Of course the Superintendent cleared me. But Mrs. Winger, it makes me tremble to think what would have happened if Sarah had not heard those girls talking. Jennie and that miserable man

Tuttle did the rotten job, and they both skipped out. 

Mr. Glidden excused me for the rest of the day. That's why I'm early, and I wanted to tell you all about it, as I am a roomer in your house, it is right that you should know all about it." "My My! that was a close squeak, Miss Brinton, and I rejoice with you that you came clear of the charge. You look tired too," sympathized Mrs. Winger. "Mr. Glidden wanted to bring me home, but I told him I wanted to go back to the store so the rest could see I had been vindicated." "You did right, perfectly right. I am glad you went back. Sarah is a good girl and dependable. She has been in the Glidden store a long time and has made you a wonderful friend. And this happening to you will make the whole force staunch friends." 

Morning found Sibyl with fever and as a consequence, spent, nervous, and unfit for duty. "Why, how can I be like this when I knew and was expecting just such trouble?" "My dear, it is reaction. It's the thought of what might have been. You'll be all right tomorrow. I'll

MISSING SIBYL 

send a message to your employer. So don't worry but take a good rest today," counseled Mrs. Winger.

Some of the girls missed their lunch, giving that time to Sibyl. Others phoned. A box of beautiful roses arrived, bearing Mr. Glidden's card. "How very thoughtful of the Gliddens to remember me, for Mr. Glidden is a very busy man," observed Sibyl. "Why do you say 'Gliddens' when Mr. Glidden is a bachelor? His father owned the large business, but at his death it reverted to his only son, who is sole owner." "9 "Oh, I didn't know that. I supposed him to be married, with a family and establishment. But these beauties! How refreshing they are! They act like a tonic upon my senses." "They would! And I suppose others of his roses have refreshed young ladies," observed Mrs. Winger with a wry grin. "Ha, ha, ha! Mrs. Winger, you will have your joke, but I'm glad I did not know he was a single man. I would have been more embarrassed, for I thought a married man was more understanding than a celibate. The celsitude of my life is self improvement and making my own way, and that keeps me busy." Thus she succinctly closed the discussion.

CHAPTER V STENOGRAPHY AND SINGING 

It was in Judge Eaton's office that Sibyl was established as stenographer. She felt that as she had lost her foster mother, she had gained a kind of foster father in the fine old Judge who had handled her case in Glidden's store. She lost no time in enrolling under a competent instructor in voice, who was very encouraging indeed, as he knew her voice to be unusually good and would add luster to him in its training. She applied herself steadily and improved accordingly. Her courage rose too, so that her engagements were a real pleasure instead of a dread, and thus many months sped by. "Mrs. Winger, I feel that I am fairly sailing, with an engagement every ten days or so, and with such splendid families patronizing me as a singer and never allowing me to set a price for my engagements. They do that at more than I am worth. I've met and found friends in a few young ladies that I prize highly. They seem not to care that I am making my own way. It surely is well that I get many engagements, for voice lessons consume a lot of it.' 99 "Well, yes, Sibyl, but you are getting the worth of that money, I can see. But you are working too hard. You are thinner and sometimes a little nervous. You know you can't stand a strain forever. You would better drop those singing lessons, for a while at least. You have used that excuse a lot to keep away some who would like your society socially, and 38

MISSING SIBYL 

39 who are nice and educated young people. You are making a perfect grind of life. I know you will say it is your chance, which I will not deny, but there is a limit. Ha, ha! You know they do come occasionally, jibed Mrs. Winger, with a playful mocking her, at which Sibyl grinned. How those two souls understood each other! 99 As Sibyl lifted a wealth of roses out of their wrappings, she exclaimed, "Oh, what beauties! And to perish! MISSING SIBYL 

43 "Most certainly you must show me your tongue, as it's a pretty fair indication of how bilious you may be, although there is much laughter about it," reiterated the Doctor. "Now, little girl, you've been working too hard. 

But you must rest, rest, rest, till your fingers twitch for a typewriter." "Poor Mrs. Winger! She is my guardian angel," wanly smiled Sibyl. "I believe it," voiced the doctor as he bowed himself out. But Mrs. Winger had slipped out a side door and met him at the front door. "Nothing serious, Mrs. Winger, but she must be kept in absolute rest for awhile. She has overtaxed her strength so much that she has none in reserve to carry her through. But we'll see, we'll see." 

He closed the door behind him as he thought: How is it that some people work themselves to death, while others die of dry rot, because they work little or none?

This is a queer old world. Yet this old city has been good to her, and given her the incentive to race forward in the attempt to improve herself. She ought to be away from here. She has a host of friends who know her, and a host of friends she doesn't know. 

They've heard her sing and admire her pluck. She'll be worn with visits, messages and phone talks-with fruit, candy and flowers, until she will miss the quiet she should have.

The days slipped by into weeks and weeks into months without the proper change although seemingly better, when Dr. Langley suddenly asked: "Miss Brinton, how would you like to take an ocean

MISSING SIBYL 

voyage? I am sure a salt water trip would put roses into those pallid cheeks."

Sibyl, looking with surprise into those twinkling eyes, countered back with: "Why, truly, Doctor, I'd like it immensely," and with a sickly grin overspreading her face, continued, "I think I'll charter the J. P. Morgan yacht and load it with the world's richest foods and rich, funny people and sally forth on the briny deep. I fear I'd make a poor sailor, so I'd feed the fishes and between times I'd catch mermaids and bring back here and turn them loose in the parks, where we'd give entertainments at fabulous prices, which in turn would pay for the use of the yacht or maybe buy it outright. 

Yes, Doctor Langley, I think I'll take an ocean voyage, since you've made me sit up and take notice."

She ended up with a real laugh, which the Doctor was glad to hear, for she had been lacking in mirth. "Now, Doctor, I don't mean to be funny, but only ridiculous." 

The Doctor gave a real guffaw and informed her he meant it. He had an Aunt who had been very sick, and being very rich, had concluded to take a rest cure on her yacht, and wanted someone who would be both companion and secretary. Such duties on a yacht would not be arduous, and he was sure he could place her in the position, if she would permit it. His aunt had asked if among his patients there was such a one who would also benefit by such a rest or change. She would pay well and be happy in so doing. He, knowing both ladies, felt certain of a pleasant consummation.

CHAPTER VI YACHTING REST 

"Dr. Langley! How could you! Why, the very thought of it makes me feel better already. Indeed, I'd be glad to go without pay." "No, no, Miss Brinton! You'll have to go on her terms and don't quibble about it." "She certainly is not troubled with venality, Doctor. "Any time she wishes will suit me, for I am on my own and there's none to condone." 

So in three days' time the yacht started, and while it wasn't J. P's, it was gorgeous, and Sibyl looked forward with great anticipation to a happy association with only a meager amount of labor, and certainly was not looking forward for much in the way of a monetary return. 45

Mrs. Winger was sure that the doctor, out of the bigness of his heart, had paved the way for the place with his aunt, and in a place of great comfort and satisfaction. 

The farewell party was a lovely occasion, and Sibyl, beautifully gowned, her eyes sparkling with joy and excitement, was at her best. "My first song is in compliment to my hostess. The second in compliment to Mrs. Winger, my old friend and hostess, and her house inmates, who are a memory. They I promised to sing one song for on the yacht. The rest will be to you kind guests.' 

She seemed full of song until Dr. Langley interfered. But she begged him to let her sing one more, which was "Auld Lang Syne." There were not many dry eyes when she finished.

The Doctor stepped out on deck, reached for his

MISSING SIBYL 

handkerchief, wiped tears, blew his nose vigorously and thought: 

That child will make friends wherever she goes.

She has so much pride that she will not let her friends advance money to cultivate that talent, but what a beautiful ambition she has, to advance herself in whatever she sets her mind to! But I'm sure she has something on her heart and mind, of which she never gives a hint.

As she sang she thought of Victor Whiteside, and she filled with tears as she realized that they were soon to be farther separated than ever, for soon she'd sail down the dear old Mississippi and be swallowed up on the briny deep. The time passed as all time will, and friends dispersed amid laughter, joy, well wishes and farewells. How she wished she could avoid telling Bennie and Jimmie good bye, for she could see they were not heart-whole, but she could not offer them one look of hope. The last party for many months was over.

The kind genial doctor, with his years of experience and observation, was near to diagnosing her particular case of trouble, and took particular pains to lay his assumption before his Aunt, requesting that she skilfully and adroitly probe Sibyl's heart in the hope of helping her to overcome what seemed insurmountable. He well knew, even with the hosts of friends she had made, none had been in daily contact and thus had not realized her heart's problems, except what had been on the surface and she seemed skillful in hiding her trouble. 

So ever her friend, Mrs. Seabright, was willed to try, but was not lost to the beauty of the grand old river and its beautiful scenery, pointing out ever and anon its loveliness. 

Sibyl was not forgetful of her duty to her newly

49 found friend, who was very kind and generous; and on the following morning as the gray light of the east was heralding the dawn, which glimmers through the dark curtain of night being pinned up by the stars which hide themselves in its folds, there fell next the first rays of the rising run about Sibyl's face as she was wrapped in slumber, thus rousing her to the busy actions and glorious achievements of day. Arising, she thought, I must not go day dreaming, but must be about my duties, or I'll get to be a sumptuous person. Splashing in a luxurious bath and attiring herself in a fresh morning frock, she felt the glory of the beautiful morning.

Entering the library she did not expect to see but to await the appearance of Mrs. Seabright, that she might learn what were her duties. This chance for building up my health is worth all the work I can do." "No, I must have my way, Sibyl. May I call you 

Sibyl ?" "Please do, Mrs. Seabright. It will seem so homey, but never let me intrude upon your kindness, nor your privacy. Outline your daily plans, so I may know how to best serve you.' 99 "Your duties as a secretary will be onerous, but as a companion you may help me manage and entertain my other guests, as I have only a few on board. "Well, here comes the rest of our crowd, and as breakfast is served, we shall see what good sailors we are." Mrs. Seabright smiled upon the group and found they were all able to smile back amid laughter, predictions and dares to get sick. The time passed pleasantly each with his or her own diversion. Some

MISSING SIBYL 

51 reading, some writing, some with light handwork, while others just sitting or lying about resting. How pleasant were the days interspersed with songs and games! 

One day in passing a beautiful scenery someone suggested anchoring for a picnic excursion. "Certainly, said the Captain, "I can send all ashore in boats while we anchor in deep water. Stay as long as you wish. 

I'll send eats in another boat and you can have a real picnic lunch ashore, breaking the monotony of shipboard service."

So the bunch, all chattering and laughing, took the delightful outing. They had day after day been making their way on big waters. Lulled and rested they enjoyed quiet, peace and increasing health, so a picnic on shore was a real treat. After luncheon they wandered about gathering shells, flowers and pretty stones until all were tired. Suddenly a yell and hasty running was heard and Louis Garrett came in sight bringing a terribly sickening odor which permeated the air. 

It was obvious to all that he had intruded upon the privacy of a skunk. So sick was he that he could not tell about it but it was evident to the others that it was not from a perfume bottle with a touch behind. the ears. They gave way before his runnings as though fleeing from a plague until they saw him jump into a boat and paddle for the yacht.

Arriving on deck he called for a bathrobe, and hiding behind a screen took off all his clothing and making them into a bundle threw them overboard as he had heard that nothing but burying them in soil would eradicate the odor; so there was nothing to do but to throw them away. After a bath he rolled into bed where he stayed the rest of the day and night. 

His poor 'innards' were so sore from retching that he could scarcely move.

MISSING SIBYL 

The rest of the picknickers drew together like magnets, full of giggles, laughter and comments, each glad that it was not himself but tickled to death that it was the "other fellow." How like the human to see the funny side but not the humiliation. 

Sibyl came up with a controlled face but smiled to see the others so convulsed with laughter, and said, "How like sheep we all are! When one laughs we all laugh, but surely we will be good enough not to tease him about his unpleasant experience.' 

Mrs. Seabright gave her a grateful and appraising look thinking: How kind she is! For she knew Louis to be sensitive. She dreaded what might be said at the next meeting for if the unfortunate incident was made a running joke, he would leave the yacht at the next stop. Of course he'd have some feasible excuse, but she believed Sibyl had saved the situation, not from the viewpoint of babying, but from the standpoint of common courtesy and sympathy, for who would have changed places with him? Not one. 

The shades of evening were lengthening before they took to the boats and rowed back to the yacht, all tired but glad of the opportunity for a little diversion from the routine of boat life. There being a man short in manning the boat, Sibyl sat in Louis' place and took up the oars. "Why Miss Brinton, can you row?" someone asked. "Certainly, I can row. I learned in childhood. 

Don't you know you never learn anything amiss?

There always may come a time when least expected that one can use that knowledge. So here goes, I can 'paddle' every one of you."

They all enjoyed her little wisecrack and showed their pleasure by a generous laugh. The little company had manned the boats themselves so as not to necessitate returns after them. After a general

MISSING SIBYL 

53 cleanup and bath they felt from the day's exercise that they were more than ready for dinner. 

The next morning there was a sonorous sound of the gong for breakfast. The butler knew they'd be slow to rise so he, they thought, expended much strength upon the call. Sibyl left her door partly open and sat where she could see Louis when he should pass by. When they were all seated he passed, meaning to be late so the joke would be all spilled at once and he hoped it would be soon over. 

He was no sooner seated than he was touched on the shoulder and Sibyl said: "How are you feeling this morning Mr. Garrett after your horrible experience of yesterday?" "Fine, fine, thank you." "Yes, I know how fine! after retching as you did yesterday. You are sore as a boil. It came nearly being I instead of you. I started after that clump of lovely flowers when I saw you start so I turned elsewhere. Had it been myself I never would have had the strength to have made it to the yacht. You folks would have had to cut long poles and rolled me under the water and said goodbye as I went down to join the great company of travelers, none of whom returns."

She passed on to her seat, as all laughed. "I thought once when you had one of those devastating paroxysms of retching, that you would capsize the boat, but it righted and you went on. You would have drowned must surely in your condition without rescue.

I ran preparing as I went to swim to you, but I was not needed." "Yes, I made it but was helped some to get up the ladder, but they dumped me like a sack and ran.

The Captain came with a bathrobe and screen so I pulled off my togs, tied them into a bundle and

MISSING SIBYL 

threw them overboard to the fishes. But with the care of the doctor, the Captain, the butler, and Aunt 

Lucy, I'm here." "Yes, I know and sore as a boil. I think we all know it is no jocular matter." "Well the next time I go gathering flowers it will not be from a skunk's roofgarden. I'll take a 'look see' around it for his domicile.' 99 So the matter was discussed, giggled and chuckled at and dropped for all time. When Louis went back to his room he unpacked his cases which had previously been packed, for he knew Sibyl had mastered the situation.

When Sibyl passed the Captain he took her hand saying, "You'll do, Miss Brinton. You'll do, I wish

I'd always had guests who so decently and intelligently settle a disagreeable fiasco for another guest as you did today." "Captain," exclaimed Sibyl, "I'm not a guest, but a paid secretary and companion with nothing worthwhile to do. He is sensitive and would not have stood up against Cousin William. But you knocked the props from under William, and all is safe. My dear, you are like a feast in the hand, both meat and drink and a pleasant memory." "My very dear Mrs. Seabright! You almost persuade me that I am both a somnambulist and a somniloquist, for I seemed not cognizant of such an achievement as you lay at my feet. You are most kind and it certainly is sweet in you to say such lovely words of praise to me. I don't deserve them. I did only what I thought to be right and was sympathetic in a case which merited help and sympathy. I know 

Mr. Taggart joculates his funs and sometimes they cut like a rapier; thus knowing his trend, I wondered if I could circumvent his weakness by helping Louis to ignore his attacks. I, too, like a joke but not at the expense of helplessness. If I had sat there at the table and snickered, I would have felt like a craven. I should hate to be pusillanimous. You know if one aims at nothing he will hit his target. I was aiming at something but I felt that it was wabbly shooting, but if it hit the target, as well as you are trying to make me believe, I shall be most happy indeed." CHAPTER VII 

NO TRACE We will return to Bilton. Victor finding Sibyl gone, tried in every way to find some trace of her but to no avail. The earth seemed to have swallowed her and left no trace.

He thought surely she would write to some of her school girl friends, but inquiry revealed nothing. She was gone, but where? Had there been foul play?

Was she carried away from the small town an easy victim because there was no one to protect her or investigate her absence? She had gently turned down his love and had now stepped away from even his friendship. Where, why and how had she gone?

Well, he mused, somehow I felt she loved me. She seemed so sweet and sparkling. Then all at once when her foster mother died and she found she had another name her heart seemed to freeze within her. 

Has she gone on the search of that name that is nothing but a myth to start on? How gladly I would give you my name, my darling, for I love you so. 

I love you so! But you refused my love and made me helpless to do anything for you, but I'll be true to you always, and pray God to take care of you, bless you, and give you happiness wherever you may be.

By inquiry at the depot he found she had taken a train for a nearby town and at the nearby town she became lost, indeed, to him. He realized that had been her ruse to throw him off the track.

Well, I can at least make a man of myself and that 57

MISSING SIBYL 

is what she wanted me to do. I've got to live, but life tastes like sawdust and ashes. Oh, if I only knew if she was safe from harm, but how distraining is the thought! Many times when he saw a shiny brown curly head he thought he had found her, but it was not she. Alas! The quarry escaped.

CHAPTER VIII VICTOR A LAWYER 

Thus, time went on and he graduated with honor. 

In the little town was a staunch friend who wished to take him in as partner, but thinking a larger town and among strangers would afford him better opportunities, he established himself in Cincinnati not knowing that his beloved had again sailed away from his nearness.

There he took up his life work with an unsatisfied heart, but he made friends.

Lester Buchannan sensed his excluseness and guessed at the reason. So entered his office with a hearty "Hello." "Whiteside, let's take a trip up river to a week-end party. I have an invitation to go and bring a friend. 

An unexpected relative came and they are short a man. Will you go on such short notice? I really want you to meet my uncle's fine household." "Surely I'll go. We have been very busy and have neglected friends too much; but I'll gladly go with you and help your friends out, thus killing two birds with the same stone," responded Victor. 59

CHAPTER IX SOCIETY 

"Fine household! I call it the best ever, and what a splendid intelligent bunch! They certainly know the proprieties and how to enjoy life. A lot of talent, too. "Yes, entertaining is an obsession with them, and they hunt for the best in friends, people of standards, high ideals and they don't look for the dollar mark; so their friends are of the wealthy, the medium, and those making their way, and I beg to inform you that you have made a hit here." "One friend has been inquiring a lot about you and you may have aroused possibilities. Expect to hear from him shortly and land him for a certainty." "Why he is head of the Greene Manufacturing 

Company, isn't he?” "Certainly, and you've been hard at work and proven yourself capable and eligible for handling big concerns. He has heard some pretty things said, so 

I am telling you. Swing in line and handle big things as well as small ones. You've got me beat and I'm not a bit jealous about it." "You deserve all you can get and I'm wishing you all the success in the world. I am glad to see you elevated, so hop to it son." With a slap on the back he said, "Let's go back to the ladies. I noticed

MISSING SIBYL 

61 "Oh, come, come, Lester, are you trying to get me started on a Will O' Wisp chase? If you are I must tell you I'm no good there." "No good there?" observed Lester, "get out of the rut and go into society more. It will bring you a full desk and that is what we are all aspiring to. 

It will make the wheels of prosperty turn. Even Aunt 

Marcelle says you sing a fine tenor, and they must see more of you. So you see you are all set for the chase. Ha, ha! Come on Victor.'

Victor, unconscious of the great gift of personality that made a setting for the jewel's beauty, life and joy, fitted him with a power to sway his audience at command, coupled with a clean, busy life, opened the doors of respectability and truth. Thus, the better element, whether rich or poor, made the circle of his friends.

He was no wealthy nabob, but his was education and goodness which made him a shining light. His life had not been soured by his disappointment in love, but often he shed tears wondering where

Sibyl could be. So at the bottom of everything lay something a yearning which would not be ostracized. In spite of misunderstanding and hurt, it would live on and on forever. Neither night nor day could eradicate it from his heart, but it made him forge ahead in justice to mankind and respect to himself, one of God's noblemen. 

Yes, Rosemary smiled upon him and her father seeing it, at an opportune time, seized a chance to snatch a few words more with him for no other reason than to ask him to dine with them the following night. Victor, having been made wise, knew it would be diplomacy to keep a little aloof and not fall headlong into his friend's clutches, replied, "I am sorry, but I have an engagement for tomorrow night.' 99

MISSING SIBYL

Bowing before Phyllis Layman and smiling, Victor said, "Our dance I believe?"

She arose and they circled away in a maze of dancers followed by many eyes, and many homes were opened to him for he had broken through the upper crust of society. A new arrival, 'twas true, but a worthy one, as all knew and wondered where he had been keeping himself outside so long, as they watched that slick head circling the room so gracefully. Phyllis smiling looked up into his face and asked, "What are we, thistle down?" "Not either," he retorted. "Thistle down would have to touch the floor occasionally, but I'm sure you are floating." "Flatterer. Where have you been keeping yourself that we have not seen anything of you before?" "Been busy establishing myself, making people be good and being among strangers. I've not been known and am here now only to fill a vacancy. Now doesn't that sound like a climber?" "You're too philosophical to be a climber and don't need to try. The world will come to you by rights." "Well," interpolated Victor, "some have gone and the rest have been just around the corner for some time." "Now don't lament." "I won't, I'll be philosophical and make my way, for I am no lounge Lizard.' 99 "Bravo! I like that," voiced Phyllis. CHAPTER X BRINY DEEP 

"Look at those birds! How loudly they scream!

They seem to accentuate the silence of the coast, like death." Seabirds were everywhere Gulls hung motionless above the yacht under the lowering sky or bobbed leisurely in the dark green waves and skimmed the water. And too, other water fowls shared their privilege of snatching up savory fish that were within their reach. "Oh, but don't you love it! I never get tired of watching their antics. Glasses draw them up so close that I seem almost to be afraid of their great outstretched wings and clutching claws. I think 

I'm a 'Scare Cat' anyway," ended Sibyl. "Captain will we anchor at Havana ?" "Yes Miss Brinton. We will give our guests one day sightseeing. Of course one gets to see a great deal of the South Seas from the vessel. We will not tarry long in these regions, for we might get caught in a hurricane, and we must avoid that." "Cuba is called "The Queen of the Antilles.' And has so much history and data of destruction pro and con that you will come away dizzy. Tomorrow we will 'storm the city,'" joked the Captain.

They went and as the Captain predicted they came back fairly dizzy with fatigue, but happy and loaded with trophies. "Mrs. Seabright, did you ever see such lovely shells!

I bought and bought till I have a good sized boxful and I have a 'Remember the Maine' vessel, with a lot of other stuff." 65

The tomorrow found the yacht on its way out of the danger zone. As the Captain wanted to be sure of safety he cruised along the beautiful Florida coast stopping here and there for supplies and excursions up the St. Johns River to Jacksonville. "Captain," said Sibyl, "I cannot make vocal the thought of the charm of life in the far reaches of this wonderful land and city. I vision that here dwell the lovers of life and brave hearts that one meets in the far courses of this beautiful world. Those lofty trees were full grown when this new world was discovered. There they yet stand lifting their branches to heaven, as in a sentiment of whispered prayer and praise to the Creator of all things. They fill me with reverence to God when I look upon them, the dear old patriarchs of the forest! St. Augustine the beautiful gem, our oldest city, at whose fountain of Youth we drank, dwells in my mind as still living against the ravages of time." "Miss Brinton, this earth is full of beauty and sentiment, if we just take time to observe it," said the Captain. "Sentiment! Ah, Captain, no wonder Jesus Christ offered himself unto physical death for the salvation

67 of souls, peopling so magnificent a world created by his Father. How small our souls, for we dwarf them so! Reverence! Reverence!

CHAPTER XI REQUEST FOR STORIES 

"Sibyl and Belle, I want each one of you to write a short story while on this trip. I want them as souvenirs of our season's outing. The frank and genial way Mrs. Seabright had with her guests made it pleasant to be with her day after day, while one is glad to escape from some people even from a day's or evening's visit. The "one of the family" attitude she maintained made her a delightful companion.

CHAPTER XII STILL YACHTING

I didn't know how it was done, but Rastus found how they caught on and clung tight, poor kid. Well he has had his lesson and he's young yet," grinned Sibyl. In that way Sibyl glossed over the boy's fright.

MISSING SIBYL 

people. She questioned within herself how her lines could be cast among such great and appreciative friends, when she was a comparatively poor struggling person. "Well, I do know God is good, and He has made me his special charge; for plenty of good has come my way to warrant the belief."

The mansion to which they were whisked was owned by Mrs. Seabright's cousins Mr. and Mrs. Burton

All were capable young people and of the world, placed well because of a rich father's position. When the party from the yacht was ushered in they found a large number of relatives assembled there which was a surprise, followed with a big dinner in the offing and a whole week's frolic ahead of them. It was a very happy occasion and all were solicitous as to Mrs. Seabright's state of health, as they knew she had taken the salt water cruise to restore her physical condition. 

They received Sibyl as an equal because of her being with Mrs. Seabright, and as must be expected Mrs. 

Seabright informed their hostess of Sibyl's ability which was called into action in after dinner music.

MISSING SIBYL 

73 her. She and Dr. Langley ran against one another with raised umbrellas in a rain storm and she fell on the street in a faint. He picked her up and got her home after restoring her enough to find out where her rooming house was and has since treated her. 

She is an orphan, a high school graduate and very ambitious to progress, but rather independent. She has a little money left her by a widowed mother. She is the sweetest-hearted thing you ever saw and she has fine principles. I heard her sing at her last engagement and I knew she would be a star when she got to it. Now you know I didn't need a secretary on this trip, but when the doctor told me about her, I made her believe I needed one and a companion.

She frets because there is scarcely anything to do. 

When her mother died she came to St. Louis to find work. She accidentally got a singing engagement through a sick friend, and the one brought her more, as you might know. Then she got an emergency place as a saleslady in the big Glidden store. She studied stenography at night and kept up her singing dates, for those rich people were good to her. As soon as she finished she got a place with Judge Eaton. All the way she was chased by luck and she gave it a merry chase and broke down under it. The doctor and I concluded we'd save her." "Well, I'd say save her, for a piece of mechanism like her is worth saving. The dear child! I'm glad you've got her and brought her to us. No wonder she broke down pushing herself like that. Bess Anderson, and I want you to come to my room and take a nap in the afternoon for we will be up at all unreasonable hours all week and you will be asked to sing at all the evenings. So keep yourself rested and in readiness each night for every occasion. 

As you and Belle share the same room you will not have a chance for rest with her. You see I want you to keep young," solicited Mrs. Seabright. "Dear Mrs. Seabright, they say you are as old as you feel and never as long as hope is alive, I'm full of hope. My heart keeps hopping and skipping like a kid." "I know it dear, and you have such an urge in your nature for 'just over yonder' that you struggle unconsciously to attain it." "Ha, ha, ha, dear friend, I guess you've hit the spot," joked Sibyl.

Yes, Sibyl sang, and got a bevy about the piano and all sang. 

She said it was sing, eat and dance the night away all week. "I truly want to compliment your people on getting good orchestras, Mrs. Seabright." "Oh yes dear, they know the ropes to the nth power.' 99 "Dear Cousin Lucy, has Sibyl handed her manuscript in to you yet? Here is mine and do mine like 

Sibyl requested you to do with hers, put it 'under lock and key' out of sight," requested Belle. "Lock and key! I'm going to read it right now and thank you for writing it. It will be a sort of

MISSING SIBYL 

75 a goodbye and the dearest keepsake you could give me in memory of our season's outing," smilingly replied Mrs. Seabright.

CHAPTER XIII FRONTIER LIFE 

They were great days of promise, those early days, when one could stand out on the prairie and see the grass land stretching as far away as the eye could reach with comparatively few furrows turned as yet for the meager crop of garden, wheat, corn or oats. 

Most of the houses were built of sod, many were dugouts, with here and there a frame consisting of one or two rooms, with the expectation of some day building on the leanto to be used as a kitchen. Water was the great problem. It had to be hauled in some cases for several miles. If it were a timbered section the houses were built of logs, of one or two rooms with an open porch room between, so the ringing of axes was heard both early and late. 

Children herded the small bunches of stock, which was looked forward to as becoming herds in a few years. The timbered land was most suitable for cotton, corn, and fruit, because it usually was sandy soil. 

The harder soil was more suitable for wheat and other small grains. Later came the digging of more wells, the building of pasture fences and better stabling for stock.

Childhood was pressed hard into service on lighter lines, as watching the herds to keep them from straying and of running errands. Their little hands and feet did splendid service in building up a new home. 

Then came the time of plowing and replowing the sod, which was killing on horses for grain was scarce, 76

MISSING SIBYL 

77 having to be bought at feed stores, so that stock suffered from insufficient grain stuff. Such was the early beginning of Oklahoma, which has since become immensely wealthy, not only in an agricultural capacity, but it also ranks as one of the richest states in oil.

Those early settlers were people of stamina, but it took time to whip those farms into line so the betterment was always something to look forward to, but like the rest of the world they met with profit and loss.

One family living in a one room basement house, upon having company to stay over night, gave their bed in the upper room to their guests while they themselves slept on the ground floor of the basement. That night in a dream it was revealed to the housewife that under the ground on which she was lying was oil, and later they became wealthy from oil drilled on their farm. So there was their fortunate gain, while many others sold such farms far too cheaply, while the last new owners became wealthy because of advantages revealed later. 

Another family, heads of which were Billy and 

Winnie Grayson, filed on a quarter section of government land. Billy was rather sickly and unable to do much work so they with their two small children were getting along slowly.

Winnie was an educated lady who had been a teacher before her marriage. She applied for the school in their district. In those earlier days in the rural districts a school term was only of three months' duration. Just long enough to draw territorial funds. "Just think, Billy," said Winnie, "the school board turned down my application and gave the school to that young, inexperienced girl from town. She will spend the money in town and so the district will lose

MISSING SIBYL 

it. While if they had let me had it for teaching, it would have been kept in the district. We could have hired a man for a month to break sod and later to put it into wheat. Thus he as well as we would have reaped a benefit from the same money. Oh, the old sod is like a rubber plaster on a farm! It has to be turned one way and later crossed and turned the other way to get it so a crop can be planted, and it is so hard and stiff on a team. I'm so discouraged. 

She hid her face in her hands and shook with weeping. 99 

Billy, rising to his elbow, drew her to him saying, "Now Winnie, don't take it so hard; the schoolboard never thought of where the money would go and you know that girl was related to one of the board, and that's why. We will find some way. You know I am getting stronger," and he patted her shoulder and kissed her saying, "See, even the children don't understand you." Both of the children had drawn near, staring with childish startled eyes at their mother in her unhappiness. "I'm ashamed, Billy, it looks so childish in me to give way. Right now it is time for your medicine and I must go out and chore up, chicks are getting right fussy." 

So she fed her chicks, milked their one cow and fed their one team of big strong mules called Tom and Dick, and as she patted Dick she had an inspiration. "O-0-0-0- Hunkadory! But why hadn't I thought of it before? Tom and Dick, you and I will do that sodbreaking; many a woman has plowed, and June in this state is the only right month in which to plow up sod and it will rot the rest of the summer.

MISSING SIBYL 

79 "Oh glory be!

It just suits me, For there yet shall be, A wheat crop."

She fully made up her mind to get everything in readiness for a month of plowing. She cleaned house, did some sewing for the children, mended up everything and the next Monday, which was the last day of May, she cooked up food to be convenient for her husband to serve the children and himself by placing the table by the bedside. Then she went out and set her stakes to plow straight lines as starters, while she thought, Now a man used to plowing wouldn't have to do that, but I must so my furrows will look straight. She stepped off sections which they call lands, plowed a few furrows on each side of her first land and burned the grass so the sod would lay over more completely and would have most of the summer to rot. Billy would be able then to rebreak the sod and harrow it well, after which he'd sow the wheat. 

Each day she would rise at early dawn and plow until eleven o'clock, then out again at two and plow till sundown. Thus she kept plowing and her heart kept singing, for was she not helping her man make a home for their family? At the end of June she had forty-five acres plowed. Billy fretted and stewed about it and said she was killing herself. "Oh fiddle and phontom! whats a few pounds of flesh to me and the mules. They will have several weeks to rest and get fat in before they are put at it again. And I'll have all summer to pick up again. 

We are not sick. It's just wear and tear and that doesn't count." She chuckled at her husband's fretting. "And those blessed mules so steady, true and

MISSING SIBYL 

strong! If I'd been a man I would have got more plowed, but I'm very well satisfied." "Winnie, it sickens me to lie here not much good and my wife working like a slave. You've done remarkably well. Some of our neighbors have not done so well because of not having strong teams. You don't know how it worries me to let you work so hard." "Oh Billy, Billy, Billy! How-so-ever, not-withstanding, never-the-less, we'll get a wheat crop in this fall," she added all this as she playfully pounded 

Billy on the back and laughed in her glee. 

He caught her hand and kissed it, and folded her in his arms saying, "You're the best wife any man was ever blessed with. If I can get well I'll make it all up to you.' 99 Just across one corner of their homestead a small brook ran which was fed by a spring. Right across this little stream the land was elevated and the side of that rise near the spring was a large flat rock lying in a slanting position. Winnie had often said. when they built their new house they would place it at the kitchen door. They were now living in a dugout. As there was no necessity of ascending the incline they seldom or never did so, but one day when 

Winnie had led Tom and Dick by their lariat ropes to the spring to water them they broke loose from her and scrambled up to the top of that incline, and as she was right after them trying to catch their ropes, all at once the earth beneath their feet gave way and they all went down, down, down. While quick as lightning Winnie thought, Goodness! is it an earthquake or a cave?

The mules frightened and squealing scrambled out and in their fright one of them knocked Winnie out of his way, bruising her but not seriously. The dust caused by this cavein and by the scrambling of the

MISSING SIBYL 

81 mules was blinding and it was some time before Winnie could see what was the cause of the catastrophe. Looking, she could see where large rotten logs had given way from the combined weight of the mules and herself. She knew at once that it was a made cave for a hideaway cave, as it was so constructed that there were no windows nor doors except the large rock that was placed with a trap that would release and replace it at will. She knew it had been built a long time as those logs had rotted through in the course of time. Out from under the rubbish of the cavern she saw the end of an old rotted bench and tearing away more found an old rusted-out oil stove. She was satisfied then that it was a hideout cave for one or more persons.

She started prowling around the other side of the habitation, and by pulling debris away she discovered a homemade bedstead on which lay a skeleton of a man to which clung rotted clothing. So startled and shaken was she that she started to run, and in so doing bumped against a homemade table which sent another shower of dirt and dust, so she waited until she could see. Wiping the dust out of her eyes she saw just beyond the table a wooden chest. 

Now, thought she, I'll find letters telling who he is and how glad will be his people to know where his bones may be found.

She lifted on the lid and it broke in her hands, but there was clothing folded down all molded and rotted, but to one side were boxes. Some of them were wood and upon opening the first one, to her amazement instead of holding letters it was filled with greenbacks of different denominations. Replacing the lid she tried the next one and found that it was filled with hard money of silver and gold. "Well, well!" she ejaculated, "This money must

MISSING SIBYL 

be put under the protection of the home," taking off a strong gingham apron she placed two of those boxes in it, tied up the corners and carried them to the house.

Coming in hastily and naturally excited, she said, "Billy, Billy! I found money back of that big rock at the spring. We are rich, rich!" Untying the apron she carefully lifted out the rotting boxes and put them on the bed before her husband. "I must go back and get the rest before it gets dark and then I'll tell you all I know." 

She made two trips more, bringing silver and gold and a few sacks of gold nuggets from which the sacks were mostly rotted away. These she had carefully carried in her bonnet. Nowhere could she find a scrap of paper in the cave as to whom it might be who lay in the bed, a skeleton. "Now, Billy, I want you to investigate and be perfectly satisfied. You take one and a half of your quieting medicine in the morning, and I'll hitch up the mules to the wagon and take you down there so you can see and hunt for evidence. You may find something that I've overlooked." "I'll do that Winnie, this is a matter that is serious." 

They both went next day, but found nothing to indicate who he could be. By examining the skeleton carefully Billy found a bullet hole in the right side of the chest, so he knew the man had been shot and in all probability his pal had been killed. He believed there had been another, as the bed was wide enough for two persons. He believed the man had got back to his cave and had died, probably for the need of care. "I feel sure that his pal had not killed him, for the money was left." So he reasoned that it could not have been murder or the money would have been

MISSING SIBYL 

83 taken. "So here we are placed with a lot of money on hands and a dead man to be buried. That cave has been his grave for years and we will not disturb him. We will give him covering and fill up the cave so it still will be his last resting place. We will place his rusted guns by him because they've been good ones and he must have prized them highly. A road scraper was left at our place and I'll take it and fill up the cave. In that way the mules will do most of the work."

The cave was not large nor deep and much dirt had fallen into it, when the mules had fallen through the top. "It just has to be done for we must not let it be known, for the public will conjecture and talk out of all proportion and, again, if they knew we had the money we would be subject to robbers and kidnapers. So we must keep it hidden even from our children. They might tell, as we know childhood is uncertain. We must work at night by moonlight while the children are sleeping.' 99 99 "Billy, you are not strong enough, it will kill you.' "No it won't dearest. I'll take heavy doses of medicine till we get it done and excitement will help me too, so don't worry.' "I'll help you Billy." "9 "Yes, dear, I'll have to let you help."

Winnie gathered wild flowers and laid them on the dead man's breast and covered him with a sheet, pulled some planks from a shed stable and laid over him box fashion so that they did not rest on his bones. They both had been raised in Christian homes so Winnie read a chapter from the Bible, Billy prayed and asked Winnie to sing. "I feel too sad to sing, Billy, but I'll repeat a verse:

'Though like the wanderer the sun gone down 

Darkness be over me, my rest a stone 

Yet in my dreams I'd be 

Nearer my God to Thee, Nearer to Thee."

When they went back to their humble home, and while Winnie got the evening meal, Billy laid abed glad of the rest, but nervous. While they were eating a neighbor and wife came to spend the evening. 

Winnie's heart sank, for she thought they must have been seen at the cave and were curious about it and had made a pretense to call. Winnie had put the children to bed and was thankful that she had hidden the money and locked it away. The children had not seen it, as the youngest was asleep and the oldest was busy playing with young kittens in the grain bin when Winnie had brought the boxes of money to the house.

Time passed as time will, till the neighbors departed for their home. Winnie never was so glad to see people go as she was to see those good people leave. "I see the moon has come up. What bright nights we are having," voiced the little lady. "Yes, aren't they lovely, but getting later each night." "Bye, bye. Come again." "Billy, I feel awful! 

I am so glad they have gone," said Winnie, and she could have screamed for very gladness. "I am glad they came for we know for a certainty that they know nothing and are not even suspicious. 

They saw nothing today, as they went to town, and you know they are our nearest neighbors."

So Billy and Winnie began the work of filling up the vacuum after first doing some digging with their hands before they could press the mules into service

MISSING SIBYL 

85 with the scraper. They worked all night, Winnie having to do most of the work and as she said, was so "blessedly tired" in hiding all evidence of the cave. 

The next day while the children were sleeping they investigated the money and found the most recent date was fifteen years old. "His people, if he had any, have given him up a long time ago. Poor fellow, how he must have suffered and never got to enjoy his money! I wonder how many died over that blood money. We must use it, Winnie, so others will get a benefit of it too." "Yes," said Winnie, "let's start a town. This is an ideal location and no other town is near. The school house being on the next corner, there about will be a good place for the little town to start. We will name it and have everything done up right by a called meeting." "Yes, Winnie, I'll buy lots, put up a blacksmith shop, a building for a store and post office, and a couple of three room houses as a starter. We must get this money in a bank somewhere, and it must not be near here." "I'll tell you. Let's write to your Uncle Jasper.

He loves you and we needn't be afraid to ask him to see about taking it into his bank and transferring it to other banks in our name. He will know just what is the wise and safe thing to do. It would never do to deposit all that money here. The bankers would talk, and we might have serious trouble." "You are right, Winnie. I will write and tell Uncle and ask him if he will help us. We will fill up some places in the yard, saying we worked while the scarper was here, as it may be taken any day. You know we must allay all suspicion. We are fortunate indeed that the scraper was left here temporarily. 

"Your fever is higher today and strength is badly spent," protested Winnie. "Oh yes, but knowing that the family is so handsomely provided for is better than medicine, for it stops the worry about it. "No, Billy, don't say my legacy, but say yours and have it in your name as it will be more convenient for us both when it comes to business transactions, of which there will be much now, for we must necessarily spend heavily. I will claim a part of it in my name. sometime but not now. We will have a good house, barns, orchards and stock, and necessarily you must see to the spending. For you are a trustworthy man and have at heart the best interests of the family." 

Uncle Jasper jumped into a light wagon schooner fashion and came at once, wearing old clothes and driving a scrub mule and a scrub horse as his team.
