Billy went, helped see that fortune through, safely banked and properly distributed. With the change of

MISSING SIBYL 

medicine he improved rapidly and when getting back home, shortly recovered his health.

Billy bought more land as soon as available and stocked it up with thoroughbred animals and prospered. He made a great name as a fair, square and intelligent man who did a great deal for the town which he founded. A few years later a railroad came through the town, thus giving its people a great advantage and the blessings of life. 

Another case, a young man whom we will call Roy came from the south into the new country to take up a claim. He made the run on a fleet horse, wrote his name on a shingle and stuck it into the ground as witness that the quarter section had been taken. 

Presently another man who was a claim jumper came along with a pal and ordered Roy off claiming that he was first on the claim. Of course Roy was loath to go for he knew he was the first one on the claim, and so would not be bluffed off. He flatly refused to move along, then bitter words followed and a shooting affair climaxed, in which Roy was shot through the arm. So in self defense he shot the man through the heart, killing him. His pal took up the body, left and never returned.

Roy got his filing papers in and started improvements at once. The years seemed long and colorless; so much to build and a long wait for fruit and nut orchards to bring forth fruitage. It seemed like a cataclysm of wasted struggle. He did not realize how rapidly the new state had grown and developed until he went back to his childhood home and saw how everything there was retrograding and how farm land had become so impoverished that it had to be clovered down ever so often or lose its fertility. He was sorry then that he had not gone back sooner as

MISSING SIBYL 

89 he would have been better satisfied with the West had he known how rich was his jewel. "Mother, I had no idea that our new state had developed so splendidly. I am going back very well satisfied indeed with the west. I am indeed," he said again by way of emphasis. Later he found his finely improved farm was right in the midst of a valuable coal region. He became a rich man, whereas so many had left and gone back to "God's Country" and rented some one's farm or took a clerkship for someone else because they lacked the snap to do their part in developing their own claims.

Well, what was one man's loss was another man's gain, for dugouts, sodhouses and log houses melted away, as it were, and comfortable houses and mansions took their place. Schools, churches, railroads, cities and towns soon embellished the new state's surface, besides the wealth of treasure that lay beneath. Enterprise brought it forth to serve the peoples of the earth.

CHAPTER XIV SOCIAL WORLD 

The apointments of the Elliot home were in keeping with a cultured family and riches. As Victor went through the intricacies of a dinner in such a home where there were especially eligible young people he thought: My! who ever marries into this household will have to be a catspaw for that base principled man who takes whatever course is needful to achieve his end. He has made money his God and is both evil and ruthless in his dealings.

Now I know what's coming, he thought as Mr. 

Elliot steered him into the library. "Let's have a smoke in here and we will join the ladies afterward." Passing the cigars he continued, "Mr. Whiteside, I have a little matter in law which 

I'd like adjusted and will give you the case if you will handle it for me. I will pay the regular fees and when it is properly adjusted will pay you a handsome bonus beside." "Elucidate," was the only word Victor interposed. "Well it's like this," and he went on to explain. "At one time another man and I went into a partnership in a deal. It was not turning out well so I dropped out at what I thought at the time was a fair price, but later the business picked up and did remarkably well. I investigated and found there was a loophole whereby an adroit lawyer might get me reinstated in the business and also get my share of the back profits. The man Rutherford Gleason has recently died." 90

MISSING SIBYL 

91 "Did he leave a family?" questioned Victor. "Only a legal heir, a widow. They had a son who died some years ago. The widow is sole heir, a very delicate woman having tuberculosis in its last stages and her life is a question of only a few months. 

She had a sister who died without issue in the small town of Bilton. Her sister had an adopted daughter which those ladies cherished greatly. Her living sister promised the one who died that at her own death she would leave the larger portion of her wealth to the adopted child of her sister whose death occurred while the other sister was abroad, and so she was not at the last rites." "What was the name of the lady who died?" quizzed Victor. "Her name was Brinton." 

Victor gave a start but quickly composed himself, but his heart pounded like a hammer and so loudly he thought it must be heard as he continued: "What is the name of Mrs. Brinton's adopted daughter?" "That I can't say. She went by the name of Brinton. Sibyl Brinton.' 99 "Do you know anything of her parentage?" continued Victor. "No, only that the mother was traveling through, was injured in a crash at Mrs. Brinton's door, was taken in there where the child was born and the mother died." "Did that child die later ?" "Not that I know of, I heard a rumor that she had been seen in St. Louis.' 99 "Well, that's not far away. You could probably have her traced if you cared to," added Victor, trying to hide his agitation and resolving to comb the city in an earnest hunt for his beloved and saying to himself: Sibyl, darling, that man shall not get your in-

MISSING SIBYL 

heritance if I can possibly protect it. I see he thinks to by a technicality, but I'll see that he doesn't if it's possible to save it for you, precious, precious darling. I rejoice to hear of even a little chance to serve you, but oh, if I could only find you! "I am sure I can count on you taking the case, as 

I'll make it worth while by a handsome gift of cash besides." 

The beast! thought Victor, I'd like to kick him out of his own house, bringing up a family in an atmosphere of thievery. But he said, "No, Mr. Elliot, I cannot undertake so vague a thing, and from what you have said it sounds rather unethical. The young lady may be a mother or a widow in need and I cannot handle what sounds shady or inimical to justice. 

Of course I do not know all there is to be known about it, but I do not care for the case at all."

In Rising to his feet he moved toward the door. the hall he received his hat and stick from the butler, and requesting Mr. Elliot to give his apologies to the ladies, departed, glad to breathe the pure air away from a place that he considered an insult to his manhood. 

Mr. Elliot stood by purple with rage, all aquiver and eyes fairly popping in anger. Apologies! I'll be apt to. If one of them ever speaks to him I'll wring her neck. Damn him!

And thus he strode back to the library in high dudgeon, while the butler turned his back and grinned. 

Rutherford Gleason, Rutherford Gleason! Ha, ha. 

That's good. He'd fight to the last ditch if he were alive, for he's that kind. And as he is dead, I'll fight for him if I may, meditated Victor as he continued on his way. Elliot, the old skinflint, would rob the widow and the orphan as well. Now I am

Yes, yes. 

Certainly she remembered the name

Whiteside, and would he come tomorrow to her home and lunch with her. 

That he would and did. "Yes, Mrs. Gleason, I well knew your sister, Mrs.

Brinton, who was an estimable lady and so devoted to her adopted daughter, Miss Sibyl. Can you tell me anything as to the whereabouts of Miss Sibyl? 

She left shortly after the burial of Mrs. Brinton without letting anyone know of her plans or destination.

I myself tried to find her at the next town to which she had bought a ticket, but could hear nothing further of her. I knew then that was purposely to throw her friends off the trail of her destined abode. Mr. Elliot informed me that she had been seen in St. Louis. 

If he knew more he was careful not to let me know. 

I sense him as being very cautious and suave.' 99 "He thinks he has a case by a technicality against your late husband's estate and wanted me to handle the case, which I flatly refused to do for I conjectured more than he was telling me. I was so hopeful that you might have some thought or drop some hint that I might catch and bring to an issue. I left him standing in his hall purple with rage and his eyes fairly snapping fire, and looking so distraught that his butler had to turn his back and smile. "I am telling you these little thangs that you may know what sort of a man you will have to deal with. 

He probably will try to frighten you into a compromise. Failing in that, he will take civil action against the estate." Rising to leave he said, "I will see you again Mrs. Gleason." Which he did some months later and found she had come through the trial with flying colors, as she had been able to get the deposition of a dying witness that when the trial did come, saved her fortune against the machination of an unprincipled shyster. She sincerely thanked Victor again for the timely warning which had enabled her to get the timely deposition. 

Thank God! thought Victor. We routed the enemy and saved Sibyl's fortune for her, and I hope some day to look into your face and clasp your hand again little friend.

It was like looking for a needle in a haystack to hunt for a person in such a great city. Thinking of course that she had taken the name of "Gordon" which she had learned her name to be, he inserted the following in the city papers. "Will Miss Sibyl Gordon communicate with Mrs. 

Rutherford Gleason, and glean important news for herself?"

Sibyl, sailing salt waters, never saw the papers, and her friends who were unaware that Sibyl Brinton was the party wanted left the advertisement unanswered.

Betty and Lester had seen much of each other and loved so truly. Had Betty conjectured at something that he had unintentionally said, or had he unwittingly slighted her at some time? If so, of course she was within her rights and was under no obligations to set matters right for an apology, as it was her privilege just to cut loose from any such friendship. But not so, always his mind and heart told him she loved him, and he believed she knew that he loved her.

CHAPTER XV VISITING 

What distressed Sibyl, while she, Belle and Mrs. 

Seabright, were visiting the Erwin family, at Norfolk, was Merton's attentions. He was falling in love, and she did not have the chance to evade him. Being a guest in his home, kept her thrown constantly in his society. How she wished the week was over with, and they on their way again. "Every night is slated Sibyl, but, Thursday we lunch with an elderly couple who have seen trouble, and rather draw away from society. The lady has been twice married. Her first husband died. She then married a Mr. Thurston, her second husband. I always visit her when I am in this city. She is a very fine lady, and was well brought up and rather aristocratic in her young days, and during her widowhood."

The luncheon was very exact in all its appointments, and very lovely in all respects. After leaving the table the guests rather strolled about. As Sibyl was passing through an adjoining room she happened to look casually upon the walls as there were portraits. 

She was suddenly arrested as one stricken. She turned pale, trembled and grasped at a chair for support. 

Turning to Mrs. Seabright, she asked: "Who is that man?" "That," replied Mrs. Seabright, "is George Gordon, Mrs. Thurston's son by her first marriage," and looking at Sibyl said; "shall we sit, you look tired?" 111

MISSING SIBYL 

Sibyl was glad to sit for she could scarecly stand or speak. "Tell me about him," she demanded. "Well, he was wayward and cost them a great deal of trouble, but, he married a splendid young lady who loved him truly, but, he really was, w-e-l-1, fanfaronade, and seldom knew his own thoughts or mind. 

His young wife found him embracing another woman, and amid a strong declaration of love, was bewailing the fact that he was married to her. She had been in New York on a visit to her parents. They had given her money to pay for help during her sickness, for she was soon to become a mother. She packed some cases and left the house for no one knows where. In her panic to get away, just anywhere must have been her thought. Her people had wished her not to marry him. So they supposed that was why she did not come back to them to live, but, fled among strangers and from that day to this, they have never heard from her, and know nothing about if she and child are living. He, later, married his amour as she was about to sue him for a breach of promise. Of course he got a divorce from his wife, on grounds. of desertion. His parents gave him a large sum of money on the stipulation that he leave the city and stay away. He had broken their hearts, and shamefully disgraced them, so that they did not want him about, to be a constant reminder of his perfidy. They fairly fed him on money, as it were, trying to make something of him, but, all to no avail. He is the black sheep among my kinsmen." 

Sibyl thought, Oh, Mother, Mother! how you must have suffered, and how glad you must have been when death claimed you as his own. "Are the lady's parents yet living, and what is their name?" "Yes, I think they are yet alive. At least, I have

MISSING SIBYL 

113 not heard them spoken of as being dead, so I am quite sure they are still living. The lady was their only daughter, but, they had two sons. The father's name is W. H. Shepperd." "They were a very musical family, and highly esteemed, but, of course their lives are saddened by the disappearance of their daughter. They advertised for her, but never did hear anything of her. They also made many trips trying to find her, but had no success." 

Sibyl offered a silent prayer of thanks to God for 

His help in her silent search for her name. Her parents she had not seen, but right now she was in her grandmother's house with the grandmother not conscious of the relationship. "You look pale, Sibyl, lie down here on the sofa awhile and rest," expostulated Mrs. Seabright. "I will, dear Mrs. Seabright. I feel tired and if our hostess will permit, I will rest here awhile if you will join the rest. They will not miss me, so please say nothing."

Sibyl lay there on the sofa studying the weak. face of her father, which was more fully portrayed in the large picture than in the small one in her mother's locket. She could see how he unfortunately like many others, was quite willing to live at the expense of others. It was really a fatuous face and she remembered Mrs. Brinton had told her that her mother had said he was "base" and she could see the sign of it in his weak shamelessness; as one who would not be regulated by anything short of law, and one who sometimes stretched the law. How any decent man could so forget a young wife, who was soon to become the mother of his own child, was beyond her comprehension. From that moment she spurned his memory, and hoped she would never meet him.

MISSING SIBYL 

Just think what he had done to his own mother, who was left living in the muck which he had dumped at her feet. He had cut out all social prestige, except pity, from under her feet and filled her with trouble and sorrow at wondering what had become of that innocent daughter-in-law, and her at the time unborn babe. Never one word had come from her or the child. How was she supporting herself or her little one, and what advantage could she offer her child?

Sibyl knew the dear soul sat in the next room with stooped shoulders and clouded eyes trying to ferret out what she never would be able to. Her heart went out to that grandmother in a great tenderness. 

Oh, Grandma, Grandma. You had the sense to cut him off from your vision, but, I dare say your heart wants him. I'll not let myself be known. I'll not throw a part of his depraved life in your dear face afresh, at least not now. I'll see what the future for your sake demands. She arose freshened her face, gave her father a last look, then entered the room where the rest of the company were assembled. "Well, dear, did you have a good rest?" asked her grandmother. "I feel better, thank you Gra- (she came nearly tripping). Dear Mrs. Seabright is certainly spoiling me. I never spent such lazy a time in my life, as 

I've spent with her," explained Sibyl. "Well, child, you burnt the candle at both ends and when that happens there comes a halt sometimes and somewhere," remonstrated Mrs. Seabright. 

Sibyl started toward the piano saying: "I'm going to sing an old song which I learned at Sunday School. 

In childhood those old gems get under the skin; but 

I've observed, with the old, they've worked their way through and nestle in the heart.

MISSING SIBYL 

115 "What a lovely thing to say, and a beautiful thought expressed," interpolated Mrs. Erwin. 

Sibyl sang, "What a Friend We Have in Jesus."

In her sorrow and state of feeling she sang it as no one else could have sung it. When she turned from the piano she noticed several handkerchiefs wiping cheeks; her grandmother and Mrs. Seabright were quite vigorous with theirs. She wanted her grandmother to remember her. There was no one but 

Christ who could help her, and she was well aware that her wound was almost healed. When her party left Mrs. Thurston's, Sibyl kissed her grandmother on both cheeks, gave her a tender hug and said: "Don't forget me." "I never will; it seems to me that I have met you before. Lucy, she makes me think of George's first wife." "Yes, I've thought she made me think of someone

I knew and since you speak of it, she does look some like her." "What was her name?" asked Sibyl. "Her name was Leila," answered her grandmother.

So Sibyl was learning more about her mother. She tried to be cautious as possible so not to make them think her snoopy, therefore she tried to drop her questions in rather a careless manner as though just making talk, but she was getting almost all the information she wanted there, until she should hunt up her people in New York. She aimed to be circumspect and might never even let Mrs. Seabright know that she was even remotely related to her. Her grandmother had a pleasant home which she could share with her granddaughter, but Sibyl did not want to live with them under such conditions; rather desiring to make her own way in the world. 

Well, the night was coming on, and another big

MISSING SIBYL 

crowd of relatives and their friends where she would sing, eat and dance the night away and she only knew what the relationship was. 99 

When Merton asked Sibyl to take a motor trip the next morning she tried to make an excuse. His mother coming in just then heard the talk of the two young people, so she chipped in, as it were, saying: "Go Miss Brinton, it will do you good to take a ride in the fresh air as the morning is lovely.' As a guest in their home she was almost compelled to go. Mrs. Seabright also seconded the request for her going, remarking that she was not getting outdoors enough. Sibyl was rather wan since the discovery of her parentage and the load of keeping it all to herself. She pitied Merton, for he was so sweet, kind and solicitous. He had showered her with orchids and candies and she knew what was coming; and she so much hated to wound him.

She realized the Erwin family were very kind to her, a stranger and a poor girl. She was distressed that they rather overwhelmed her in so much that she could not escape the more than attentions of their son, or of the whole family, as far as that was concerned. Of course they saw she was gifted with a wonderful voice, and that she was good looking, energetic, intelligent and sensible, as well as having a fine personality. So who could be more fitting for their son's wife. As they could see, he was deeply in love with her, and they would accept any one whom he so chose, since wealth did not weigh in the balance. 

They had enough for all so they very sensibly preferred it should be a love match; so whether or not she went riding with him, he would find a means of declaring his love. But in refusing him, while a guest in his home, she was in honor bound to explain to

MISSING SIBYL 

117 him of a love which she held for another, so could not love him in that way. "Oh, what a mess my silent hunt has precipitated me into, within the Mrs. Seabright relations! We are all related, both near and distant; and everything is closing around me so fast I can scarcely keep my head above the waterline," she thought.

Merton came around with his shiny roadster, handed her in and away they sped amid smiles and handwaving. 

His mother was sure he would get his heart's desire, for was he not a match for any girl? She was sure none would turn down her handsome well informed boy, who was climbing up rapidly in his father's business and was his father's right hand; whom they had educated and then turned into the business. Yes, that beautiful gifted girl would be a shining star in their family. 

She smiled happily to herself. Mrs. Seabright saw and understood.

The young couple motored around over the city, seeing the worthwhile sights; then out to Mr. Erwin's fine estate, where everything seemed to speak in allurement for his son.

They walked in the grove down to the cataract, where he seated Sibyl on a stone bench in the shade and they sat talking. Merton told her of his interest in his father's business in the mills, and how much higher he intended to climb. Then he took Sibyl's hand and said: "Sibyl, may I call you Sibyl? I love you, darling, I loved you at first sight and want you to be my wife. Darling, I love you so dearly! Will you give me your hand and heart in marriage and bless my life with your love?" 

Sibyl did not draw away her hand, but with the other hand reached and covered his hand, and with

MISSING SIBYL 

tears in her eyes looked kindly into his saying: "Merton, you are a prince. You did not ask me for my hand alone. You asked for my heart also, dear friend. I denied my love, and told him I would not be a millstone around his neck. He was a poor law student and I would not allow him to give up his struggle for a career by a marriage to me, so I told him I did not love him that way. He has my heart, and I could never love another. Don't you see a loveless marriage would be perdition? I very much admire you as a dear friend, and I highly esteem the honor you have conferred upon me by asking me to be your wife."

She noticed his face set like a stone, and white as death, but she had to go on saying: "Don't you see I cannot do it dear friend? I've told you all because I am a guest in your home, where I've been treated royally." She withdrew her hand and wiped the tears from her eyes and cheeks.

He rose to his feet taking hold of her arm to assist her in rising as he said: "Don't weep about this, Sibyl. I compliment you for your high courage. I can do as you have done. I can love and live. I will never forget, little friend. You are a goddess.' "And you are a prince," she added, "let us be going. 99 They motored back a sorrowful, but not an angry pair. Merton helped her out of the car near the side entrance, which she entered and quickly ascended the stairs to Mrs. Seabright's room, not feeling like talking to Belle. She had sad thoughts for company.

She had seen him drive away and he had not returned. 

The next morning Sibyl sent the maid away with her breakfast untasted. She had felt a coolness in the house, although she felt sure Merton had said nothing derogatory, and doubted if he had returned to the house. 121 "I do so want to thank you kindly, Mrs. Erwin, for your royal hospitality. You have been most generous indeed, and I shall always gratefully remember it. Will you please carry my good bye to the young people of your household? They have been very pleasant."

When she presented herself for lunch the Captain looked long and hard at her. Then turning to Mrs. Seabright, asked: "What have you done to Miss Brinton?

I am afraid our good work has all been undone." "Captain, we have all, or almost all of us, had a jolt. She was offered marriage by the handsomest, finest, wealthy young man in the city. She turned him down because she didn't love him. It has covered us all with a cloud and we all feel slapped." "Well, that was her privilege, and I honor her for it. There are too many marriages of convenience which leave the taste of wormwood in the mouth and hidden tears in the eyes forever," vouched the Captain. "She sings tonight for a charity affair, so cheer her up if you can." 99 "I certainly will," acquiesced the Captain, "and I will accompany you ladies tonight if I may, and too

That day the Captain and Sibyl had a talk and it threw iron into her heart instead of tears, and it brought her through the evening with flying colors. 

Merton had sent two dozen red roses (love) to her, and Mr. and Mrs. Thurston had sent a dozen white ones. Some of these she made up into a cluster corsage bouquet and holding them up she gazed upon them, kissed them, and said, "Grandma, Grandma.

These she wore with a beautiful white satin dress and she looked very lovely, but how she did sing!

The Captain was so well pleased, while poor Merton, standing outside looked and listened through it all, then with a sigh turned and walked out of her life forever.

Mother used to comment upon it, even when I was a child. So she kept me faithfully at the piano practice, and I really didn't dislike it as do so many children. I was most always made up in programs even when quite small. 

I sang soprano and Mother's pastor sang bass in one of our programs. He sat in a chair while I leaned against his crossed knee. I was a kid and how that house did storm, but I wouldn't sing again. I ran clear out of the house. I felt as though I'd been made the 'surprise packet' after it was all over with, at which I rebelled. Mother slipped out a side door and we walked home together. "My mother was always patient with me and I hold her in loving memory. Hers was a life with busy doings, a wiped-off slate for the recording of a fine soul, always looking after the sick, the poor and discouraged. In fact she shared other people's troubles too much for her own good,

125 unless it was the purifying of her soul. She had a remarkable gift for prayer which is so lacking in many persons, but with her it was like a benediction which you felt. When I asked about my father, she told me he had been dead some years and said, 'Let's not talk about it child.' And the tears stole down her cheeks, so I never again mentioned the matter as it so distressed her." "Sibyl," said Mrs. Seabright, "I know you must have had a good mother, for her precepts shine forth in your life and you have been entrusted with a wonderful talent which ought to be further trained.

The special voice training would open up a wonderful career for you as you have a very splendid musical foundation to begin with. There is that pathos which is sympathetic and helps the listener to lift to his ideals, purify the soul, and give courage for higher action in life. You can be the means of helping the public from the standpoint of goodnes. It is a gift from God and you ought to cultivate it and take your place before the public just as do evangelists, great preachers, and great doctors. 

God blesses them in their efforts and makes them more and more helpful. They not only realize that they are able to help the world but really enjoy their work for God and humanity. Thus by helping His people the world will find in you the 'surprise packet' which your friends found and enjoyed when you rested against the crossed knee of your pastor and poured forth your childhood talent in song. They heard the treasure you possess and it did them good, as it was a lifting power which they recognized and appreciated."

her friend's side, taking her hand in both of hers and kissing it said, "My dear, very kind friend, you have offered me a very great and special opportunity, and because of its very great expensiveness, I will have to decline most humbly to accept it. My dear, the offer is so great that I just can't bring myself to accept it, but oh, believe me when I tell you that I am grateful for your most kind offer. You already have done so much for me. "Do you know," said Sibyl, "a time like this always makes me think of Spring blossoming time, when the air is fresh and full of fragrance and the very earth is challenging one to cover her acres with seed that she may fill your lap with abundance and fill your nostrils with her choicest odors, while she lays her choicest garlands at your feet. And all with a condescension of love, for we are only creatures, not angels. How the pleasant moments enthrall us if we will only give them eye, ear, and a loving consideration.' 99 "Sibyl, you should be a writer as well as a singer, for your heart responds to sentiment of lovely thought.

CHAPTER XVI DAUNTLESS 

It was a sweet spring day, soft and glorious as summer. Any one looking across the green meadows of the Somers plantation, now so full of promise and early fruitfulness, would have wondered what could make the laborers so gloomy and the workmen sigh instead of singing at their work if they knew nothing of what was happening only a comparative few miles away.

It was the year eighteen sixty-two and one long year of the Civil War had been raging in the United States of America. Two rival governments had been set up. One presided over by Lincoln of the North, and the other one by Davis of the South. It was not to be wondered at that men looked gloomy and sorrowful, for these were dark sad times for everyone. 

Somers plantation consisted of many acres of orchard, groves and cotton fields, which served as a beautiful setting for the fine, roomy old mansion whose towers lifted skyward, embellished the grand old building which glistened and gleamed in the morning rays of the rising sun.

There sat one, at an upper window, whose heart was heavy and sad and whose eyes saw not the beautiful golden ripples which broke into a myriad of golden stars as the Savannah River flower peacefully on to lose itself on the bosom of the broad Atlantic. 

The Savannah, with its skirted fringe of trees, was in close proximity to this lovely home, and was a source of great enjoyment to its inmates. 128

MISSING SIBYL 

129 The one at the upper window was listening for the whistle of the morning train. Had it been able to make its way through? It was time. Bridges were burned, railroads torn up, and the country devastated. 

Just the evening before her guardian, a distant relative whom she called Uncle James, had sighed and looked worried for very discouraging reports were abroad. Fort Pulaski at the mouth of the Savannah had been taken, and other posts were yielding. Rumors were very dark indeed. Hastily brushing aside these dismal thoughts, Joy Lilliard descended to the breakfast room, there to be eyed sternly by her Aunt Louise.

She had brought up her family to be on time at all times so as to cause no delay of the servants. She managed well and drove them all hard in service. As 

Joy was late her Aunt looked her disapproval. The breakfast was a quiet one. A cloud without the "silver lining" hung over the well appointed table, and scarcely lifted with the last course when the family, cheered with the hope of soon being released, showed some animation. 

As Joy left the chilled atmosphere of the group, she thought: Those who remember yesterday and forget tomorrow are downright failures. Aunt Louise knows the niggers are restless and her manner makes them more unsettled and panicky. Uncle James, in his indignation and worried heart makes some of the slaves in a degree saucy and treacherous. As for me, I'm going to some of those cabins.

There were rows, groups and dots of cabins, for there were many slaves. "La, la, Bless yo chile. How glad Cindy be to see yo all. Do set right heah in dis yere cheer," which chair Cindy set forward while wiping her steaming face and hands. She had been bending over the

MISSING SIBYL 

wash tub doing the washing for the big house. The first boilings were already lazily fluttering on the line, showing Aunt Cindy had been up and about early. "Now, deah chile, wat be a ailing yo? Youse looks in de eyes lak a hote bode." "Oh, Aunt Cindy, Lydon comes home tonight." "Yas, yas.

Now doan yo worit bout hit. I'se knows ow yo alls feels bout hit.' 99 "Aunt Cindy, I dread it so, I can't think, I can't do. I must go back somehow; I feel I'm sorta watched."

She passed out into the sunshine and stopped at another cabin where she heard a loom pounding, and stopping in the doorway surprised Aunt Chloe, busily engaged at weaving a lovely carpet for the mansion. "La sakes, dar am de deah chile! Do cum rit heah." "No, Aunt Chloe, I must go back or Aunt Louise will send for me and I want to start a piece of fancy work this morning.' 99 "Yas, yas. An you got ole chists ful a booful thins mak by yo booful hands."

She passed other cabins, stopping just long enough for a nod, a smile and a wave of the hand, for she had heard the spinning wheels and so did not tarry until she came to Aunt Liza's door, where she found her sitting and hemming sheets and pillow cases for the rich house; while Uncle Ezra sat near by knitting himself a pair of galluses. Uncle Ezra was old and decrepit. Because of this he did not work in the fields but performed only light tasks. Aunt Liza rose as quickly as her great avordupois would permit; that, coupled with her rheumatism, made her slow of motion. "De Lord bless yo dear chile! Dis am de fus time i'se seed yo sen you cummed home fum colej."

MISSING SIBYL 

131 "Well but Aunt Liza, I've been home just two days and it seems good to see the faithful faces of you and Uncle Ezra. How's Bill?" 

"Bill! Him well, and him wanna go to de wah too," enlightened Uncle Ezra as he stood meekly, hat in hand. "Oh, there's Virgil now. sent for me. Good bye.' 

Aunt Louise must have "Joy, Joy! Mamma says you must come right now. 

Joy, Joy, are bees for the honey or is honey for the bees?" questioned Virgil as he flicked his cap at a droning bee. Joy, Joy, I was down at the river and saw a lot of fishes, and tell me, do fishes ever drown?"?

Not being in the humor to satisfy his childish questions, Joy bantered him for a race to the house, where she threw herself panting into a chair on the veranda, while Virgil raced pantingly into the hall, from which side door emerged Aunt Louise and daughter May, who eagerly inquired of the where-abouts of Joy. Being informed that she was on the porch she lost no time in finding her and let out in a tirade upon her. "Why do you go gadding to the cabins? You'd better practice that new music that Papa got you." 

Joy was instantly upon her feet. "What right have you to interrogate me?" She had always to fence against the tyranny of her distant cousin, May. "Go to your room and stay there until summoned," commanded Mrs. Somers. "Until summoned! Until summoned?" inquired 

Joy, giving her Aunt a long searching look. "I'll go 

Aunt Louise, because it's you and I've known no other authority for several years, but Aunt Louise-❞ and she was gone.

James K. Somers, owner of the plantation, was blessed with a wife and children. The eldest a son, Lydon by name, a daughter May, and Virgil, a younger

MISSING SIBYL 

son, who, only eight years old, was the chatterbox of the family. 

May and Joy Lilliard were almost the same age, being nineteen with only a few months intervening. 

They moved in the same circle and were very much together as would be natural since they were of the same household and their needs were much the same. 

Joy, having been left an orphan at the death of her mother, was mistress of an independent fortune left by her father who had died some years before.

She had graduated with honor from an eastern university, was an accomplished pianist, and as Aunt Liza said, "could sing like an angel."

Lydon, too, had graduated but from a southern college and instead of returning immediately home to the family, had stopped on the way to visit and rest a short time with a classmate, and on this evening was returning home.

She went slowly to her room and pulled out her own college songs, which she sang and played on her own beloved piano until she was tired. Then taking up a piece of fancy work she kept her fingers busy even while her brain was in a turmoil, which seemed hard to explain. She did not realize how the evening shadows were lengthening until she heard the crunching wheels in the driveway and sensed the fact that Lydon had been brought home. She could hear the echoes of greetings below and had realized she had not been permitted to be one of them.

By and by she heard a knock on her door to which she called out, "Come in." One of the maids entered bearing a tray laden with food from the kitchen, at sight of which Joy flushed scarlet. "The misstis sen yo all yo dinnah," said the maid as she deposited the tray on a small table. "Take it right back, Tillie, as I do not want it."

MISSING SIBYL 

133 "De misstis don tole me not to tote it back." "Take it right back to Aunt Louise with my compliments, and if she sends it back I'll throw it out the window, and you'll have the trouble of picking up the fragments. Remember, take it back to her with my compliments." At which the maid picked up the tray and retreated.

Taking the tray to Mrs. Somers, as Joy had instructed her to do, she said, "Missi Joy don tole me fetch her dinnah tray bak to you wif her complents or she frow outen de winnah en I'se had tak up de pieces."

The family were seated on the front veranda, and heard Tillie tell it all. "Louise, did you send Joy's dinner to her room?

Why was she not down in her place at the table as usual?"

Mrs. Somers became very much flustered and parried not very truthfully that Joy was insolent and she sent her away. "What did she do that you took such liberty with a grown person who was not your child? Do you know in a short time I must as guardian settle her goodly estate into her own hands? Again I ask, what did she do?" "Papa, I'll tell you what she did. She went to see Aunt Liza and Aunt Chloe. When Mama sent for her she came back. When May commenced bossing her and told her to practice the music you got for her, Joy asked May what right she had to boss her, and then Mama sent her to her room and told her to stay there till she was summoned. She went but they were all mad." Virgil stretched his feet farther apart and placed his hand in a strutting attitude. Lydon laughed at his attitude, but May

MISSING SIBYL 

reached over and slapped him, calling him a tattle tale. "May," commanded her father, "you go to your room and stay there until breakfast. Louise, you go and apologize to Joy, or I'll send her away. It shall not be said that the ladies of my house may do such a trick with impunity to an orphaned young lady who was invited to make her home with us. She is no longer a child that you may abuse. She always has gone to Chloe's and Liza's cabins, and felt free to do so and may do so again. Now it's up to you to apologize or I'll send her away if it's possible to get her through the lines." 

Joy went to an unpacked case, brought forth fruit, nuts and dainty confections, for she, like other college girls, kept a supply of such dainties. So she ate a toothsome meal, if not a wholesome one. "What can Aunt Louise mean by treating me like a naughty child?" 

The tears stole down her face and her whole frame shook. She felt hurt, heart broken, but pulling herself together with an effort she said to herself: I must not give way to my feelings like this. Aunt Louise is much worried, and surely she has reason to be." 

She sat in the evening twilight thinking, thinking and yet unsettled, when a tapping on the door aroused her, and when being bidden to enter, Mrs. Somers came in. "Why did you send your tray away?" "Mrs. Somers, why was a tray sent up to me? Am

I a criminal that I was not invited to dine with the family. I have many friends in the North, and was invited by different friends to spend the summer in the North." "North, North! Don't say North to me when we

MISSING SIBYL 

135 are all working for the cause of the South. You are safer here than anywhere else." "Safe! Safe! Why Mrs. Somers, safety is hanging for us all as by a thread. Because you have made a stranger of me the family tie is broken. To you 

I am an outcast," replied Joy. "These are troublous times, child, and I am afraid 

I was hasty in sending you away to your room. I am anxious and nervous. I've been pushing the work hard to get as much done as possible, for it looks as though the slaves would all go, or at least most of them. Forgive me. Come down for breakfast. 

Good night." "North! North! I see she is divided from us. 

What will James say?" 

When Joy awoke the sun was streaming across her face. She sprang out of bed, plunged into her bath and after a careful toilet, in which she wore a simple blue frock just the color of her eyes, descended to the lower hall and hastily tripped into the dining room just as the family were being seated for breakfast.

Lydon quickly arose from his chair and met her with extended hands in greeting, but his heart gave a great bound as he looked into that fair face with its wide open trusting blue eyes, faultless nose, exquisite lips curved as the dainty petals of an opening rose, wealth of golden hair with strong glints of sunshine, and her highbred manner of graciousness. "Why, Cousin Joy," he exclaimed, "how you have changed since I saw you last! It has been two years, you may remember, since we last met, as every time one was home the other was away at school or touring. You have grown into quite a beauty." He led her by the hand to the chair. "When I get to be a beauty, Cousin Lydon, I'll do

MISSING SIBYL 

as they do at the banks. I'll call some one in to identify me.' 99 Mrs. Somers scowled when she noticed the graciousness each bestowed upon the other. 

May hastily remarked, "A pretty face is not everything. The most important is a fine mind.' 99 "Mind, mind! How intangible a thing is the mind.

The best mind is the one which attends to its own business, Cousin May." She turned to Lydon, asking questions about his homeward journey. 

May flushed crimson but said, "Joy thinks she's mighty smart.' 99 "Maybe I'm like the man in the moon, who came down too soon with no welcome place to stay. But you talk like one hard pushed, May. Uncle, is it possible for one to get through the lines?"

Mrs. Somers looked hard into her plate. Lydon lifted a napkin to his lips but the smile shone in his eyes. While Mrs. Somers replied, "I am unable just now to say, Joy."

The meal passed off in a desultory way and immediately the family dispersed. They all seemed gloomy and distraught about something more than usual. 

Joy strolled out on the grounds and up to a fountain where a beautiful figure was in the act of pouring water from a seashell into the basin and the birds were taking their morning bath. Virgil came up leading his white pony and said, "Cousin Joy, I'm going to take a ride on snowdrop. I do wish I was a man like

Lydon so I could go to the war. I'd-I'd-" "Cousin Virgil, older heads than yours are perplexed about this war and the problems it entails, so let's you and I not talk about it."

At which he climbed on his pony whose head was turned toward the driveway and soon his silver heels were heard pounding the graveled road.

MISSING SIBYL 

137 Joy, having gathered a basket of roses and ladened an arm with lilies, was slowly passing through the hall, when she heard a quick hst! Looking hurriedly about she discovered Lydon beckoning her to enter the library in which he was standing and looking much concerned. 

Lowering her gaze to the lilies, she moved unconcernedly along to where she saw one of the colored maids. "Here Celia, fill the vases with these. I fear I have not enough so must gather more. Take these on the side porch so as not to make a mess in the house." "Yes, Missi Joy," and taking the flowers she moved away as Joy sped through, and looking squarely into 

Lydon's eyes questioned, "What is it Lydon?" "Joy you are being watched." "I know it Cousin Lydon. May was at an upstairs window watching me while I was gathering flowers, and I felt sure your mother was behind a curtain also watching me." "I told Mrs. Somers I could go to friends in the

North. I revealed to her my sympathies were with the North, and she readily sensed the fact. I was as delicate as possible about it. Aunt Cindy told me when I last saw her that you were unwilling to espouse the Southern cause.' 99 "Joy, I will not, but I didn't know about your sympathies, and conjectured only when I discovered that you were being watched, and I wanted to hear it from your own lips.' 99 "I told Mrs. Somers I could go to friends in the 

North," interposed Joy. "Don't do it Joy. Stay with the family as long as possible. I feel that it may be safer with them,

MISSING SIBYL 

although things look mighty dark indeed. Father wants me to join the Southern forces and has a command offered me. We talked it over until a very late hour. I never saw him more decided about anything than the fact that I must join the Southern army, and has given me just till today to comply with his wishes. 

Oh, it seems terrible to me! I do so hate to disobey my father. He will be so disappointed, but what shall

I do? I can't—” "Lydon, study this question well and follow the promptings of your own heart. Do not do that which will leave a sorrow hanging over all your future life. 

Bill wants to go to war, too," informed Joy. "Bill! Why, Bill is mine. I'll free him and take him along with me. Hark! I hear footsteps. Get into that closet, quick, as it will not do for us to be seen talking together today, away from the family, as it will make it harder for you. I'll not see you again dear cousin, so this is good bye. Take courage, and may God keep you." "Farewell, Lydon," was Joy's succinct reply as she closed the closet door very cautiously, just as she heard 

Mrs. Somers asking for Joy as she stepped right to the closet door, pulled up a chair and sat down. 

Lydon stared, blanched white, for he was certain she aimed to open the closet door and dreaded the result of his mother's discovery, but as she sat down he abruptly thrust his hands in his pockets and replied, "She has been gathering flowers, hasn't she?" As he walked to the window and looked out he concealed his agitation by coughing. "Is "What is that noise?" his mother went on. there a kitten here? The wenches are not careful enough about keeping out the cats. Joy sympathizes with the North. Of course it is because her Broadacres are there."

MISSING SIBYL 

139 "Mother, I don't think Joy allows Broadacres or anything else to mix up with her principles. Let us walk out on the lawn, where we can enjoy the lovely morning of my last day."

He offered his arm, and escorted her out of the room. "Oh, Son, must you go so soon?" "Yes, Mother, this is no time for men, especially young men, to tarry at home. I am glad I could come home long enough to tell you all good bye and remember how it looked to see your dear faces again."

The closet concealing Joy was one used to store much worn books, magazines and such small stuffs, and there hung a fancy apron which Mrs. Somers frequently donned when at light tasks. So when Joy heard her voice just outside the door, she was certain she was after the apron, and she herself be found hiding there, which to Mrs. Somers would be everlasting disgrace. Joy, in her panic, found her trembling body grow so weak that she slowly sank to the floor, and the slight noise had been attributed to the kitten by Mrs. Somers. As soon as the voices were gone Joy left the closet and stumbled upstairs as fast as her trembling limbs could carry her, to be greeted at the top of the stairs by May, who asked, "What is the matter? You look as though you'd seen a ghost." 

Oh, horrors! thought Joy. What close shaves I do have! Will it be so always? Perhaps I am the ghost. You're "skeered" arn't you?

She passed on to her room, locked the door, threw herself on the bed and wept. Oh, the blessed tears! 

How they ease the burden of the heart! Those burdens that must be borne. She heard hoof beats outside, and was certain it was Lydon on Selim, his favorite horse. 

He had said she would not see him again before his going, but she was determined she would if it were

MISSING SIBYL 

possible, when he announced his decision to the family. 

In the meantime she must act her part. So rising she rang for her maid. "Rella, lay out my pink silk. I want a very careful toilet for luncheon, and another for dinner."

Being very well satisfied, she descended to the drawing room just as Lieutenant Wingate and his mother were announced. She bit her lip 

She bit her lip with vexation, as she would rather have missed seeing Litutenant Wingate, who always showed a preference for her society, but as she would not be rude she greeted him cordially and turning to his mother remarked: "I did not know your son was home from the army." "He is home for a very short time, leaving this evening for duty," answered Mrs. Wingate. "That's why, Miss Lilliard, we came at this early hour, for I have to take in a Yank yet this evening. 

You remember George Adler? Well, he has been reported as making radical utterances; we're after him. 

He can't do that here. I'm telling you, as I know you are for our cause.' 

Just then May entered the parlor, and the subject was dropped. But Joy thanked God in her heart that he had told her as much as he had. They did not see that Joy clutched the back of a chair to keep from falling, nor how deathly white she became at those words which had so trenchant a meaning; for George 

Adler was her very own cousin, being a son of her father's sister. His health was so frail that his parents had left the North and came South to a more congenial climate, hoping to restore him. He was young, not having reached maturity. Oh, she must save the boy. But how? She resolved to keep the lieutenant's lips from again uttering the things he had told her, so during May's greetings she had time to compose herself and keep up a safe flow of conversation

MISSING SIBYL 

141 until luncheon was announced, where the Wingates were graciously invited to eat with the family. 

Joy cudgeled her brain to keep up a conversation. "So you are home for a short respite?" observed 

Mr. Somers to the Lieutenant. "Yes," replied the Lieutenant, "I have a duty to-" "Lieutenant Wingate, do you remember how you used to chase Uncle's cattle and appropriate his melons?" Amidst the laugh which was general the Lieutenant turned scarlet. "I am sure I was a naughty boy and should have been taken to the proverbial woodshed." In his confusion he forgot to tell further of his duty. "Oh, well, boys will be boys," remarked Mr. Somers with a wry grin. "I declare, Joy, you would be as bad as a yankee if you just smoked and drank," voiced May. "Well, as for that, we've got a lot of that right here in the homeland," interposed Joy, "and that reminds me, some one spoke to President Lincoln about 

General Grant's drinking. Lincoln replied that he wondered where Grant got his whiskey, for he'd like to send some of the same brand to some of his other generals." "Lieutenant Wingate," said May, "we intend making an author of Cousin Joy. She is so very wise." 

Joy came back at her. "Cousin May, with your help I could write an Ode to Oblivion, and I'm sure it would reach its destination."

In the laughter, good will and hospitality, Lieutenant Wingate inquired when Lydon would return from college. "He has returned. He came home last evening. 

This morning he mounted Selim and has gone for a

MISSING SIBYL 

long ride and has not yet returned. I am sorry you missed him, Lieutenant," enlightened Mr. Somers. 

Lieutenant Wingate darted a quick glance at Joy, and wondered why she had not mentioned the fact to him. They arose from the table, and Joy's heart thrilled with gladness. Yet there was danger as long as that man was in the house. As they passed out 

Joy lingered behind and beckoned one of the maids to her. "Tell Bill to saddle Madcap at once," she whispered. "But leave him at the stable till I come. Tell 

Rella to lay out my riding togs, and please do so quickly." "Yassum, I tells bofe of em," replied the maid.

Mrs. Wingate and son made their apologies, and were leaving when the lieutenant stepped over to Joy, and taking her hand murmured quietly, "Miss Lilliard, when I come again I will have something to say to you. May I hope you will hear me?"

Looking him squarely in the face she found there was no mistaking what he wished to "say" to her.

Not wishing to wound him she said in her gentle way: "That may be a long time Lieutenant Wingate. 

Let us not speak of such a thing now."

He dropped her hand, sighed and turned away thinking to himself and hoping in his heart that some time he might reach the citadel of her heart. Not until he handed his mother into the carriage and he himself picked up the ribbons, did Joy leave the group. 

Then quietly she slipped away, bounded up the stairs, disrobing from her beautiful costume as she went, so that by the time she reached her room she was kicking out of it and was snatching up her riding clothes. 

She hastily donned them with Rella's help, and cautioned her maid:

MISSING SIBYL 

143 "If Mrs. Somers asks where I am tell her you don't know." She sped down the back way and out to the stables as on winged feet. Rella stood with staring eyes and open mouth, and thought, There is something terrible, but I can trust her. 

Bill helped her to mount saying, "Missi, yo sho gotta watch Madcap, fo him sho am de debil for runnin." "Bill, I wish he could fly," said Joy. "Don't tell 

Uncle James I rode him unless you have to." And touching the horse she raced away through a back lane to avoid being seen by the family, knowing there was no time to lose, as she had to make a long detour to avoid passing the Wingate plantation, which was in close proximity to the Adler estate. "If dat ain flyin hit sho an nex ting to hit," muttered Bill as he stood watching Madcap lope away. "I'se wonner wa heh skeered bout." So he turned to 

Aunt Cindy's cabin to ask her about it. Aunt Cindy had been a sort of mammy to Joy for a few years, so she was much excited at Bill's explanation. "Doan yo tells me yo let dat chile ride dat snortin black debil. She sho git kilt," and forthwith she threw her apron over her head and rocked back and forth like a shaken reed. "Hush Aunt Cindy, da gal sho knows how to ride an tak cah hehsef. But wat I'se wanna know wat heh skeered ob? Heah! Here comes de misses wid some woke. Tak dat apon offen yo haid, an doan you tells heh bout youg missi and Madcap," commanded Bill as he slunk away to avoid any questioning by Mrs. 

Somers. Black man as he was and a slave, he felt as at that time did most men and women of the United States, that he had lost a battle of life, but how could he find it? He entertained principles and thoughts equal to the best of his kind.

MISSING SIBYL 

"Cindy, I have some pieces here for you to do up special. Where is Joy?" interrogated Mrs. Somers as she peered around as though she expected Joy to creep out from under the bed. "I'se doan no misses, heh not bin heah." But she thought, De misses lak ole lobstah wid no hawt but ahl belly an claw. Heh doan cah fo de otha pusson. 

Mrs. Somers went back mumbling to herself and wearing a clouded brow. "Where can she be? She's not in the house, and nobody knows anything about her? I declare this girl is exasperating. I wonder if she's putting the niggers up to something in the line of mischief, in these critical times and exciting attitudes. She may overstep the conventions of life any moment. She is so adroit and parries so well that it's like a battle with drawn swords, so that I am learning to hate her. I must be more watchful.” "Go, Madcap go! We must save that splendid cousin of mine who has the promise of being a noble man as well as a talented one. Hark! What was that? A bullet shrieked past us, Madcap. Can it be someone after me? Well they nearly got one of us, Madcap. I know it's hardly safe for me in the 

Southland, but I'm going to stay awhile longer anyway, Madcap. Oh, it's hunters! Madcap. He stands there with his mouth open and eyes wide, more frightened than we, for he knows that stray shot nearly got us. On! On! Madcap, on! On! You wonderful horse! 

You are racing the wind but I dare not spare you.' 99 

Talking thus, Joy braced her own nerves and steadied the horse until she swung around the corner of the stables just as George was leading out a saddled horse to take a ride. Her uncle, standing at the stable door looked startled, and voiced his disapproval. "Why Joy, you'll kill that horse riding him hard as you have been. He certainly looks hot and winded."

MISSING SIBYL 

145 "I had to ride hard as you will understand when 

I tell you George must leave at once, must fly for his life. Lieutenant Wingate lunched with us today and told me that Cousin George had said things about the South, which makes him a marked man. He did not know of our relationship and I was careful not to make him wise to it, hoping thereby to help George. 

They are coming before night. My God! Think of the consequences! Sell what stock you can and run the rest off. They will retaliate on you. You will be robbed and watched on George's account. Be careful what you say. Listen! I hear hoofbeats. We must fly.' 99 George, learning this, leaned over to his father and said, "Father, I am sorry for your sakes more than for myself." Being mounted he reached over and took his father's hand and with tears on his face said, "Good bye, tell Mother-" "Come, we must go at once," said Joy, and giving his horse a slap led the way. "We must get behind that tall hedge before they come from behind that grove.' 99 

They started their horses off at a brisk walk, as they dared not lope for fear of their hoofbeats being heard. George, being so frightened and chagrined and with good reason to be so, was easily led by Joy, and she was thinking hard. "Now we must ride for that run that is half full of water; make our horses jump off the grassy bank so as to leave no hoofprints. I'll plunge in first as 

Madcap would almost jump the moon," voiced Joy. "There! you made that fine, Joy. My horse follows better than he leads, and now we are hidden!" exclaimed George.

Riding along in the water they left no hoofprints.

MISSING SIBYL 

the house, Joy, seeing a bunch of cattle coming down to drink, hastened her horse saying: "Come quickly, George, we will go up this path and the cattle coming down will obliterate our tracks." Bill says Madcap is a 'black debil' and I believe that is just about right. He might bolt with me. Tie your horse good and tight to a tree."

After descending from the tree he came back astride his horse, and an awful look was upon his face. "Joy, there are five Rebs. One is with the horses and the rest are taking Father to the house. Oh, my poor parents. How much trouble I've brought upon them by one outburst of mine !" "I think you need not be alarmed about them, as

Wingate spoke disparagingly of you only," assured

She is watching me very closely. And May is a fury. 

I will in some way communicate with your parents, and my people will think Mary Johnston is one of my college mates. Take this purse. You didn't get the chance to replenish your pocket. I snatched up mine and brought it along, as I was afraid you might forget even if you had the chance."

MISSING SIBYL 

147 "My own dear Cousin George, remember I'll miss you, and you be very, very careful." "Good bye, dear Joy. What an angel of mercy and salvation you've been to me! You've saved my life, and I'm afraid endangered your own. My dear, noble cousin, good bye." The warmly pressed hand clasp, and they had parted. 

Joy turned her horse, and, with a farewell wave of her hand, started homeward, riding rapidly when no one was in sight, but slower when meeting folks.

She adopted this as looking less suspicious if the case got noised about. "So the Lieutenant had a duty to perform ha, ha, ha. My dignity and sense of right forbade him performing that duty. Yet he must never know that it was I who snatched the noose, or the bullet out of his hands, and saved my kinsman's life. Oh, God has been good to me, using me only as an instrument to his glory. The thing that the Lieutenant has to tell me I must stop, too. I must never let him know, for a refusal, however kindly told, would anger him and make an enemy. I know him to be over-bearing and hot tempered. "Aunt Louise thought I'd made a careful toilet to meet Cousin Lydon at luncheon. Ha, ha. That's just what I wanted her to think. She was not aware of my knowledge. If I have to live among them a while longer I will have to take care of myself. 

Truly, I am as one walking on thin ice, which is liable to break through and engulf me. I cannot believe as they do, but Mrs. Somers would compel me to put on the semblance of believing, thus making me a slave to her will as much as her colored cook is a slave to her kitchen." 

Riding up to the block at the stables, Joy saw Uncle

She thought by giving a partial explanation that his curiosity would be assuaged and he would keep the secret of her taking the horse. a margin of about two minutes, this afternoon, and I never could have done it if I had not taken without asking the fleetest horse Uncle James possesses. The excitement of the work in hand, and the rigorous ride has left me without vigor. I am prostrate.' "The fleetest horse! Did you ride Madcap?"

The dinner passed in a clouded way, the family making only a pretense at eating. "Joy, you look very tired. Where have you been?" queried Mr. Somers. "I took a ride, Uncle James, and went farther than 

I suppose was good for me. You know it's been some time since I was in the saddle." She dropped her hands in her lap that none could see how they trembled. 

May was quick to get her lambast in as usual, and icily reiterated: "Tired! you never know what Joy's going to do. She looks as if she'd laid a horse shoe around each eye." 99 "We will always have you for a monitor, May, but your eyes are perverted. You do not look upon the bright side. MISSING SIBYL 

151 "Mama, since Joy has come home she's so important and smart that there's no living with her." "That night you had me go to her she said she would thenceforth address me as Mrs. Somers." "Uh huh, I don't blame her for I think I know about how you approached her. May, I hate to say anything to you before Joy, but you are most unkind by your temper and vicious remarks. You drive her outside the family circle. Such petty nonsence is very foolish. Especially now when our nerves are all on ragged edge."

They dispersed from the table, but lingered below expecting Lydon. Joy had slipped under Lydon's door a note which she had hastily written as follows: 

My dear Coz: 

From what you said this morning I am sure you will need another horse as the railroads are uncertain after a certain distance, so I am begging you to take "Lightfoot" for Bill, as you have Selim. I will be happy to do that much for my country, and will feel hurt if you refuse my_gift.

Get some one to superscribe your letters and sign your name "Darleen Reynolds," and write occasionally. May the God of Luck be with you as you fight for your country and the stars and stripes, under the God of Right. 

Your cousin,

Joy.

MISSING SIBYL 

"Well, Rella, I was almost a failure at dinner," said Joy. "A failure? Not much! You weren't. I stood there where I could hear and I thought you carried it off fine. If you had not gone down, they would have been up here inquiring a lot, and conjecturing more. They would have known it was more than an unreasonably long ride and might have connected you up with your cousin's disappearance. And Bill said, 'Da sho gotta kep Madcap outa massa's site a cuppa o'days.' 999 "Poor Madcap! I'd like to put him to bed and feed him tonics a couple of days," replied Joy.

Lydon had just mounted the stairs and passed into his room. Soon he met the family below. Joy slipped out of her room and sat on a step above the landing where she could hear everything, but not be seen. After some childish prattle from Virgil, she heard Lydon say, “Be a good boy, Virgil. Grow up big and strong and take care of Mama and Papa."

As he extended his hand to his sister he said, "Father, I am disappointing you, I cannot fight for the 

Southern cause." 

May put her hand behind her, refusing his good bye clasp and said: "Do you mean to say you are going to fight for the North, and thus disgrace the family name for all time?"

He ignored a reply, but clasped his mother in his arms. "Mother, dearest, Mother what shall I say to you? 

Some time, Mother, you will understand. Good bye, Father. I have made my decision. I go to join the 

Northern forces, for freedom. My conscience forbids me fighting against the north. My Father-" "Call me not 'Father,'" interrupted Mr. Somers, hastily stepping backward and overlooking the prof-

MISSING SIBYL 

153 fered hand of his son. "Unless you will espouse the cause of the South, I will cast you off. From this hour you are no son of mine, if you take up arms against our cause and bring shame to the Somers name.' 99 "I will never bring shame, Father-" "Did I not tell you not to call me by that name?"

He stood trembling and almost shaking with rage, when quick as the wind Joy sped to his side and throwing her arms around his neck pled: "Uncle, do not cast him off." 

Mr. Somers endeavored to cast her aside saying, "Girl, what do you know about such things? He is wilfully disobedient, and so is no son of mine." "Yes he is Uncle. He is your flesh and blood and there were times when only a child I saw him fight for the honor of his father's name. Do not ask him to trample his conscience. You yourself won't do that. 

Do not send him away an outcast. Oh, my God, Uncle! Your big generous heart will not permit that.

You are all worked up and disappointed as is many another father in the South, but don't let such a hasty action bring your dear, gray head down in sorrow through all your future years. Uncle, please, call him 'son' again." 

Mr. Somers clasped her to his bosom. "Joy, oh, Joy, you always had the faculty of seeing straight." Releasing her he turned to Lydon with outstretched hands and said: "My son, my son. God bless you. Good bye."

Then suddenly collapsing into a chair, he covered his weeping eyes with a handkerchief, therefore did not know that Joy had fallen a crumpled heap by his side.

Rella, who had followed Joy as far as the hall door, hastily called a maid, and they were bearing her away

MISSING SIBYL 

when Lydon quickly stepped forward to assist. But 

Rella, fearing another outburst from his father, frantically waved him away saying, "Go, go! She has only fainted."

Lydon, with eyes full of tears, passed out of the great doors mumbling: "Son, son. Father, father, father." Hastening to the stables he ordered Bill to hurry for a doctor, as Miss Lilliard had fainted. "Fo de lawd sak no wonner effen de ride heh don hab dis affernoon. Madcap mos daid, too." "Hurry Bill! Fly! Don't lose a minute."

He walked and walked aimlessly, until Bill returned and brought word from the house that the doctor said it was complete exhaustion. "Aun Cindy sa yo com ter heh cabin; heh wanna sede you all fo yo go wa."

Lydon went to bid Aunt Cindy good bye, as requested, but there she gave him a meal,-cooked fowl, cornpone, radishes fresh from the garden, a dish of fresh berries and steaming hot coffee. It was a repast that brought back snap to his harried nerves. "Aunt Cindy, I've eaten nothing since breakfast, but didn't realize I needed food till now. I'm afraid the angels will want you up there to cook for them, but don't you go. You stay here and take care of 

Miss Joy, for I'm going away to the Union army." "Lawd be pras. I'se no noting. Miss Joy sen wode to cok ham and chikn an cornpone." "Did she do that? How thoughtful she is! Always planning ahead, arrogating light tasks to others, but assuming the responsible ones herself." 

When the evening shades were setting upon them 

Aunt Cindy filled their saddle bags, not forgetting to add her "blessing." Lydon and Bill mounted their horses and were swallowed up by the night. There was no one present but Rella and Cindy.

MISSING SIBYL 

155 When the light of reason and understanding came to Joy, it was in the waning glow of another day. 

When she opened her eyes and saw the anxious look on each face, she framed the first coherent words on her lips. "What's happened, Rella?" "You have been very ill, Miss Lilliard. The Doctor says you must be quiet and not talk. Your condition was caused by exhaustion and excitement. You fainted the night Mr. Lydon left for the front." "How long ago was that Rella?" And "Dear Miss Lilliard, 'mum's' the word." placing her finger on her lips, turned to Aunt Cindy who was walking the floor, clapping her hands and giving vent to her emotions. "Pras de Lawd. My lil lam com bac to heh ol mammy. O glora hal lu jer.” "Aunt Cindy, the doctor says absolute quiet is most essential." 

At which Aunt Cindy huddled into a chair and threw her apron over her head. Thinking thus to hide her great emotion. 

Joy wanly smiled, for she remembered that her apron over her head was Aunt Cindy's panacea for excess feeling. Again Joy's mind sank away into the abyss of forgetfulness. When next she opened her eyes to consciousness she looked inquiringly at Rella. "Have I been lucid all the time?" "Oh, no, Miss Lilliard. You talked about that ride, Lieutenant Wingate, Lydon's parting with the family, and that apron in the closet. But I'll tell you at once I kept the family away as much as possible so they did not hear much. Yet Mrs. Somers came nearly hearing it once, but I got her out on the plea of quiet," enlightened Rella.

MISSING SIBYL 

Joy smiled and observed, "If I had that apron now 

I'd do as Aunt Cindy does, I'd throw it over my head and moan and groan my thanks to you Rella, for you have been most kind in caring for, and shielding me." "I was most happy to be of that service to you Miss Lilliard, and am telling you those things at once that your mind may be at rest on that score. You were particularly angry at the way Miss May parted with her brother."

The beautiful spring days melted away into the intense heat of the summer, when Joy was again able to saunter out on the lawn down to the brink of the river and sit in the shade of the great trees, gathering there again, and wondering why she had never heard from Mary Johnston. She had received a short letter from Darleen Reynolds, not saying much, but that all was well. Thus she knew he had gotten through the lines some way. But Bill's name was not mentioned and Joy was worried, saying: "Bill, that black pearl! I would be glad to know he was with Lydon."

Joy's uncle and aunt had been to see her often, but they had never heard from their son George, either. "I am puzzled, Rella, I feel like a disembodied spirit traveling back into dim forgotten ages. How could so much happen in one day, then everything drop and break into oblivion?" "Well, it was a mighty bad thing for you that so much happened in one day. You escaped by a close margin," interposed Rella. "Mr. Somers said that

Madcap was grieving for Bill and that was the reason he looked badly so long. You know he had been Bill's special charge, and Bill left the night after your 'Mad Ride."" 

Rella and Joy both had a good laugh about it. "I'm so worried about Cousin George. No wonder

MISSING SIBYL 

157 men prevaricate and use expletives when they are ready to explode. War is a thing that tries men's souls, for they are all worked up to such a tension that they want to kill one another and disown their own children. It is laughable to those out of it, but heart breaking to those in it. Certainly our nation is hard. hit, and it makes our pity and love the deeper for both sides. I talk. He is picking up imaginary trash at the roots of a bush and every time I look at him he gives a slight jerk of his head. You stay here and I'll pick up a stick or leaf and take it to him and deposit it in his basket. Rest assured we are watched at the house." 

So saying Joy picked up a stick and a leaf off the ground, stepped over to him, laughingly placed it in his basket and inquired: "What is it, Uncle Ezra ?" "To Aun' Liza's cab'n, an' now." He stooped and picked up a scrap. 

Joy at once left him and sauntered around among the shrubbery a moment, then back to Rella and told her. With a slow indifference they gathered up their books and walked back stopping a moment at a fountain, then rather casually on to the house. On entering her room she took off her shoes and slipped on a pair of soft moccasins so as not to be heard walking, and fled through the house down the back stairs straight to Aunt Liza's cabin. MISSING SIBYL 

159 lines with a negro's help. I've been in fighting, got hurt and just recently was able to resume duty. Uncle Ezra helped me in, saying they would take care of me. The forces are gathering and it looks like we might have fighting in this region, and that means soon too. If all is well tonight, I'll give the whippoorwill signal to my comrades" "George, you are talking too much. It tires you. 

I will tell you what I will do. I will ride over to your home and your father and mother can come tonight. They must conceal their horses in the grove and come in the back way. It is rather risky, but I think it can be done," explained Joy. "Dear Cousin, you make me very happy. How I would love to see their faces again. I know how much they have looked forward to seeing me," feebly said George. "I know, now you be quiet and sleep," said Joy.

By being very cautious, she managed to elude the watchfulness of Mrs. Somers and May. Mr. Somers having bought her another horse, she put him through his best paces and safely reached her Uncle's home.

She explained everything, how they must both ride horses and hide them from the eyes of the overseer, who was ever watchful, and woe to the man whom he thought not loyal to the Southern cause. 

She was dismayed on leaving to see a cloud heavy and ominous coming up rapidly, making it sure she would be caught in a storm. "Oh, no Uncle," (as he importuned) "I cannot stay as none but my maid knows I'm away. I must get back and slip in if possible without their knowing it.'

She hurriedly departed and had covered only half

the distance when the storm burst in its fury upon her, drenching her thoroughly.

The lightning and thunder were appalling but she forged ahead; arriving home she threw the reins to a stable boy and hoped to evade the family by stepping in the back way, but to her chagrin there they stood. Her sneaking in had been a futile effort. Mr. 

Somers looked at her sharply, demanding bitingly: "Joy, is it necessary for you to go so far that you could not get home ahead of the storm?" "Uncle James, I certainly did go farther than I should have gone, but only a little way in this storm would soak one. Believe me I got plenty of it and it made the roads heavy." "And such a terrible electrical storm," scolded Mrs. Somers. "Yes," bludgeoned May, "you'll be sick again exposing yourself like that.' "Sick! Joy assembled with the family at breakfast the next morning and as Mr. Somers arose from the table he 

"Joy, I want to see you in the library right away, as I have much to do this morning." 

Joy's heart gave a great bound, then stopped for a moment. She gripped the back of the chair, but she could not look at Mr. Somers nor speak to him. 

Her feet were like leaden weights. She was like one paralyzed from fear, for she was certain that her Uncle Gregory, Aunt Ada and Cousin George had been discovered and handed over or perhaps shot as enemies to the Southern cause. Whilst all the while she had been certain the drizzling rain, which had lasted all night, had been a means of protecting them from observation. She cudgeled herself by thinking: Come, this will never do. It shall never be said that they could cover my courage with a copper cent."

She managed somehow to get into the library, and dropping into a chair laid back her head, closed her eyes and sat expecting the worst while Mr. Somers went on with the preliminaries. "Yesterday while you were gone, Joy-" 

Oh, God! thought Joy, they got Cousin George yesterday while I was gone. And he did not get to see his parents, after all. (She blanched whiter and slumped lowed in her chair.) "Why, Joy! Your sickness and yesterday's expos-

163 is losing heavily while some of our Southern highups are like jealous school boys." "Joy," scolded Mr. Somers. "Uncle," scolded Joy. Get rid of those valuable trotters and "salt" the money down, as the old saying is, in bonds which you know will be safe so if the worst does happen you'll have something left. You've got cattle that ought to be sold, for they will be stolen perhaps.

Joy went to Aunt Cindy's cabin in case they should miss her and investigate and would not find her at Aunt Liza's. On the way to the house she met Hodges. I'm going through experiences which try my very soul, and my nerves are as a bird's flight. Keep me from breaking. O, keep me from breaking! Keep my beloved and bring him safely back to me."

Wingate standing before her with blazing eyes and saying, "How dared you?" "Pardon me, Lieutenant Wingate, I'm certain you said you'd send the roses with which to play a joke and I'm certain you've complicated affairs by compromising yourself tonight. So please return to your

MISSING SIBYL 

red, red rose. Ta, ta," and she lightly waved him away. He turned with a scowl, knowing there was nothing he could say to Joy. Ah! How the joy of this night had turned to ashes. 

Yet, May Somers would preside over his home beautifully, and her father was a wealthy man. Lieutenant Wingate went back to May, making himself so agreeable that she went home in an ecstacy of joy. 

Joy's heart, too, was singing and she knew so was

Lydon's also for did he not tell her he was not heartwhole? And in such a delicate way did he tell her, not asking for a promise for he, as a soldier, was in danger of life or limb. But, oh, how she trembled at the danger which he faced in order to warn her against Lieutenant Wingate, and staying to tell her of his new birth of love and high regard. 

When Joy came down to breakfast a little late, she stepped behind Mr. Somers, and putting a hand on each side of his head said: "Who's late to breakfast?" "Everybody," responded Uncle James. "But anybody who tripped the light fantastic as you did last night ought to sleep all day, as May's doing."

173 refused to do. Is it possible you did not know your own brother, May?"
