Mr. Somers bounded to his feet, his face expressing a multitude of emotions. "Do you tell me Lydon dared to come to such a place among all those officers? 

Of course it's impossible, Joy.' 

"You mean, he dared to beard the lion in his den?

I didn't know who he could be until just before unmasking. We were in the conservatory just a few moments. I can't tell you anything about him, for

I put in my time begging him to fly for his life, just as soon as I found it was he. Of course he brought me back to the dancers in order to appear less suspicious, but when he retreated, I expected to see a dozen men in pursuit. Well, there is one thing sure, we all saw him and know he is alive and well. Did you hear any commotion, Uncle James?" "No, but I saw you reel and nearly collapse when he waved to you from the door." "Uncle, it was foolhardy in him. It was like saying that he was leaving and would not unmask and I expected them to follow him and unmask him. He was in the gravest danger and I expected to hear that awful word, 'halt!' roar like a cannon, with all his family there to hear and see. It proves to me that the Union and Confederate armies are near and such proximity means fighting in this locality. What do you think, Uncle ?" "Joy, it is ominous, but dear child, it was plucky in you to carry it off without giving him away. If it had been May, it would have been otherwise." "Oh, Papa, even now I could scream! I'm so nervous I shake."

MISSING SIBYL 

Mrs. Somers had to be led to her room, where she spent the day under conflicting emotions. She really hated Joy, but she saw her son's devotions to her were no longer concealed.

When Mr. Somers returned from the stables he said: "Joy, there seems to be a penchant for your horses. Some one rode your horse away last night, but in some way he managed to slip his bridle, got away and came home saddled." "Who could it have been? Are any of the blacks missing, Uncle James?" "Oh, I think it was one of the horse thieves. There is a great deal of stealing being done, and there will be more as the war gets closer." "Well, I'm certainly glad my pet got home," interposed Joy. "Yes, Uncle, I thought last night when

I saw so many officers that many of them would be killed in battle, and in all probability close to our homes, for who knows? Now I don't mean to bring up a disagreeable subject, but we cannot shirk the probability of it.”

The days dragged by and battles were won and lost. Old neighbors and relatives never came back, and often the only reports were the lists. "Joy, we ought to feel comparatively safe yet," said Mr. Somers. "Lieutenant Wingate told me quite a while back that he'd watch over us, and throw protection around us.' 99 "Oh, poppycock, Wingate,-he's more interested in banquets and dancing than anything else. No, we will have to look to the hills (using a Biblical expression) for comfort and help, and the Lord wants us to use our own good senses. 

"Killed in action or missing" was the awful dread looked for each day. The South was devastated because it was invaded territory, and many were killed

MISSING SIBYL 

175 and robbed who were not in the ranks. Oh, it was a terrible thing for a nation to be divided against itself. President Lincoln issued a proclamation freeing the slaves and it made rejoicing and insolence among the slaves throughout all the South. The slaves on the Somers plantation, as did those on other plantations, held a great meeting of thanksgiving.

They built a great bonfire and were circled around it seated on whatever would afford a seat. First one and then another would talk in gladness of freedom. 

Aunt Cindy in her ampleness arose to make a talk. "Freedom be's a great blessin but de sponsibility be's powful, you got to uhn yo ahls mony an wok for our vittles. But da ain no big whip over you an from dis da you never be cawd slabes. Pras de Lawd, doan forgit the capn of yo's salvation." They prayed, they sang, they talked until the wee small hours of the morning. "I've heard that kind of talk before, Rella, but they don't know what it means," breathed Joy. "Not yet," replied Rella.

It was for these untutored children and the holding together a nation that Lydon and George were risking their lives and enduring the privations incident to war. 

She silently breathed a prayer in their behalf.

Mr. Somers was able to persuade some of the colored people to stay and share in the crops, but a large per cent. left seeking novelty of change. Uncle Ezra, Aunt Liza, Aunt Chloe and Aunt Cindy would not leave their old master who, like many another old master in the South, had known what financial reverses were.

May and the Litutenant were married, but Mr. 

Somers could not understand how it was that the Lieutenant had in so short a time found a second love.

He knew it was not that way, he had wooed and won,

but if May wanted the Lieutenant, he had nothing to say, for the Lieutenant was of good family and had riches enough left to provide for a wife and a home. 

Just before May's wedding, she whisked into Joy's room and at once launched into the subject of a wedding gift. "Joy, I know you will give me something nice as a wedding present and may I tell you what I would like to have?" "Certainly, my cousin, and what would milady want as a reminder of the great occasion of her life?" MISSING SIBYL 

177 brance of all that had transpired. Her tastes were too refined for such insulting snobbishness, so she bought beautiful solid silver which was more expensive than the desired china set. She came home with the information that the red rose china had been sold. 

The days were busy days, indeed, with much planning and working for Mrs. Somers was anxious to give and did give her only daughter a beautiful and expensive wedding, regardless of how hard pressed they might be financially. Young Wingate could be home only on short leave so they could not establish a home just then. May divided her time between his people and her own.

There were more skirmishes in that region between the scouting parties of both sides and the dark ominous cloud of it continued to roll up in a cataclysmic upheaval, each side thinking the other wrong, and each as set as a wart on a nose. "Come, Rella, let's take a walk in the woods. The house today seems to oppress me and I long to get out under the blue sky. It is such a lovely afternoon, too." As she went swinging along she smiled, for she hugged to her heart the precious jewel of sweet memory, and over and over came these words: 'God holds the key of all unknown 

And I am glad. 

If other hands should hold the key; 

Or if he trusted it to me, I might be sad.'

Ah, how wonderful is this love, to which God holds the key. 

They seated themselves on the mossy bank, threw pebbles and laved their hands in its waters, while it

MISSING SIBYL 

tinkled out its silver notes as it trickled along its pebbly bed carrying along the little leaves which fluttered into its bosom. "We must hurry back. See the sun, like a golden ball, is getting low behind a murky horizon. I have been looking for someone to be sent after us," remarked Joy. "I suppose Aunt Louise thinks now that as far as I am concerned all danger is past. It has been like death to keep me, a Yankee, in the family, but she has kept a steady guard over me. 

In spite of it I have accomplished all that came my way, and now she is as a cipher with the rim off. Watching does not intercept, as there is nothing to intercept. You know so long as we keep our faces to the light, the shadows fall behind. Just think what awful shadows would now be before me if I had not been watching." "Yes, and I, too, watched for that little white tuft laid on that cabin window sill, Miss Lilliard." "Oh, you noticed that did you?" questioned Joy. "It was the sign that all was well, and the whippoorwill songs were never so sweet. By it I knew he had comrades who were cheering him up, but how trying it must have been to lie there a whole week in danger at any moment. It was like throwing him into a rose bush and telling him not to get scratched." "It frightens me even yet, Miss Lilliard, when I think how nearly he came to being found." "Oh well, Rella, a miss is as good as a mile. He just simply could not have left until that wound was healed sufficiently for him to ride. He got home and ate three good meals with his parents before going back to his comrades. But come, we must be going back or we will be late to dinner."

MISSING SIBYL 

179 in the tree under which she had been sitting. The opening was very well concealed by some branches just below and the man she saw had on the Northern uniform. She was agitated for she was well aware of his great danger and, too, he looked haggard and spent. "Ladies, do not be frightened. I shall conduct myself as a gentleman. I am a hunted man with no way of escape and am almost starved having been without food for three days. I waded up this stream as my captors were after me with blood hounds. I climbed this tree by pulling down an over-hanging bough and scrambled up in that manner and discovered this large hole after I climbed up here. I am an escaped prisoner, captured in one of the skirmishes. 

I could not help hearing your conversation below me, thereby learning your sentiments concerning this great conflict between the North and the South. So I appeal to you for assistance in getting away from this locality. I had been here an hour when an armed 

Confederate stood just across this stream, watching for two hours for me to pass up or down this stream.' There is one of red pepper; rub the pepper well in the soles of your shoes and you will be free of blood hounds. Here also are the apples first.

Virgil was rejoiced that Joy rode with him, for with so many grave matters before the family he was compelled to take childish pleasures alone, at least pretty much so and that being the case, he enjoyed a canter with his cousin to the fullest.

At luncheon Joy told Mr. Somers that she and 

Rella would go to the city next day to do shopping, saying: "Uncle, I will deplete my bank account as I am afraid of Confederate money. I am aiming to buy some good jewels, and know I am safe there. I will not spend all, but it will be safer well concealed than in a bank when an enemy army comes marching through, and, too, I fancy jewels are cheaper right now, as merchants will be glad to unload their stocks as fighting comes nearer." "You are right, Joy, do as you please about it. I wouldn't dare let my daughter do it. You be mighty careful what you say, Joy."

MISSING SIBYL 

"Careful is my (middle name)" laughed Joy, "so don't worry, Uncle." "I don't see why she should have any more right than we do to do such a thing." "May, you ought to be able to see why. If I had the prestige of a father like yours, I wouldn't dare to do it either, but I am an orphan in this land without wealthy parents here to help hold up a cause, as your father is doing, even to his hurt. But enough of this."

When she and Rella boarded the train they found the car pretty well filled with only scattering seats vacant. Motioning her maid to one of them she took one by a gentleman who she noticed was a young man dressed in civilian clothes. She almost gasped when she observed a line of the Union soldier uniform showing at the wrist. Terrified lest he raise his hand and others see as she had, she at once reached over to his cuff and lightly tapping that line said, “Hide that; be more careful." He started, flushed and turned pale as death, for he did not know what Joy meant to do, and his life was in her hands. She saw how strained and haggard he looked as though he was going through strenuous conditions. She leaned nearer to him and in a low tone told him he need not fear her. "I am a friend and I am impelled to ask you if you have any Confederate money about you.' 99 He shook his head and told her he pawned his mother's ring which he'd been wearing to get Confederate money with which to purchase a ticket and it had taken it all. "If you have any Federal money, I will exchange at present values as I am a girl from the North, and some day I can use it."

They had just cautiously effected the change, and he was expressing his gratitude when Joy, looking

MISSING SIBYL 

187 out, observed two Confederate soldiers standing with the group that would board their train now slowing up at a station. She instantly told her companion of their presence and he, looking out, said he knew one of them. "We had an altercation. He was beating an old slave when I threw him away. He said he'd square up with me if he had to follow me to the gates of hell. I told him he'd better not as I'd push him in."

Joy was compelled to smile even in his great danger. At a quick inspiration she said to him, "My carpet bag is at our feet and when I say 'down,' you stoop down and rummage through it as though you were looking for something and keep looking until 

I say 'up.' They will never suspect a gentleman who has the appearance of traveling with a lady, in the South, of being an escaped Union soldier. I'll watch. 

Do as I tell you, 'Down,' and 'now'"-and as he hesitated she said, "you must."

He did so half heartedly, for going through a lady's bag was a delicate matter to him. While he was so engaged she looked down smilingly and said, "Look in another place for it," thus making it appear as though they were traveling together. She cared not what the other passengers thought, just so she saved this soldier's life. "Up now." They had passed on through and dropped off on the other side as the train moved underway again. He raised up again but was white as death.

His head dropped against the side of the coach and he was in a dead faint. Joy snatched up a drinking cup out of her bag, brought it full of water and on the pretense of stumbling she caught the back of the seat and slung the full cup of water into his face. "Oh, I beg your pardon. Did I nearly drown you? 

How awkward in me to stumble."

MISSING SIBYL 

He shivered, opened his eyes, smiled feebly and muttered some reply as she turned back, got herself a drink and brought a cupful to him. "Oh! If Aunt Louise saw me she would kill me."

After her act of dashing water in the young man's face, she knew those near her had ears as well as eyes so she extracted a small pad from her bag and wrote, asking him how long since he had eaten. "One cup of coffee in three days." She extracted some pins from her bag and wrote, "With these pins you must pin back that obstreperous sleeve. You can buy food now and when the fruit comes through get three oranges and suck them dry. The slowest possible way will be the best for your weakened stomach, and promise me that your next meal will be a light one, otherwise there is danger of sickness; then what might not happen to you? My name is Joy Lilliard, a girl from the North originally, and I am wishing you well." "Indeed, are you Miss Lilliard, the Northern heiress? You and my sister graduated together. How glad she will be when I tell her everything and your kindness to me. I was taken in a skirmish, my horse was shot from under me, but I escaped and am trying to get back. I shall remember your kindness in all gratitude." "We get off here so good bye. Remember me to your sister."

He silently extended his hand, almost crushing hers in his gratitude. They are talented boys.' 99 "Miss Lilliard, you always find some one who needs help. It seems almost uncanny the way cases come to your notice, and the efficiency with which you deal with them."

189 

While Mr. Verne thought, Ah me! Little girl, if ever I get home something very, very nice will find its way to Miss Joy Lilliard, the quickest little thinker

"Yes, numbers make it safer for him but terribly bloody, while you walk into the lion's mouth, pulling out one. Think what would have happened to you and to him if that train load of people had known what you were doing. It simply makes me quake to think of it," said Rella. "Well, Rella, I've had great opportunity and great faith in God who says in the Bible, 'cast thy burden upon the Lord and He shall sustain thee.' I do not go into heroism but I try to do the thing which is safe. For instance, I made him stoop over and rummage in my bag when he probably would have slumped down and pulled his hat over his face. By that act they would suspected him of traveling alone, and probably would have reached over and pulled his hat off, at which indignity the passengers would have chuckled knowing as they did that they were Confederate officers. His enemy would have recognized him even in citizen's clothes. I am afraid some things 

I made him do for his safety may make him think that I am not very circumspect. He did not tell how he procured civilian's clothes, but I noticed they were not a good fit. You know those officers search trains for escaped Union soldiers. My! Talk about poise! 

My flesh prickled like a burn and my heart quaked, but I had to smile and act cool."

MISSING SIBYL 

They lunched, bought jewels, and knew they had done well, indeed, and returned safely to the plantation home. Glancing at the table Joy saw that the mail had been brought and requested Rella to bring hers up. "I must have a bath first and rest while I'm reading." 

As she sat by the cool open window she read. Picking up the last letter she thought, I wonder who this can be from? I don't recognize the chirography. 

But when she read the name, Darleen Reynolds, she was startled, and when she read the contents she was like one paralyzed. She read as follows:

My Dear Joy: 

You will probably be surprised to hear from me, after so long a time. I am well. 

All of you must go on a visit to occasionally have a good time, and tease Uncle Bert, up country, for we are believing you need the rest, which is coming at once to that part of the country where folks work so hard, with no recreation. 

Trust me for giving advice and go easy as you can and don't worry. 

All of you take something. Danger not yet there. 

Ever your friend, Darleen Reynolds. 

My! How did this ever get through? He most surely went into danger to get this into the mail, was 

Joy's thought when she read her letter. But she was rejoiced to know Lydon was alive and well. She thought how good God was to let her know that, but yet there was a great undertaking ahead for her.

MISSING SIBYL 

191 The family was to be apprised of the news and sent away that night, sometime even before midnight, when their going would be safer. Oh, what a confusion there would be! She could visualize May raising pandemonium.

She rang for her maid and asked her if she would be afraid to stay with her, to ask tolerance and to protect the dear, old home. "No, Miss Lilliard, I will not be afraid to stay where you are. 

In fact I shall feel safer," answered

Rella. "Dress me in something simple and washable, for

I shall have a strenuous time getting them off. I want to get Uncle James off the premises, for he never would quietly submit to the ravaging of his plantation, and unless he did submit gracefully, his fine old home would be reduced to ashes, for such is war."

When Joy entered the dining room May let out a jeering laugh as she said, "Joy, you look as if you were going fishing, hunting, or something in that dress." "What's the matter with my frock, May? It certainly is clean and well ironed, but instead of hunting or fishing, it must be for something that I will do. 

But say, let's all eat a hearty meal." And as she noticed Mrs. Somers was not eating well, she said, "Mrs. 

Somers, eat another one of those delicious biscuits." 

And to Virgil, said, "Eat your desert." "Don't want it, Cousin Joy." "Perhaps you don't, but eat it to please me." 

Mr. Somers eyed her wondering what she could be driving at, but his better judgment prompted him to help, so he said, "Yes, Louise, eat another. Give me more, everybody eat one more," and amid the general laugh, they grabbed a biscuit in play, spread it

MISSING SIBYL 

with butter and jam, and ate while Joy thought, Oh, when will we ever have so happy a feast again? 

Mr. Somers, with a twinkle in his eye, said, "Now, Joy, show your hand. I think I see the sign." "Very well, Uncle. You remember you called me to the library once upon a time? It's my turn to call you and all the rest to the library, as I have something of importance to impart to you."

Mr. and Mrs. Somers were plainly astonished, but 

May sneered as Joy took pains to see the doors were well closed, lest the servants should hear. 

When they were all seated Joy produced the letter and read it aloud. Before she could explain, May exclaimed with a whoop. "Well I declare, Joy, you acted like you had something important to tell us, and here you read a silly school girl's letter." "Listen, Uncle, if the letter had sounded with a better arrangement I might have overlooked its importance. Rather, I should say it sounded queerly enough for me to study it. A long time ago as children, Lydon and I perfected a type of writing in which every other line was to be read for the hidden meaning, in case the name should be underscored. Now, that is not new to the world at present, but it was to us as children. In this letter you will see that my name is underscored and I will read every other line as follows: 

"Now listen again. I wrote a note to Lydon and slipped it under his door, telling him if it ever was dangerous to write openly to his family, to write to me under the name of Darleen Reynolds, and it would be thought it was one of my college mates. Today this missive bears that name, and you can see yourselves how important it is." "Well, I don't see why he didn't write to Papa in-

MISSING SIBYL 

193 stead of to you. As for me, I'm not going one step." "Very well, May, stay and suffer the consequences.

The very fact that your husband is an officer in the Confederacy will make this place, and Uncle James, a marked man. Suppose he had written to your father and the letter not gotten through? Indeed, I hardly know how this came through. You ought to know that any letter from him to his father, touching such a subject, might have brought a raid on us tonight as sympathizers of the Union cause. I am sure Uncle understands how it is, and we should be grateful to Lydon for the information even in this round-about way. You notice he says to go at once. It will be better to get away with a few valuables than to stay and lose all, and perhaps your lives as well. Tonight ought to find you started by midnight." "Joy, you are right. It is a sure fact that the boy thought best to avert all suspicion of any intercourse with his family, as he is not on our side of this awful conflict. I've been careful about discussing war with my family as I wanted to save you the torture of worry and anxiety as much as possible. But I'm telling you right now that Sherman, with his great force, is marching through Georgia and laying waste as he comes," informed Mr. Somers. "Oh, My God! Will they get the Lieutenant," wailed May. "We'll pray God that he may be spared May," said her father, "but, there will be many who will not be left about their firesides, and their homes will be burnt to ashes." "Oh, let's go, Father. Let's go this night." "Yes, James, I think we had better go, and go at once, but I do think Lydon might have found some means of communicating with you or me," complained 

Mrs. Somers.

MISSING SIBYL 

"Means, means! I'd like to know if that letter wasn't means. Louise, I am glad he wrote as he did. It makes it safer for us all, and I am glad he and Joy made up that little by-play of writing, for it is serving us all a good turn. You know we have not suffered as much as those who lived up the river, nor as much as those who lived farther down stream. 

Now we must plan how we are going and what to take." "I think Joy might have told us sooner," growled

May. "I might have told you an hour sooner, but wanted all of you to have a full meal first. I did not know until I got back from the city, and that was not long before dinner was announced. I knew it would be better for your strength, to let you eat before breaking the news.' 99 "Is that why you dared me to eat that extra roll?” said Virgil. At this they all laughed and he threw his cap at Joy, while she gayly ran to him giving him a tight hug and kissing him, for she was very fond of this little cousin of hers. "I wanted to be sure that you had eaten a great big dinner, as long rides make one very hungry. So 

I was glad to see you eat the roll. Well, folks, what have you decided upon?" "James, we must go tonight; the darkness will shelter us.' 99 "Yes, Louise, we will take both carriages. Elon will drive one team while I will drive the other." "I'll have a nigger drive some horses and another one cows, but they may never get through. Those animals will require feed which will be sacked; and the light wagon will be filled with sacks, so don't think of putting anything in that wagon.' 99 "Now, Louise, don't think of taking any silver ex-

MISSING SIBYL 

195 cept knives, forks, spoons and a few extra small pieces." "Oh, James, I must take our solid plate such as trays, pitchers, etc." "No, Louise, if we get back we will probably find our home in ashes, and we can dispense with our fine plate. The things to take must be some bedding and clothing." "Now everybody get busy, get busy, and have the niggers help you; for we will leave them here to look after the crops and stock and to protect the place the best they can.' 

Joy tripped upstairs glad to avoid the confusion which she knew would follow, so she gained her room to think for a few minutes. "Miss Lilliard, that May is perfectly exasperating!

I felt just like I could slap her up one side and down the other. It's terrible out there now, with her storming around at the colored help. Have you fully made up your mind to stay? If so, I will stay with you, for I want to be where you are." "Rella, I did not press you hard for an answer. I gave you your time to think, and I wanted to know what the family intended doing about it. Why, Rella, love, marriage, death, all the amenities and sanctities of life are bound up in this beautiful house, and I am certain that I as a Northern sympathizer can successfully plead for it, and be the means of saving it from being destroyed." "I believe so too, Miss Lilliard. Did you notice how many loads of families and stuff we saw yesterday?" "Yes, I did take note of the fact, and as soon as

I read Lydon's letter, I understood it. Now we must get busy. Take two of my cases and fill with their linen clothing. They will think we have our clothing within them and thus we will fool them when loading

MISSING SIBYL 

up. I think after all I'll go out and help them, but first I'll go to the kitchen and get the blacks to cooking, for the family will have to eat their breakfast on the way. 

Aunt Louise will never think to have it prepared, but she will blame everyone else for not having it done. She and May are incased in a crust of self-sufficiency, and expect the rest of us to look after outside interests in which they wish to share." 

Going back to the library, she found her Uncle James sitting just where they had left him, with a deeply perplexed brow as though trying to piece a way through all this war trouble. "Uncle James, have you gathered up your important papers or do you have them all banked?" "Yes, most of them, Joy, but there are some which 

I must take since you've reminded me. Tell the coachman to get two of the best driving teams to the carriages and bring them around to be loaded up."

Joy was everywhere at once it seemed. They all wanted her at once. They wanted to know what to take, and it kept her busy preventing them from taking what must be left out for others more useful and most needed. "Joy, you sort out what you think I must take.

When I try to, I find I get so many the cases will not hold them." "Certainly, Mrs. Somers, I know how one gets caught in that whirl. All of you must have plenty of changes, but it must be plain serviceable changes. 99 "I wish you'd help May, Joy," begged Mrs. Somers. "I offered my services, but she sent me away and I'm sure she has a carriage loaded now. She doesn't pack properly. She just throws them into her cases and in that way wastes space.' 99 So it was hurry scurry. When they started the carriages were so loaded there was not room enough

MISSING SIBYL 

197 for the family. Joy and her maid went to the carriage that Mrs. Somers was driving, but it was full as she well knew. "I declare, Joy, you'll have to take the other carriage as we are full up in this one." "Very well, Mrs. Somers." 

She turned back to the other carriage saying to May, "Have you room in here for us?" "My lands! No. I've hardly got a place for myself let alone for two more," May slammed back in irritation. "All right, May. Drive on Mose." But Joy felt very sober when she saw such selfishness exemplified. 

The girls quietly stepped behind a cedar, where 

Rella indulged in a quiet laugh. Joy felt wounded and realized how very grave was their situation, knowing there are moments more important than half a lifetime. She thought this was one of the moments for her and her maid, who was so loyal. She was quite certain her uncle would not know they were left until the stop for breakfast, which would be much over half way on their journey. Well, that was just what she aimed should happen, and how easy it happened, showing how little they cared for her. That act sank deep in her heart and as she stood there she silently prayed

God's protection, of herself and all under her care. "Aunt Cindy!" "Fo de lawd's sak, Missi Joy, I'se sho yo wen wif yo unk." "There wasn't room, Aunt Cindy, and now I want you and Aunt Chloe to sleep here in the house in a room next to mine. Please tell Hodges and the butler to come to me here in the hall. I want a moment with them," commanded Joy. "Yas, yas'm, I'se gwine to gits em," answered Aunt Cindy.

"Wait, Rella, don't go up yet. I wish to speak to 

Hodges, and I want you present. There he comes now. . . . Hodges, there was not room for me and my maid in the carriages so we are left on your hands with the aid of the butler to watch this house and protect us all. You and the butler will sleep in the hall." "Very well, Miss Lilliard, I'll make it a time to watch close," said Hodges. "Tomorrow morning I want you to bring me a pistol, and give us a half hour's practice with it. See that it is a good pistol. It is almost daylight now.' 99 "I will, Miss Lilliard, and you go get some sleep now, as it's way past midnight. I'll be on hand later. "All right, Hodges. I'll depend on you," so saying, she and Rella passed up stairs to their rooms. "Rella, you must sleep with me in my room. We must stay close together these trying days," counseled 

Joy. 

She lay down to rest, but could not sleep. She thought: Of course Mr. Somers had told Hodges to watch the house, but I wanted him to know we were here. He will be more careful and not so apt to pilfer. 

Daylight found Joy up collecting the silver plate which, with a lot of other things that were valuable, she carefully packed in a large box which Ezra carefully buried and then removed all traces of newly turned soil. After breakfast she summoned Hodges. "Hodges, I suppose Uncle told you what to do with the stock?" “Well, he said it wan't any use to do anything, Miss, as the Yanks would take them anyway, and I guess he was right." "I am not so sure of that," countered Joy; "you have the blacks fix up stalls and mangers in some of

MISSING SIBYL 

199 those caves that have washed into the river banks, and put grown mules and horses in one and as many thoroughbred cows as you can in another. In one fix a pen for a lot of those thoroughbred hogs and shoats. Those penned up creatures will have to be well fed or else they will be noisy. They can get water themselves, as it runs part way into the caves. 

Of course, you'll have to put up some fencing or they may swim out. Fill one cave with grain. Loads and loads of it, as they will need it after they are let out of there; that is if we are able to keep them from being discovered. Fill one of the smaller caves with a lot of those thoroughbred half-grown chickens that Mrs. Somers is so crazy about. Don't put the grown ones in as they will crow and cackle and give everything away. Take the head stalls off the older colts and turn into the woods, they can not be ridden nor are they easily caught; of course they could be killed, but we will have to risk something. Put the young calves in with their mothers or there will be bawling which we must not risk. Some stuff will get trampled or killed, but we have to risk it." "Well I'll be jiggered, Miss Joy, you are some schemer, and the pint of it is I think it will work," exclaimed Hodges, with a broad grin. "Listen, Hodges, for keeping the secret tell them they may barbecue a dry cow. You choosing the one and divide it up among them.” "Barbecue a cow! Why for that," roared the overseer popping his fists together, "they'll hurry and hold their tongues, too." "Now, Hodges, I'm going to delegate another duty to you. If any soldier, Confederate or Union, comes on the place you send him to the front door. Please be polite. I'll meet them there, and do the talking.

MISSING SIBYL 

Now don't forget, for we've got to use diplomacy. 

That is all now, Hodges." 66 'Plomacy, 'plomacy," muttered Hodges, as he turned away. "What is that? Well, I think I'll let her use it, whatever it is. It is a good thing for the boss that she got left behind."

Joy and Rella had their gun practice until they were no longer nervous of a pistol, and felt safer by having acquired the knowledge of shooting. "Miss Lilliard, we've truly become 'gunmen' or I should say 'gunwomen,'" joked Rella. "I hardly dare say, Rella, and yet the gun may save the woman; at least we feel that we have an added protection, and I am rejoiced that everything was so well placed, as the overseer reported." 

When the rain came beating noisily on the roof and the downward rush into the grass rose to a sibilant note, it gave Joy a glimmering of the erasure of all tracks to the caves, and she was elated that it had been done that day. "How did you ever think of those caves, Miss Lilliard? It surely was a bright idea," inquired Rella. "Rella, I hark back to childhood days, when we used to play Indian there. In fact some folks think they were dug out by the Indians years and years ago, but of course, no one knows for what purpose. We waded in the shallow water that extended into the cave. One day, when May waded too far out and stepped into a hole, Lydon and I both had a hard time saving her. She nearly drowned me, and Lydon, seeing it, slapped her in the face to make her let loose. 

We all went home, and she was in high dudgeon, telling her mother we tried to drown her, and her mother believed it. We had to explain to Uncle James how it all was.

He sent us out of the room while he spoke very plainly to his wife, and he turned May

MISSING SIBYL 

201 across his knee and administered a dose of what she needed. We heard the commotion while standing outside. Lydon actually laughed in glee, jumping up and down saying, 'little liar, little liar.' So you see,

Rella, I had reason to remember the caves and ha, ha, ha! I suppose the good turn I tried to do Uncle today is compensation for the protection he gave me that day. Rella, the South is being impoverished to an extreme extent. Some places have not a hoof left on them, and a few only have any left. If we can save those animals it will give Uncle James something to start with next year, God willing. Such fine pedigreed stuff! I can't bear to think of losing it. He has already lost a lot, by both sides. "Look, Rella, over there in the west is a light. It must be a fire. Yes, there are others; surely it must be camp fires. Oh, girl! Oh, oh, oh! It's the Union army encamped, and tomorrow they'll be here. Call 

Hodges, Tillie, and the others for there is more work, and that bright and early." 

When Hodges came he grinned and said: "Miss, I can't save the South, if that's what you're goin to be askin'." "No, Hodges. It's the fruit in the big orchard, tons of it, you know, peaches, apples, pears, grapes and late plums. Get all the men and women together, and have them gather it, bringing bushels and bushels of it, and when that's enough, all of you gather for yourselves and take to your cabins. And Hodges, it will take early work, for by daylight the orchard will be overrun by soldiers, who may object to your gathering any for yourselves.' 99 "By golly! Beg pardon, Miss Joy. We'll fool 'em a trip! Thar's a late moon now, an we'll pick before morning. It'll be muddy but we'll do it anyway.' 99

MISSING SIBYL 

"Send for a man to stay at the door, and he will take the fruit up in the garret and empty the baskets into boxes so there will be no muddy stairs to clean. 

Thus everything will be in shape at any moment. They may be here even before daylight, and we do not want them to know what has been going on, even if they do come. Remember, be polite and send them to me.

Keep the blacks busy for we haven't any too much time. I'm glad I saw those fires in time," voiced

Joy. 

"I too am glad, Miss Joy, for by Jupiter! you have brains, and them niggers will work hard for that fruit, an' will keep their tongues still or they may lose the fruit. I'll tell 'em to hide all they can. I'll stand by you. 99 "Good for you, Hodges. You've got the kind of pluck I admire. I've surely needed you and depended on you a lot." 

That morning found Joy up with a determination to face what might come. Early in the day the doorbell rang. Joy answered it in person and found a 

Federal officer standing on the mat, twirling belligerently a cap in his hand. "Good morning," said Joy with a smile. "Good morning, I would like to see the gentleman of the house." "My friend, the gentleman of the house is not at home. He went up country and has not returned. 

Something must have intercepted him. If there is any business you would wish to discuss, I am at your service as there is none other in authority here." "This is the Somers plantation, is it not?" "Yes, sir. This is my Uncle James Somers' plantation." "Madam, you are a woman and will not be molested, but I am warning you that in an hour's time this

MISSING SIBYL 

203 house will be in ashes. James Somers is a rebel and his son-in-law is a lieutenant in the Confederate army, and I am remembering the vile things he let pass his lips." "My dear sir, you see I am wearing the stars and the stripes," and she pointed to a flag which she had pinned to her shoulder. She raised a fold to her lips and kissed it saying: "I love my country's flag and am honored in so doing. As to Lieutenant Wingate, we tolerate him because he married my Uncle's only daughter. Uncle is a gentleman. You yourself would say so if you knew him. I believe you to be a gentleman and I beg you not to burn the roof from over a 

Union girl's head. This has been my home ever since

I was ten years old, and I love every angle of it. 

Please spare it for my sake."

Stepping outside she ran the staff of her flag through an ornament of a porch post while at the same time saying, "See, I place the 'stars and stripes,' over this plantation home. The blacks have an interest in the crops, but I suppose they will be willing to give much of it to the Union army. So take what you are disposed to take, of what you can find, but please, please, spare the buildings and the house. My Uncle James has a son who is a Union soldier, and I plead for his sake which stands preeminent above my love for the home, which has sheltered him all his life. He is an educated man and a gentleman in every sense of the word as well as a staunch Unionist." "Madam-" "Miss Lilliard, if you please," Joy corrected. "Pardon me, Miss Lilliard. introducing you and Miss Joy Lilliard. She it is who saved my life on the train as I told you. I want to say, Miss Lilliard," as he took her hand which the

She and the Lieutenant echoed a hearty laugh while the Captain, with moisture in his eyes, mumbled "a cup of cold water." "You will remember I had been without food for quite a while and so was not feeling top notch and it took my promise to you to bring my will power to quit eating at the next meal, as food tasted so good." "Pardon me," and Rella stepped out the door, "I desire to tell what Miss Lilliard has been too modest to tell. I am her maid, and we are both for the 

North, and while she is reluctant to tell all, I am not."

Thus beginning she told all about how Joy had been the means of saving those whom you already knew about, and when she told of Joy's collapse and delirium, the Captain fumbled for his handkerchief, blew his nose vigorously and wiped the moisture from his eyes, while the Lieutenant took a step nearer her as an added protection offered. At the conclusion of the exact recital, the Captain stepped nearer. "I want again to shake hands with one who had so loyal a heart and so brave a spirit." 205 will leave orders that no depredations be committed here." "Please, Captain Hayden, I have one more request to make. My Uncle Gregory Adler has an only son in the Union army, and I beg that you will not allow ny harm to come to him nor Aunt, nor to their home. it was their only child, my cousin, whom I got away just as the mob came and who again, as a wounded Union soldier, I nursed in Uncle Ezra's cabin loft for a week. I am telling you this, as I think he has some claim upon you and I am pressing that claim in his behalf, as well as making it my request also." "My dear Miss Lilliard, they and their home shall not be harmed, and further I shall write home of this wonderful girl I have found in the South.' 

"My dear Captain, as soon as this war is over I shall go to my lands in the North, as it will never again be quite the same here." 

The Lieutenant saluted and, lifting his cap to Joy, left. "Now, Captain, bring your staff up and I will see that the colored cooks prepare as good a meal as they can on short notice.' 

"Indeed, Miss Lilliard, I shall be grateful." "And I shall be happy," replied Joy. 

Joy went to give order with a singing heart. A hearty breakfast of corncake, fried chicken, hot potatoes and eggs, hot coffee and cream, milk and fruit was served. Joy came through the dining room just as the breakfast was finished. She received many glances of admiration. "I will pay for our breakfasts, Miss Lilliard," said the Captain. "Oh, no, no! Captain. I am so glad, glad, glad you came, and want nothing for your breakfasts.

what stock is left. Take what you want. I know this is war, and what goes with it. Please see that we and this place are protected." "I will, Miss Lilliard, and now good bye." "Good bye, Captain. I shall always remember you as the kind hearted Union army Captain. Good bye, bood bye," continued Joy, shaking their hands as they filed out of the house. Her face was suffused with tears, but her heart was singing with gratitude. "Oh, Rella, I couldn't see what they all looked like. 

I had too much water in my eyes." "Ha, ha, ha," laughed Rella. "I saw that they, too, had water in their eyes, especially the captain. 

My! What a fine man he is.' 99 It was not necessary for Ezra to tell Joy that almost everything in sight in the way of stock had been taken. "Eferting cepn de ole big he cow." "Uncle Ezra, he would be safe right in their camp for they wouldn't eat that old tough monster, and I'm mighty glad they did not kill him for he draws state prizes," explained Joy.

When she was informed that they had left Snowball as being useless to them, and had also left her riding horse, she was touched at the gentleness of

Captain Hayden, as it was certain that he had been left at the express command of the Captain. "Uncle Ezra, the soldiers will be in the neighborhood perhaps several days, so you and Hodges must see that the stock be left in the caves until everything becomes settled and quiet," cautioned Joy. 

Joy was glad that they had taken everything at once so there would be no future affairs or depredations.

In the wee small hours of the night Joy was aroused

MISSING SIBYL 

207 by the knocking on her door and the calling by one of the colored maids. "Missi Joy, Massas down sta wan yos comed down rite wa.' 99 "Tell him I'll be down in a minute, Nellie."

Quickly robing and slipping her feet in slippers she hastened down to the library. There sat Hodges in the middle of the room, and Mr. Somers was lying on a couch looking pale as death and much shaken. "Oh, Uncle, Uncle! Are you hurt? Has something happened to you?" asked Joy as she knelt by the couch. Taking his hand she said, "You look quite miserable." "Joy, dear child, will you ever forgive me for leaving you? I did not know until breakfast that you and your maid were not along. I thought you were in the other carriage. We were so far along that they went on and I started back after you. As I got nearer 

I found the roads full of fugitives and squads of soldiers. One of the soldiers took my horse and I had to walk the rest of the way home." "Uncle, did he shoot you or hurt you in any way? 

Is that why you are so pale?" "No, Joy, I had one of my attacks and could scarcely get here. Oh, if I'd only had a gun I would have killed that soldier." "I rejoice that you didn't have one, for if you had shot him you would have been riddled with bullets. 

Uncle, can't you understand this is war? It is mighty fortunate that you got here alive." "That's what I been a tellin him," edged in Hodges, who up till now had been a spectator. "And it's lucky for this plantation that he wasn't here today.' "That's not all he's been telling me. He says it's at your instigation that the stock was taken to the caves. I never thought of the caves, for there are

MISSING SIBYL 

times when those caves are full of water to the ceiling," explained Mr. Somers. "I know it, Uncle, and I thought as far as you were concerned we'd risk it, for they would as well be drowned as run off. They've got to be kept there a while longer too, for you'll be harassed by stragglers that always follow a great army. I've got something else to tell you. The soldiers came here expressly to burn your house down. They asked for you, too, because of your being a Confederate, and your son-inlaw a Confederate Lieutenant, who had said a lot (you know Lieutenant Wingate would, for like a Roman candle, he splutters a lot). They intended burning everything down. Captain Hayden told me personally that in an hour's time the house would be in ashes." "Oh, the devils!" shouted Mr. Somers. "Uncle, it's war, and you can't expect anything else.

I told them to take what stock was in sight and grain and a good breakfast. I pled as a Union girl for them to protect us and this place. I believe right now there are soldiers watching this place." "Joy, you are a little general. If I had been here our house would have been in ashes," acknowledged 

Mr. Somers. "I know it would, Uncle, and it's not too late yet. 

If you get peppery, as you do a lot, we'll see the ashes probably. Hodges, you get him to his room and keep him there. Take good care of him. Uncle, keep quiet. Don't worry. Don't come down. If we need you we will come to you, and I'm telling you in all candor, you're no good handling this case. Try to get some sleep. I'll see you later. So long," she smilingly waved her hand as she left the room. "Oh, Joy, Joy! Hodges, if she were a man she'd be out there a captain or a general, for that's the way

MISSING SIBYL 

209 she believes. We'll protect her even if she is not on our side. There's one thing; she has protected us all." "There's no use discussin' that pint, for we sure know it, and I'm almighty glad you came in afoot and after night. There's one more thing she didn't tell you. When she saw the campfires she had me call all the hands out and pick fruit by moonlight, so there is a heck of a lot hid in the garret of this house. 

Then she told them to gather for themselves so every cabin is fuller than they can care for. Rella knew how to make jelly and preserves and such like, so she and Miss Joy, and Tillie, Nellie, Chloe and Cindy air a puttin' up gallons and gallons for the family use. 

They're a workin' their heads off but Miss Joy say it will keep for another year or we can sell it at the stores. All the blacks air a workin', women and children, to get theirs up too. up too. An' she done told me to tell the niggers if they hurry and gets everything to the caves before the rain they could barbecue a dry cow that I should select, so I picked one of the small ones. The niggers most worship her, because they say she filled their cabins instead of givin' it all to the soldiers. How they did work to get the stock all housed before the rain came up to wash away the tracks! And Mr. Somers, the caves are full of the best stuff. She had us fill one of the smaller caves with half grown chickens that couldn't cackle nor crow, the ported kind. She said you might have a terrible time to find that kind again, for they be best strain. 

She had me get a pistol and teach her and her maid to shoot till they were not scared of it. She sure was afeared, but she says she use 'plomacy, whatever that was, and I let her use it. 99 Mr. Somers rolled over on his face and laughed until he shook. Then he said, "Hodges, financially

MISSING SIBYL 

I am a saved man, and she did it. I'll always be grateful to her. Of course I've lost a lot, but I'll have enough stock to start all over again, and the home is left. I bought some foreign bonds which will bring me Federal money when I need it, but I'm saying: our Confederate money will be worthless and then what will become of the South, Oh! There are dark days ahead. I am losing heart for I believe the 

Yankees will win out," and Mr. Somers groaned aloud from agony of soul.

Mr. Somers did not improve as he had hoped to so the next day he sent for Dr. Williams, who upon arriving and looking about remarked: "How in blazes did you escape the depredations as well as you have. A lot of folks are homeless and a lot killed. I came here afoot as my nag is taken. 

I tell you, Somers, the South is ruined." "Doctor," said Mr. Somers, "it will take the South a long time to get adjusted, as it is the invaded territory.' 99 The doctor gave medicine and prescribed absolute rest. Joy was sorry to see Mr. Somers suffer, but she was glad he was ordered to stay in bed. She found her days full of details, service, and cares. She had her maid pack their belongings, and sent them to her Uncle Gregory's preparatory for her going away, and the day before Mrs. Somers and May came home she went to Uncle James with tears in her eyes for she had always been very fond of her uncle, who had always been kind to her, so she told him of the intention of leaving his roof, and why. Mrs. Somers and May had thrust the sword of antagonism and indifference so deeply into her heart that she could never be happy with them again, and she preferred her independence. She would visit her Uncle Gregory and

MISSING SIBYL 

211 Aunt Ada before she should go to her estate in the 

Mr. Somers wept and remonstrated against her going away, but when he found how she was loathe to stay he could not insist even though he loved this brilliant child of his distant relative. He well knew that his wife and daughter by their conduct had driven her to another household, which would be more tolerant of her happiness.

Thus she slipped out into a more congenial atmosphere, and became more lovely than ever. Rella was delighted to go. "Oh, Miss Lilliard," she said, looking back at the place when they were leaving, "how many anxious, dangerous hours we've spent in that place in which you stayed that you might help others and you always had a case in hand, too. You'd walk into the lion's mouth and lead out a soldier, and send him on the road to safety." "Rella, I wondered many times if I had overlooked some opportunity to help. Of course that time of help is past, but this is now captured territory and that gives us a chance to get out, and dear girl, when 

I hear the whippoorwill or the screech owl notes it strikes a terror, a fear, an anxiety to my heart. Really 

I cannot find words that express my feelings, for if even Hodges had caught on we would have been in great danger and those we were helping would have met death, but think of Hodges now!" "When I got protection for us, that meant him too, so instead of his being a terror to me, he would fairly eat out of my hand like a lamb. But, oh thank 

God! I did my mite, I am glad of it and that we are safely out of that hateful atmosphere.'

The weeks passed, giving Joy the placid rest which

MISSING SIBYL 

she needed and enjoyed, but it could not last to stagnation, for one day a letter came. "My dearest Joy: 

Dear old schoolmate, I received a letter or rather a continuation of letters from Everest, telling me of the gracious way in which you helped him on the train. 

Oh, it was just like you. I know just how you did it, as though I had seen you. Joy, you always were the first to see a thing, and you always acted at once. You're a darling girl! I could hug you to death!

I want you to come and spend a month with us and I shall be offended if you do not come. Let me know when it is convenient for you to come. 

Papa and Mama can scarcely wait to welcome the wonderful girl who saved Everest from capture and possibly death. We will have a good time, since this awful war is about to close. With loads of love. 

From your old classmate,

Edith" “Oh, Aunt Ada, here's a letter from my old, dear friend and classmate." And she read it aloud. "Now, dear Aunt Ada, I think I shall accept this very kind invitation. We have been living in such fear, gloom, dread, and awful realities, that a change to our kind will be an uplift and an inspiration. Surely, from what Uncle says, you will be coming North in the course of time, and I believe for George's sake it would be well to do so." "Go, child, and have a good time. You have been through an awful period of anxieties and carnage during this war, but come back to us again."

MISSING SIBYL 

213 "Dear Aunt Ada, I do appreciate your kind invitation and your kindness, but I do so want to settle on my father's estate and live in his old home again."

So it was settled, and she departed for her visit in the Northland. Her friend Edith did everything possible for her happiness and comfort. The social whirl into which she was flung was the very incentive needed to bring her out of her war-shocked condition. 

She made friends and was much sought after for her goodness, as well as her brilliancy and beauty. The month's visit lengthened, for the Vernes would not let her go. "Joy," quizzed Edith, "you are an enigma to me.

You have turned down two offers of marriage, but I notice you have been very gracious about it, and they still love you even though you made known however much you were honored, you would not bestow your hand where you did not love. There are others at your feet, but they read the sign of no hope for them. My dear you are as though there was a dead past and that your heart would not reawaken. You remind me of the words of the poet: 'Do noble deeds, not dream along and so make life, death and that sweet forever one grand sweet song, Can't you take the dreams of love along with them, dear friend?'" 

Joy smiled upon her friend. "Why try to coerce my heart, dear friend? Your happiness is assured and 

I am rejoiced that you have chosen so wisely.' "Thank you, Joy, but I am vexed at you. May 

I make a request of you? I do so much want you to sing at the reception given by Mrs. Brinkman for the returned soldiers." "Why, my dear Edith, I shall be delighted to do anything I can to entertain those blessed boys, who fought so valiantly for our cause, and they are bear-

MISSING SIBYL 

ing the scars of battle, too. easy as possible for them."

Mrs. Brinkman was noted for her fine entertainments and being an ideal hostess, the reception was a veritable crush of greetings for many. She brought a young man to Joy. "Miss Lilliard, this is Mr. Gayner, a soldier who has just recently returned from the South, and he had some bitter experiences while there." "Hold on there! Are you trying to introduce me to Miss Joy Lilliard of the South?" "That's what I started to do, Mr. Gayner. Have you met before?" "Have we? Well, a Miss Joy Lilliard saved my life, and I rowed away in her skiff and by much trouble and many scares I got back to my regiment." By this time a group had surrounded them, all eager to hear of another one of Joy's rescues. 999 "Well, Edith, I cast him away in my boat, so I guess it's a case of 'Cast thy bread upon the waters and after many days it will return unto you.' Amid the general laugh at her quotation she said to the young man, "I'm so glad you got through safely. Somehow it relaxes a tension in my soul, for I often wondered if that little by-play really put you in the way out, or if it was the means of throwing you into death. And now this relief! Well, I just can't find words to explain it." "Joy, Joy! Didn't you do anything but find soldiers in distress? I'll declare I thought my brother was the only one saved by you, but here comes another one to decorate your brow with laurels. 'Byplay' she says," echoed Edith. "Now that's just like Joy Lilliard, who was a sort of detective general in the South, and who was in as much danger as any of those whom she was the means of saving."

MISSING SIBYL 

215 "She started you to eating by just two apples, and my brother by sucking just three oranges, and my brother says he was so hungry he could have slain and eaten a rebel." 99 "Well, he would have died then sure enough,' laughed everyone, when two husky lads snatched Joy up to their shoulders and headed a parade around the spacious hall all singing "The Star Spangled Banner. 99 

When they set Joy upon her feet she said, “Dear friends, I want to tell you I came from invaded territory, and it was horrible what those down there suffered, suffered, suffered! When will the future pages of history be written without that horrible picture of the open-mouthed dragon of war, war, war! staring the peoples of the earth in the face, breaking their hearts, and searing their souls with fear and hate, for there are always two sides to every question of great importance, but I'm glad to have done my bit in this encounter." "Joy, you are a wonder," responded Edith. 99 "Edith, I think of the seven, I must be the Hanging Gardens," laughed Joy, "for you've all planted laurels upon me.' And she escaped from the room while her friends stood in groups discussing her hair breadth escapes.

The entertainment was a most happy occasion of talking, eating, singing and dancing. Joy was singing her promised solo when as she turned she found unexpectedly one stand nearby. Lydon had arrived late and she had not noticed him coming in. As she was singing, he had not been announced, but when she turned from the piano their eyes met. She turned pale but controlled her emotion. Lydon stepped forward with outstretched hands, while Mrs. Brinkman bustled forward saying, "Ah, I see you are acquainted."

MISSING SIBYL 

"Why, Mrs. Brinkman, Mr. Somers is my remote cousin. We grew up together," explained Joy. "I see! little minx!" thought her friend Edith. "No wonder she turned all others down, for he is the handsomest_man I ever saw, and such polished manners. Oh, Joy, Joy! How glad I am. I surely read the sign of love in your face, sweet darling.'

Well, well, so many things for the returned boys in blue to talk about. One other soldier told her how he had found her skiff with a little food in it and how both had been the means of getting him back to his comrades and thus the hours sped by. 

Lydon led Joy out on the veranda, where they stood in the moonlight and as Lydon captured her hand he said, "Joy, I have come through this war whole in body and mind, but as I told you at the masquerade ball atWingates, I am not heart-whole. Does not your own heart tell you why I am here tonight? 

Darling, I will never go back to the South to live. 

I love you with all my heart and want to make you my wife. I have an important appointment tendered me abroad, from Washington. Darling, will you go with me as my precious wife, across the ocean from the turmoils of this divided people until such time as our nation can get more settled and happy?" He opened his arms saying, "Will you Joy?" 

She placed her arms around his neck and said, "Yes, beloved, anywhere with you will be heaven." And their lips met in their first kiss, and first promise.

CHAPTER XVII 

WAITING Lester went at once to his people at Norfolk who received him graciously and took him with them to the pleasant entertainments and homes of their friends. 

His cousin Phillip said on his first evening- "Tonight we will go to an entertainment given for charity, and a fine singer who with a friend, a widow, has been cruising about in a yacht will sing. The widow, a wealthy woman, has people here whom she has been visiting, but they are leaving again at once on her yacht."

The cousins came. They saw, heard, and were conquered. "What a magnificent voice!" praised Lester. "She must have been abroad to finish for it seems that nothing is lacking and she is pretty, too, with a fine personality." Consulting his program he found her to be Miss Sibyl Brinton, but the name meant nothing to him, for Victor had never mentioned her name when he told Lester of his lost love.

Sibyl responded to encores, and the last number was one of her own compositions on 'Giving' with catchy music. It brought the house down with a wild demonstration, and she, bowing to the right and to the left, had the satisfaction of knowing that pocket books were opening wider than their owners had intended to open them, for the silver jingled a merry rythm and the greenbacks fluttered all to a pretty sentiment, so that she had the joy of knowing that many an extra 217

MISSING SIBYL 

comfort had been added to the cause by the rendition of her song. "Oh, boy! Phillip, that song was a master stroke. 

She stormed the house." "Ha, ha, ha!" laughed Phillip. "That girl will lead the world by the nose, for she has real talent,' augmented Lester. 

Victor, completing his investigation, dined with Lester's people before he and Lester started on their homeward journey. Next day when both were in the office, Lester inadvertently dropped a paper from his pocket, and upon picking it up found it to be the program which he had hastily and thoughtlessly stuffed in his pocket the night Sibyl sang.

"Oh, Lester, thank God! She's safe," voiced Victor. "Safe and among friends. Did she seem to be happy and well?"

She at least is living, and has a wealthy friend in Mrs. Seabright, who is a widow and seemed very pleased at the young lady's singing. They immediately sailed out of port that night, so there is no possibility of getting her there." So he left Betty with food for thought, and happier than she had been for many days. 

Betty understood how he wanted to tell her of Lester, and in her heart thanked God he was well even if "glum sometimes." She knew it was for her sake that Victor had almost pushed his way into the house by persistence with the butler. She had seen him in the sidewalk even before he reached the door,

227 and had flitted to the library where she stood just inside the door from the hall where she could hear, and at the right moment had stepped into the colloquy so that the butler could not intervene. Now that she had angered him he would be more vigilant than ever, and take pleasure in his many unreasonable ways of thwarting her.

Betty knew the butler was afraid of Victor, for Victor had shown him a measuring and menacing eye.

She wondered if her father had relented, as Victor had said his knowledge had come through means of the family. She wondered by what means, and if her father would soon permit her to come home.

That part was an enigma to her, but she was sure she could go to the university in Washington, and that suited her much better than going home and receiving Ancel Upham as a suitor, for that she would not do. 

That night she wrote Lester a short wholesome letter as a friend, not telling him all her woes, for she had much to learn first.

The next day while her aunt was napping she thrust her letter and a beret into her blouse, tripped down to the library, and there she found the butler as she had expected to, where he could watch her movements about leaving the house. Humming a snatch of song, she selected a book and went out on the porch and settled down to reading. Seeing her bareheaded he thought she was there for the afternoon. 

Soon she heard the butler moving quietly down the hall; she put on her cap, swung over the porch railing and hurried down to the next corner where she boarded a street car. Going to the nearest divisional post-office, she rented a box, enclosed numbers, sealed

When Victor got back home, he found Lester had received Betty's letter and was a happy man. I was so happy when I read your letter of love and devotion. All along I knew you loved me, and that it was false pride that sealed your lips, but I was willing to wait until you could find it easier to make a home. Really I admire your independence for you know my father would have been glad at that time to have helped us to one, but not now since Ancel spoke to him. 

I shall be very happy, sweetheart, as your wife in a cottage. But as I am quite young yet, I feel that I would like more university work, therefore I will stay with Auntie and go to school another year, hoping that before that time father will become reconciled to my refusal of Ancel Upham.

229 I will write to Mother and tell her that I have accepted your suit. So we will await developments of the future.

That gives me a chance to get my home paid up and everything will turn out all O. K. I see she is not anxious to come back to her father's, as she has planned a year's school work there at the university, but oh, how I want to look upon her dear face! I hate her being away, but I shall see her occasionally in some way. I am so happy that this long year will place her at my side as my wife." "Lester, the time will roll around. Of course, sometimes it will seem a long waiting, but oh, boy! This extra Stubblefield commission will net you up fine." "Yes, Victor, I think I shall build on one large room below and two above it, thereby getting three more rooms, which will make a splendid home. I'll have it all furnished according to her taste, so when we come back we can go immediately to our own home. I will show that old dodger that we are not depending upon him in any way." "That's right, you are all straightened out now and money comes in fast, so I am rejoicing for you.

I believe these are confidential remarks, and that we often make them," remarked Victor. "Most assuredly, Victor, they will not be repeated else it would be savory and spoil the game, but don't think it is irrelevant. We most certainly can differentiate between Mr. Greene, and Mr. Addison who is a travesty on humanity, trying to sell his child for pelf. I certainly admire her strength of soul, who

gives up home and its ties rather than become involved in a loveless marriage. "She is more precious to me than ever, for such love is priceless. She has given herself wholly, and is willing for the world to know it. How I will miss her at all these parties and frills we will have to attend !" "Ha, ha!" laughed Victor and said, "Yes, when you know others will have expectations, but naturally, 'murder will out,' they will understand and cease to expect, although you will have friends who will be nice, for there will be times when you will have to have a lady, to make out couples." I missed her at Norfolk and again at Washington.

"The financial affairs of the family were getting pretty badly juggled and at a low ebb when he took his place at the helm and brought them safely through, and things have gone well ever since." 

As Sibyl appeared at breakfast, William said, "I see you are able to smile this morning, Miss Brinton." "Sure thing!" retorted Sibyl. "They say it takes the exercise of sixty muscles to scowl and only sixteen to smile, so why not smile and save the difference." 

At the general laugh that followed Bella said, "You never know what Sibyl has got stored away.' 99 "No," augmented Sibyl, "I'm like measles-always breaking out; or is it itch?" 233 "Well, I'm going to do justice to this delicious toast and coffee," parried Sibyl. 

While her hostess thought, What a delightful guest she is! Even if she is poor she is received in the lap of riches with honor, but she is too proud to let them polish her gift. Lucy must get around her in some way to let her take her abroad. These two are so congenial that it would be a shame to separate them. 

Sibyl had in such matters an intuition against accepting large gifts, which she had probably inherited from her mother, and in that way had kept her soul free from worry. She had always felt that her will power must be squared by her won't power.

She realized that work was honest and left no bitter taste in the mouth, whereas worry was a maggot which worked over time. Therefore she must watch her step and make no mistake, which was not easy to do. She thought so often, Mother, Mother! If I only had Mother and dear Victor! Well, he has finished his studies, and is out somewhere carving out a future, but wherever he is he is looking the world squarely in the face, never losing his poise.

They were about to leave Washington, and the last day on Sunday morning she, to please William, sang her song of sweetness, to shower, sparkle and float away. William felt she had left a treasure in the heart of that audience that it would long remember.

Back to the yacht to rest and think. She had an inspiration to write to Mrs. Gleason. She chided herself for having neglected her old friend, but she had started out with the intention of finding out who she could possibly be, and she had been very busy supporting herself, which she had found easy in a simple way. And why shouldn't it be in a simple way when she had an identity to hide and one to gain by getting out in the world where she could rub up against its

MISSING SIBYL 

people with both eyes and ears open for information?

She had found out about her father, but had not pained her grandmother by asking if her outcast father was still living, as she never expected to claim him of her own choice. 

So her letter went out to comfort the heart of her friend, Mrs. Gleason. 

My dear Auntie : 

You may be surprised to hear from me at this remote period of time, but dear Auntie, (or will you permit me to call you Auntie as I used to, since I've learned I am no blood relation to you?) I love you just the same, and look back upon the kindness and substantial gifts you used to pour out upon me. My dear mother, Mrs. 

Brinton, what shall I say about her? She was everything that love, devotion, and goodness could be to me. I revere her memory and shall always call her Mother Brinton, and am happy to bear her name, for she was a mother to me, indeed. 

How many sacrifices she must have made to bring me up, to educate me, and to provide for my wants though I was not her own flesh and blood. It was a great shock to me when she told me I was not her child, but she died without being able to let me know who I am, other than my name of "Gordon." That I have found out since, but I am ahead of my story. 

Two days after she was buried an agent told me she had deeded me her home without my knowledge. I ran away from home to make my own way. 

I went to St. Louis and within two or three days found an emergency job as saleslady in a big department store, but even before

I secured it, by accident, I was given a singing

MISSING SIBYL 

235 date at a big reception. A young lady who had the date was taken sick, and her brother who was a tenant in the same rooming house as myself, heard me singing in my room, so he asked me to fill the vacancy. After that I got such dates every seven or ten days, or rather nights I should say, and it was among the rich who were very generous.

I kept my position and studied stenography at night. When I finished that, I secured a position with a judge, so I dropped the store work and took voice culture lessons at night. 

Before leaving the store, jealous parties hatched up a lie of theft on me and had me arrested, but a girl friend overheard them so we fixed a trap for them and caught them. At the trial I was cleared and they fled before the trial was over.

I made many friends, as it was in the papers. 

Those rich patrons I'd been singing for became indignant and it opened many homes for singing dates. (You see, I worked too hard and went down with nervous prostration!) 

One day hurrying home in a storm with a raised umbrella, I ran against a big man who also carried an umbrella, and I went down in a faint. He carried me to the nearest porch and brought me out of it. Then he stopped a passing acquaintance and sent me home in a car. I was sick a long while. He attended me and told me he had an Aunt who was going to take an ocean cruise for her health, and she would give me a position as secretary and companion at regular salary. I told him I would take the position for nothing in order to get the ocean travel, which he said would restore my health, and it has. But she insisted on paying me, as I tell her for no-

MISSING SIBYL 

thing, for on the yacht there is so little to do, and she keeps a maid also. The doctor said

I was burning the candle at both ends, and so 

I didn't last as long as I hoped to, but I was anxious to get ahead so that I could earn my own living in an honest way. 

This lady, whose name is Mrs. Lucy Seabright, is a childless widow, and very wealthy. She wants to take me abroad to study voice for a career. She wants to bear all expenses in so doing. Somehow I can't make up my mind to let her do it as it would take such piles of money. 

Now, while talking of money, I want to know if you would like me to send you the money which I received for Mrs. Brinton's house? 

You're the nearest kin, and if you would like I will send it to you. I have it banked in St. Louis, and never spent it. 

Now I hinted that I'd found out who I was. 

My father is a son of a lady in Norfolk, Virginia, by a former marriage. He was so dissolute and disgraced them so shamelessly that they gave him a large sum of money, that he would leave the city and stay away. 

The last woman whom he finally married was in his arms, and he was bewailing the fact that he was married to my mother, when she happened to come into the room and stepped to the door, saw and heard it all. He and the woman were on a porch. Mother immediately left and you know the rest. The woman began a breach of promise suit, so he married her, but we don't know where he is. 

Mrs. Seabright, with whom I am traveling is related to his mother, who is my grandmother, and we took dinner with her once. I did not

MISSING SIBYL 

237 let her know I was her grandchild, as I did not want to open the wound, for she had sent father away. Not yet will I let her know, and maybe never. She remarked that evening that I made her think of father's first wife, (my mother) so you see, I resemble mother. Grandmother had a large picture of him hanging on the wall, and I studied it carefully, as you know the one I have in the locket is small. Now you see that makes 

Mrs. Seabright and me related remotely, but she doesn't know it. Did you ever hear of such a mess as I'm in. I'm holding my own council. 

Mrs. Seabright says my mother's parents are living in New York and were very much opposed to her marriage, so I suppose that was why she did not go home to stay when she ran away. We will soon be in New York City, where I will quietly go to see them without letting her know until after the interview. They may repudiate me, and if they do I will not tell her anything. 

When grandmother said I made her think of father's first wife, I innocently inquired, "What was her name?" She replied, "Leila," so her name was Leila Shepherd. Mrs. Seabright had told me her parents' name is Shepherd and that they are a very musical family, so I suppose I have inherited my mother's gift. 

I am enclosing the address of Mrs. Seabright's apartment where we will be when we get to the city. She has a caretaker who will look after our incoming mail, which might arrive ahead of us. I would love to see you and oh, if I just had dear Mother Brinton, I could do without a good many other things in this world! You will never know how I have missed her. How many
