My dear Sibyl: 

I will never be able to express the gladness 

I felt when your dear letter came to me. My dear child! no wonder you broke, putting yourself through such a strain of improvement. You certainly did need someone to hold you back, but you have made a wonderful record, and dear, I am glad you have found your people. I congratulate your strength of character in not letting yourself be known and probe those wounds of sorrow, for believe me there will come a time when it won't hurt them or, if there never is such a time you will have the consciousness of a good deed in saving the agony of your presence testifying of the open disgrace of such a son. If they should ever need your help, then will be the time to go to them in the way of succor. Once in a while a case like that crops up when they are shamed and crushed, and so it were better to keep out of sight and knowledge and let it blow over as much as possible.

I admire you for saving the fresh open sorrow right now, but believe me there will be a happier consummation in the future, for the wheels of time move forward, not backward, and it is well that they do, else there would be undesirable crushings; for the drop out of the human family are left anchorless on the way and only as they

MISSING SIBYL 

239 strike out for themselves and grow strong by action and looking to God can they overcome the handicap. "Your heavenly Father knoweth that you have need of these things," so if we trust Him, He will supply our needs in principle and material things. In principle, you always were strong, so it's second nature to you to move right of your own volition, and I want to compliment you on your cute adroitness in not letting Mrs. 

Seabright, the wonderful woman, know anything of what you found. 

Investigate the grandparents in New York, and if they doubt or repudiate you, say nothing but come right to me, who loves you for what you are,-I know what you are, as I saw you develop year after year and was glad. 

You know my husband is dead and I am left with a big fortune with no child to leave it to. 

Come to me and rest. Give me joy of your presence and with me let's enjoy my husband's fortune. He often said how he wished we had a daughter like you! 

My health is very low, and I have made a will which I will alter somewhat and will leave a goodly slice of wealth to you, and besides that 

I will pay your expenses for voice training lessons, to go anywhere you wish to take them. I feel that you are ours more than theirs, for while you have their blood, we had your life with us, and the life is more than blood. So don't hesitate to take my money which is so cheerfully offered. 

I am sending you five thousand dollars now with which to get some good clothes in the city. 

When I say good clothes, I mean suitable clothes, not spectacular clothes like some actresses sport.

MISSING SIBYL

Oh, yes, I almost forgot to speak of the two thousand dollars which you have banked in St. 

Louis. Of course I do not want it, child. You should have used it instead of working so hard, for it was yours. Another thing I want to write.

Your mother's grave is in a cemetery at which was a small town called "Byron" which was wiped out by a cyclone, but of course the cemetery still remains. Her grave is marked by a small marble slab, which says, "Mother, Mrs. Gordon," and the date of her death, which was all Sister knew.

Even that little will help you, and ought to be a great comfort to her parents, if they care to investigate. 

Now, pet, I think I must close. Hoping you understand how much I want you, and want you soon, I am, Lovingly,

Aunt Kittie.

One day in a nonchalant way Sibyl asked Mrs. Seabright where the parents of Mrs. Gordon lived, adding that she perhaps had been told but had forgotten. So

Making an excuse that she wished to match a certain color in gloves and shoes, Sibyl said she would go alone while Mrs. Seabright was taking her rest. 

So taking her mother's ring, chain and locket, and arriving at her grandparents' home, was careful to take the opposite side of the street, as she wished to view the house well from a short distance. While she was so doing, a car stopped in front of the house, and an elderly couple stepped out and entered the house. Sibyl was sure it was her grandparents but she walked on down to the corner, crossed over and came back. Ringing the doorbell she thought, My poor mother had a lovely home, and I wonder if it was closed to her to come back to live with her parents?

While she was thus thinking, a butler appeared, to whom she handed her card saying, "I wish to see Mr. and Mrs. W. H. Shepherd.' "Mr. and Mrs. Shepherd are not in," answered the butler. "I beg to differ with you," retorted Sibyl. "I just now saw them enter their home." She did not know it to be them but she aimed to make the butler believe she knew, and added, "Please tell them I have something important to see them about, and that I

Please say on." "Mr. and Mrs. Shepherd, how I wish I could 'say on,' for it is a matter which is very important to you and to me that I wish to speak about. You had a daughter who married George Gordon. Upon finding out his perfidy, she ran away from him, and in an auto crash was picked up unconscious. By the work of a doctor and nurse she was kept alive long enough to give birth to a child, after which she shortly died."

She came wondering what it could mean. She told 

Belle to watch out for Sibyl when she should come back. So she came, and, as Sibyl said, "walked into a trap." It took a long time to tell all, but she had been told a great deal by Dr. Langley, though not concerning the relationship; all that Sibyl had put herself through and by so doing had thrown her in the channel of discovering her people. They, too, thought as she did that it was not wise to let her father's people know of the relationship, for her grandmother's sake, until some time in the future, and that none but Mrs. Seabright should know.

While they were yet talking, the telephone rang, and there was a call for Mrs. Seabright. It was Belle telling her she had a call from home. There was sickness, a telegram. "So, dear, I am telling you good bye over the telephone, as I am leaving this minute. Sibyl is not here yet, but she is sensible and will be here shortly. She probably took in a show. So again good bye. 99 "Now, Mrs. Seabright, you must stay all night with

MISSING SIBYL 

249 us. 

We will manage somehow to get along without a butler, and we will talk, talk, almost all night.' 

"Oh, I am so glad that I think I could spend the rest of my life without a butler, and never miss him," interposed Mrs. Seabright.

They talked and talked into the wee hours of the night, changing suddenly from laughter into tears, and again from tears into laughter. When they did finally get to bed it was to think and toss, and toss and think, and sigh and smile. It was a very late breakfast that was served the family that next morning. The servants wondered what it could all mean, until Mrs. Shepherd called them together and told them all they needed to know. They were surprised and happy to know that the young woman who, in their midst, had called herself Sibyl Brinton, was gone, for she had been a tyrant to the servants. "Just to think," said Mrs. Seabright who couldn't keep off the subject, "how you dug out, step by step, who your people were, and so secretly, too. I never will forget how you looked when you discovered 

George Gordon's portrait on the wall at your other grandmother's, and how you came in the room and played and sang that comforting old song, 'What a

Friend We Have in Jesus,' and how we were all touched by it. Yes, and she said you made her think of George's first wife." "I asked what her name was and she said, 'Leila.' 

That was the first time I'd heard her name," interposed Sibyl. "I never can tell you how I dreaded to come yesterday, I just gritted my teeth and said, 'I will take the bull by the horns and go.' Now if I hadn't, that impostor would have stayed on to the end, for she would not have been discovered in her sham and there is no telling what consequences may have fol-

lowed. What other kin in the family have I?" questioned Sibyl. "Kin? Ray and Frank," answered her grandfather, "and they will be happy for they never liked Sibyl. 

She snubbed them so that frequently I had to call her down for such treatment of her uncles. She did not like them." "One other thing, Grandmother, may I see a picture of my mother?" "Oh, surely, child, I had forgotten that you had never seen a picture of her." She took only Sibyl to the portrait gallery, saying: "You look very much like her, my dear child." 

Sibyl looked her fill, and the tears streamed down her cheeks, and she was not ashamed of them but she said to her grandmother, "What a teary time we are having,—it's both sorrow and thanksgiving.' 

The following day the little party of four left for 

Cincinnati to be with Mrs. Gleason, who took them all to what had been the village of Byron, and to the cemetery. She led them to the grave of Mrs. Gordon, where they found on the modest stone that she was 

Mrs. Gordon, and a mother, and the date of her death. "Well, there is not much there, but it speaks a plenty with no dishonor to her. Oh, Leila, Leila, dear child, we have found you at last!"

They all knelt about the grave. It seemed a sacred hour in which they wept and sat by Leila's grave, talking. Grandfather Shepherd told of a dying woman's confession of keeping the child which Mrs. Gordon had paid her to keep, and the tale was all backed up by Preston Elliot of Cincinnati, Ohio. "Preston Elliot!" exclaimed Mrs. Gleason, as she sprang to her feet. "No wonder you got deceived. I know him well. There was a time when he was in

MISSING SIBYL 

251 business with my husband, and when the business was going bad he sold his half to my husband for more than it was worth. My husband was glad to get rid of him. When my husband died, Elliott tried to get the one-half of the business back with accrued profits for all those years, but by the advice of a young influential lawyer, I got a deposition from a certain man who was dying, so I was ready for Elliot. "When Elliot later brought suit, he tried to get this same young lawyer to handle the case, but he refused and came to me and told me to get that deposition, and be ready. If it hadn't been for that deposition, I would have lost the whole half of my fortune. That man is a scoundrel, I tell you! Now catch him Mr. Shepherd; I'll make a star witness against him, and Sibyl here will make another, so you will have two stars against him. The slippery old eel! I dare say he, the butler, and the young woman are across the Canadian line right now, by airplane. 

You can bring them back if you can find them.' 99 "I will find them. I've got a million that will start the ball to rolling," said Mr. Shepherd. "I've got a half million to add, if you need it," chipped in Mrs. Gleason. "Who is that lawyer you spoke of," quizzed Mr. Shepherd. "His name is Victor Whiteside. He is in New 

York right now and will be gone probably a month looking after interest for a multi-millionaire, but he has a partner named Lester Buchannan, who is fine, and he will start it for you. 99 "I will see him," said Mr. Shepherd. 

Mrs. Gleason took a sly glance at Sibyl and saw she was white as death, and she understood. Sibyl thought, Dear Victor made his career.

would for he had the brains that could, and I am so glad to hear of him in such a casual way.

She started toward the car, knowing she would be at her mother's grave again.

The last few days had been very busy days, indeed. 

The last three had been so exciting and wearing on the nerves that Sibyl felt a few days rest would be welcome indeed, wherefore they planned for her future abroad, when old friends were not present. 

Lester started the tracing of Mr. Elliot and his accomplises, and had the pleasure of again hearing 

Sibyl sing at the church service. "Dear Aunt Kittie, I prefer Budapest. It is a wonderful city, so I don't care to go with the young couple to London as you spoke of me doing.' 99 "Well, dear, you many go where you please, but

I don't want you to work yourself to death, you little cyclone. Take things easier. I don't want you to go alone, but in my state of health I am hardly equal to it." "Well," said Mrs. Seabright, "I have no one holding me anywhere, and as you are a relative I feel like I might go and be of some real use to you, if you care to have me." "Care to!" reiterated Sibyl, "Oh, oh, oh! Do I hear aright? My! this is my lucky day. By the way, what would you have me call you?" "Call me Aunt Lucy. It sounds so sweet to hear you call Mrs. Gleason, Aunt Kittie, and it is in keeping with our years compared to your age.' 99 "Oh, my sakes alive, how sweet! I could shout, shout it from the house tops. How soon, Aunt Lucy, could you be ready?" "In a day's time. We would not be loaded with a lot of luggage, for we can get anything we want over there," answered Aunt Lucy.

253 "Well, we won't hurry for Aunt Kittie has a right to a couple of week's visit, anyway," voiced Sibyl. "I'd say a right! I want you for a month anyway," countered Mrs. Gleason. "No," bossed grandfather, "you mustn't wait that long. Now is the safest time to cross the ocean, and two weeks must see you gone.' 99 "We will be ready, grandfather. Oh, I feel so safe! I've been so alone in the world, but now I feel like I'm standing on the rock of Gibralter with the dearest friends in all the world related to me."

They all smiled upon her and rejoiced with her in the fact that everything was all ironed out about their relationship. "Grandmother, what is your name?" "Darling, my name is Lou," and the tears started afresh to think a grandchild twenty-two years old had never known she had a living grandmother, nor her name. "Grandfather's name is Wallace Henry." "I like that," said Sibyl, "it sounds like stars." 

Mrs. Gleason gave a big party, for which Sibyl rather admonished her as it was trying on her strength, for she could see that Mrs. Gleason's health had failed rapidly. Mrs. Gleason sprung a happy surprise on

Sibyl by inviting some of her schoolmates at Bilton. 

So the old happy girlhood days were lived over, and her friends were so glad of the happy consummation of her finding her parentage and people. Lester was there, and he so deplored the fact that Victor could not be; for he had tried to connect with him but failed both by wire and phone. Oh, how heartbroken Victor would be! "Mr. Whiteside will be very much disappointed that he could not get to see you," said Lester. "Tell Mr. Whiteside, please, that I rejoice at his

MISSING SIBYL 

making such a brilliant career. He had it in him, and I knew it well," nonchalantly interposed Sibyl. 

In a few days she had said the last good bye, and the ocean was rolling between her and her people and friends. She went to work with a will, for she never did anything in a half-hearted manner, and time sped on and Victor had missed her again. 

Lester had taken her deposition. Mr. Shepherd had hunted the culprits down, and they were languishing in prison as such parasites on society should, and should be kept there instead of being let out by bribery and the pleas of sob sisters. 

Lester and Victor most heartily enjoyed handling the case, and they gained added laurels by it. Mrs. 

Gleason had the satisfaction of appearing in court against Mr. Elliot, who had tried to rob her of half her fortune, and who had tried to keep Sibyl from knowing her grandparents by falsely placing another in her stead, thus robbing her of her portion of her rich grandfather's estate. Mr. Shepherd had talked with Mrs. Gleason about the expense of Sibyl's voice lessons. He wished to bear the expense, but Mrs. 

Gleason had her heart set upon it, and told him he might do something great for such a great child. 

Mrs. Gleason made him understand that as she had known Sibyl all her life, she felt that she had a claim upon the girl. She said that she herself had no children, and her sister, if she were living, would be glad to have her do it, and that she was also leaving a nice large bestowment upon Sibyl. Since she had found her she had changed her will, leaving her much more. "The poor little thing had made such a brave fight alone, whereas if she had only known, it would have made it easier for the girl. She believed Mrs. Seabright purposely went with her to keep her from over-

255 doing as she had in the past, and too, it was as easy for Mrs. Seabright to live abroad as in America, for she was not niggardly anywhere. I am so happy to have them together. It is such a protection to each and company for both." 

Mrs. Seabright sent many letters to Mrs. Gleason that she might know how an older person looked upon Sibyl's progress, and her letters were always truthfully encouraging reports that filled Mrs. Gleason's heart with the joy of helpfulness to her dear young friend.

Dear Aunt Kittie: 

You sent me more money than I needed, but

I've banked it until such time as I shall need it. Aunt Lucy laughs. She says you will swamp me with money, but she is quite as bad. I have to hold her down too. 

She gave me the loveliest gold hair band studded with diamonds. It is very valuable. I put it in the bank for I know I'd be robbed. will wear it only when appearing before great audiences. 

You will be glad when I tell you Professor (that great long name you don't like) says I have made unusual progress and that I will soon be through. Aunt Lucy is very enthusiastic about it.

We do a great deal of sightseeing as we dart about here, there and yonder seeing Europe, but I've written you that before when we had been to some big city and seen its great things. We are in a real world of beauty. This wonderful 

Budapest. I never get tired of it. You know

Aunt Lucy is experienced, and teaches me how to appreciate the world of art. She is a world

of knowledge, but you know she saw many of these beauties before when she traveled with her husband, who was very learned in the arts. She is a wonderful friend and knows how to round out my life. I am a very fortunate girl indeed to have the kind of friends who are caring for me. 

I have appeared several times, and had splendid audiences. Been well received, and some repeat dates. 

When Grandfather sends me money, Aunt Lucy goes with me and we buy some fine jewels suitable for me. Grandfather wrote me a long time ago that he had a nice monument put to Mother's grave. I am so glad he did it, for it proves to the world that she was not an outcast from her father's house.

We have made some very warm friends here whom we will regret to leave.

Now I must get to practicing and don't forget to write "pretty soon directly." Ha, ha.

Betty Addison had finished school, but her aunt was as obdurate as was her father about her marrying Lester, as she, like him wanted her to marry wealth instead. She refused to permit her to receive Lester at her house but he met her a few times in a hotel parlor. They scarcely knew how to manage the situation with dignity. They let her father and Aunt know they were not caring, because, since they were. not to be given a house wedding, they decided to have an airplane wedding. When Lester told Russell 

Ainsworth, he became jubilant, and said, "Why not both couples have a double airplane wedding, each

257 couple witnessing for the other? I will speak to

You know Ruth's mother is an invalid, and it would save her a lot of work and worry." "Say, Lester, your house is a peach! and so large since you've built on to it, and all spruced up fine as a fiddle. Have you got it finished ready for Betty?" "Just the kitchen and a lovely bedroom suite which 

Mrs. Gleason had sent down as a wedding present.

I want Betty to choose the furnishings to suit herself, otherwise I would have had it furnished throughout. 

I want it all to be high class stuff, good stuff that she will like, for it will be home."

Ruth's mother finally consented to allow her to have an airplane wedding, so they and Lester flew to Washington where they found Betty. While her aunt was out she sent her luggage to the depot, as she had it all packed ready to send at the first opportunity, and shipped it to Cincinnati, Ohio. She dressed in a new smart traveling suit, packed two bags to take along, which she sent to the hotel. She wrote her aunt a note telling her she was leaving, addressed, sealed, and left it on the hall table. 

Lester met Betty at the gate, and they joined the other couple with a minister who had baptized Betty when a child. They all motored to an airport where a plane was waiting for them to take them to Chicago for their honeymoon.

They were married on the way, each couple witnessing for the other, and each couple sent back home a message saying: "Married and the happiest couple on earth.' 99 Victor remembered them with a fine dining room

set, while Mr. and Mrs. Shepherd sent a check for five hundred dollars. We will have her visiting, and your dad too, if he will come, for in our happiness we can 'forgive and forget,'" said Lester.

Mr. Addison did not know of the good house that had been bought and furnished and all out of debt, by Lester, and when he found it out was ashamed and chagrined that he had been so unreasonably obstinate about receiving Lester as a son-in-law. He came across with an apology, which was the best way. 

Mrs. Addison was very happy to have her lovely daughter near her, and with so splendid a husband as Lester Buchannan, whom she knew was upright in business, and with a clean life. She always had told her husband that he was making a mistake. The sister whom Mr. Addison had made a "catspaw" was so disgusted she never even visited her brother, who in his error had misrepresented matters so that she had been really cruel to Betty.

Mrs. Gleason had been steadily failing in health, yet was about much of the time. She kept hoping 

Sibyl would get back to the United States before she might be no more. But Sibyl was a star, touring the best parts of Europe as a renowned singer, and she was reaping a star's reward. The world was clamoring for her, and she was sending many fat checks back to Mrs. Gleason to bank for her; in fact she was laying up a fortune, which was growing rapidly.

MISSING SIBYL 

259 

Mrs. Seabright was so happy to see Sibyl's name in glittering electric lights, and packed houses to hear her sing. She said to her friends, "I knew she could do it for she had the ability and was always so sweet about it that people loved her." 

Her instructor was gay with joy, for her success covered him with glory as a voice culture teacher and brought him more aspirants than he could take through the work.

Sibyl did not get back to see her friend before the sad news of her demise was cabled to her. She was distressed and heart broken and blamed herself that she had stayed away too long and so missed seeing her again. She felt that she should have gone to her. "No, dear, you could not well have done that. You had many engagements ahead, and in honor had to fill them. You did not know her condition which terminated so suddenly as do many such cases, and yet she might have lived longer. One never can tell. I am so sorry, for in another month we would have been back for your engagements in New York City, and to your grandmother who is waiting and watching as are many other friends. From the messages we get, St. Louis must be buzzing like a bee hive. Now don't worry over your loss of such a good friend, which she truly was, and her love for you was marvelous. She would be grieved if you persisted in distress. But think of and look for the good, the true, the beautiful in life, for life is only a short span, and you will go to her again over there. She will be watching and waiting for you, so don't grieve." "Aunt Lucy, you are a tower of strength to me.

We have made many friends, but you are the solid front and have always been the stone wall to back. 

I think Doctor Langley knew well your characteristics when he put me in your care. He certainly

MISSING SIBYL 

knew I needed you, so sweet and honest, with beauty of sunlight in your soul; and how good to let down the bars from the heartache and rest with such a friend that sticketh closer than a brother."

When Mrs. Gleason's will was read it was found that Sibyl had been left a million dollars, and among the other legatees, Victor had been willed Mrs. Gleason's elegant home, by reason of his advising her in the saving of her fortune from the machination of Mr. Elliot. 

Well, the month was up. Sibyl and Mrs. Seabright were back in New York to be met by Grandfather and Grandmother Shepherd, Sibyl's Uncle Ray and wife Aunt Tena, and Uncle Frank with his little daughter. It was her first meeting with her uncles and their families, and was another happy occasion. 

Yes, back home indeed! and her name glittered with electric lights on broadway. News had traveled far, and why not in this time of automobiles, newspapers and radios? Sibyl was at her best when she stepped forward before that packed house. The audience leaned tensely forward to be feasted by this sweet lush of song, while she eagerly scanned the attention and joy of the multitude of faces before her. 

There she saw Victor with eyes fastened upon her. 

Dear Victor, she thought, as sweetly, tenderly, and graciously her song vibrated in remembrance of happy moments of the past, in which her heart reveled as delightfully as the fragrance of a wreath of roses at her feet. Tears started unbidden, and those ashamed to be seen applying a handkerchief allowed them to trickle down the cheek unchecked, and with swelling hearts listened.

She lifted the veil of her heart, and Victor passed within with a dearer love than ever before, making her understand that enthusiastic, true as steel, ever

MISSING SIBYL 

261 abiding love nature as she never understood it before in her earlier girl-hood experience. It created a climax which only a few can fathom for it was of and with God. She offered a silent, "I thank Thee, Oh

God, for bringing me to this hour a wedded soul." "A phone call for you Miss Brinton," as she listened to the call a glad smile overspread her face. 

She answered. "Certainly, Victor, I shall be happy indeed to see you. We will have so much to talk, talk, talk about. 

The old days will be vivid." 

With joy Victor went. Oh, truly there was so much to talk about after so long a separation. Victor, possessing himself of her hand, which he carried to his lips, "Sibyl, I have missed you so many times.

It was always a case of 'so near and yet so far. Once you said you did not love me. But my soul claims you as my dearest love. Will you be my wife? 

Darling! I love you so. Oh, how long I have loved and waited! Do say yes, darling, if you can be happier with me." "Yes, Victor, sweetheart, for I love even more than before, but dear, I have always loved you even when I denied you for the purpose of finding out who I am, which I did and found Mother's people to be fine. And oh,-well you already know that."

So in his arms she surrendered her love into his keeping, sealed with their kisses, while within his heart he was thanking God for such a love. "In my sky of delight there's a beautiful star. 'Tis the sun and the moon of my days, And the doors of its glory are ever ajar, And I live in the glow of its rays.

MISSING SIBYL 

'Tis my winter of joy, and my summer of rest. 'Tis my future, my present, my past.

And all through the east, and the clouds of the 

West; I shall follow my star to the last."
